  Why were Hooks introduced in React?        
  Why were Hooks introduced in React?        
  Why would you create a custom hook?        

1. Can hooks be used inside loops or conditions?

Category: Hooks Rules
Hooks in React must always be called at the top level of a component or a custom hook to maintain consistent behavior. Using hooks inside loops or conditional statements breaks the predictable order of hook calls, which React relies on internally to manage state and effects. This can lead to bugs and unexpected behaviors because React cannot guarantee which hooks are called or in what order. By enforcing this rule, React ensures the hook state and effects are stable and consistent across renders, making your components predictable and maintainable.

2. Can React components return multiple elements?

Category: Basics
React components can indeed return multiple elements, but these elements must be wrapped in a single parent element or a React fragment. This approach allows React to properly manage and reconcile the DOM tree efficiently. Fragments are especially useful because they let you group elements without introducing additional nodes in the DOM, which keeps the structure clean and performance optimized. Returning multiple elements fosters component flexibility and composability, allowing developers to build complex interfaces out of simple, reusable pieces.

3. Can you set state directly?

Category: State
Directly modifying state variables is discouraged in React because it circumvents React’s state management system, leading to inconsistencies and unpredictable UI updates. State updates should always be performed through designated setter functions or dispatchers that React provides. These functions ensure that changes trigger re-renders when necessary and that updates are batched for optimal performance. Maintaining this discipline allows React to synchronize the state with the UI seamlessly and avoids subtle bugs caused by direct mutations.

4. Can you use if statements inside JSX?

Category: JSX
JSX does not support traditional if statements inside its syntax because JSX is an expression language, not a statement-based one. Instead, conditional rendering is achieved using JavaScript expressions like ternary operators or logical AND operators inside JSX. This preserves the declarative nature of JSX and keeps the markup concise and readable. For complex conditions, it’s common practice to perform conditional logic outside the JSX and then use the results inside it, keeping the UI code clean and straightforward.

5. Can you use multiple useEffect hooks in a component?

Category: Hooks
Yes, React allows multiple useEffect hooks in the same component. This design enables developers to separate different side effects into isolated hooks, each handling a specific concern such as fetching data, subscriptions, or DOM updates. This modularization improves code clarity, reusability, and maintainability by avoiding complex monolithic effects. Each useEffect runs independently and in the order they appear, helping to organize side effects efficiently.

6. How can useRef be used to access DOM elements?

Category: Hooks
The useRef hook provides a way to create a persistent reference to a DOM element, enabling direct manipulation or access to its properties. By attaching the ref to an element, React updates the ref’s current property with the DOM node once it is rendered. This reference enables imperative operations like focusing inputs, measuring size, or interacting with third-party libraries that require direct DOM access, all without causing re-renders, preserving React’s declarative nature.

7. How can useRef help avoid re-renders?

Category: Hooks
Unlike state variables, updating a useRef’s current property does not trigger a re-render. This makes useRef ideal for storing mutable values that persist across renders but do not directly affect the rendered output. For example, timers, previous values, or any non-visual data can be stored in refs to avoid unnecessary component updates and optimize performance by reducing render cycles.

8. How can you comment inside JSX?

Category: JSX
Commenting inside JSX requires embedding JavaScript comments inside curly braces because standard HTML comments are not recognized inside JSX syntax. This means that block comments or inline comments must be wrapped appropriately to avoid syntax errors. Proper commenting is important for clarifying code intent, documenting complex JSX markup, and facilitating teamwork while maintaining the integrity of the JSX structure.

9. How can you implement accessibility roles and attributes in React?

Category: Accessibility
Accessibility in React is implemented by adding semantic ARIA roles and attributes directly through props on elements. This informs assistive technologies about the purpose and state of components, improving usability for users with disabilities. React supports all standard accessibility attributes, such as aria-label, aria-hidden, and role, allowing developers to build inclusive interfaces that communicate effectively with screen readers and other tools. Ensuring proper accessibility practices not only benefits users but also improves overall application quality and compliance.

10. How can you implement drag and drop functionality in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Drag and drop functionality in React involves managing the drag source, drop targets, and the transfer of data between them, often through event handlers and state management. It can be implemented using native browser drag events or through specialized libraries that abstract complexities and improve cross-browser compatibility. Proper drag and drop implementation requires updating UI state to reflect dragging progress, handling drop logic, and ensuring accessibility for keyboard and assistive technology users. This feature enhances user interaction and enables dynamic, intuitive interfaces.

11. How can you implement internationalization (i18n) in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Implementing internationalization in React involves preparing your application to support multiple languages and regional formats, allowing it to adapt content based on the user’s locale. This typically includes externalizing all user-facing strings and resources, detecting or selecting the user’s preferred language, and dynamically loading the appropriate translations. Tools and libraries such as i18next or React Intl facilitate this process by managing translation files, formatting dates, numbers, and currencies according to locale rules, and providing context-aware components. Proper internationalization enhances user experience globally and ensures inclusivity across diverse audiences.

 

12. How can you improve accessibility with ARIA roles in React?

Category: Accessibility
Improving accessibility with ARIA roles in React involves explicitly defining roles and attributes on UI components that convey semantic meaning to assistive technologies. By assigning proper ARIA roles like "button", "dialog", or "navigation", and using states and properties such as aria-expanded or aria-checked, developers provide context and interaction cues that screen readers and other tools rely on. This practice bridges gaps where native HTML elements do not suffice, enhancing the usability of custom components. Careful and correct use of ARIA attributes helps create inclusive applications that comply with accessibility standards.

13. How can you memoize a component with React.memo?

Category: Performance
React.memo is a higher-order component that optimizes functional components by memoizing their rendered output. When a component wrapped with React.memo receives the same props on subsequent renders, React reuses the previous render result instead of recalculating the component tree. This prevents unnecessary rendering and improves performance, especially for components that render complex UI or are used frequently. Memoization is particularly effective when combined with immutable props and pure functions, ensuring that renders are only triggered when relevant data changes.

14. How can you optimize bundle size in React apps?

Category: Performance Optimization
Optimizing bundle size in React applications involves techniques that reduce the amount of JavaScript and assets sent to the client, enhancing load times and responsiveness. This includes code splitting, where parts of the app are lazily loaded only when needed; tree shaking, which eliminates unused code during bundling; and using efficient libraries that avoid unnecessary dependencies. Minimizing the use of large third-party packages, leveraging caching strategies, and compressing assets further contribute to a leaner bundle. Together, these approaches improve user experience, especially on slower networks or devices.

15. How can you optimize images in React applications?

Category: Performance Optimization
Optimizing images in React apps is essential for fast rendering and low bandwidth usage. Techniques include using responsive images with different resolutions for various screen sizes, lazy loading images so that only those in the viewport are fetched, and employing modern formats like WebP for better compression. Image optimization tools can compress images without losing quality, and CDN delivery ensures faster load times globally. Proper image optimization reduces the strain on network resources and improves overall application performance and accessibility.

16. How can you prevent excessive re-renders in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Preventing excessive re-renders in React requires careful state management and optimization strategies. Avoiding unnecessary state changes, memoizing components and callbacks with React.memo and useCallback, and splitting components to isolate state changes are key approaches. Ensuring that props passed down to child components are stable and avoiding anonymous functions or objects inline can also reduce needless updates. Profiling with React DevTools helps identify render bottlenecks, enabling targeted optimizations that keep the UI performant and responsive.

17. How can you reduce bundle size in a React app?

Category: Performance
Reducing bundle size in React apps involves careful selection of dependencies, using dynamic imports for code splitting, and removing dead code through tree shaking. Leveraging smaller, modular libraries or rewriting parts of the codebase to use native APIs can also shrink bundles. Additionally, analyzing the bundle with tools such as webpack Bundle Analyzer helps pinpoint large modules, guiding efforts to refactor or replace them. Efficient bundling improves load times and user experience by decreasing the initial download size.

18. How can you trigger an effect only once using useEffect?

Category: Hooks
To trigger an effect only once using useEffect, you pass an empty dependency array as the second argument to the hook. This tells React that the effect does not depend on any state or props and should only run after the initial render. This pattern is commonly used for initialization tasks like fetching data, subscribing to events, or setting up timers. By controlling the dependencies carefully, developers avoid repeated executions and potential side effects on subsequent renders.

19. How do atoms and selectors work in Recoil?

Category: State Management
In Recoil, atoms represent pieces of state that components can subscribe to and modify. They serve as the source of truth for individual state units. Selectors derive computed or filtered data from atoms or other selectors, enabling efficient state derivation without redundant data storage. Both atoms and selectors leverage React’s subscription model to update components reactively. This architecture simplifies complex state logic, allowing for predictable and scalable state management in React apps.

20. How do HOCs differ from render props?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Higher-Order Components (HOCs) and render props are both patterns to share logic between components. HOCs wrap a component and inject additional props or behavior, while render props involve passing a function as a prop that returns JSX, giving fine-grained control over what is rendered. HOCs are more declarative but can lead to nested wrappers, whereas render props provide explicit control but may result in more verbose code. Both enable reusable, composable components but differ in syntax and flexibility.

21. How do lifecycle methods compare to hooks like useEffect?

Category: Lifecycle
Lifecycle methods in class components provide explicit hooks into the phases of a component’s existence such as mounting, updating, and unmounting. Hooks like useEffect in functional components unify these lifecycle stages into a single API, making it more flexible and composable. Unlike lifecycle methods which are segmented, useEffect allows running side effects conditionally based on dependencies, simplifying logic and improving readability. This shift also promotes functional programming principles and reduces boilerplate associated with class components.

22. How do portals help with modals and tooltips?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Portals allow rendering of components outside their parent DOM hierarchy, which is especially useful for modals and tooltips. This helps avoid CSS and stacking context issues by placing these elements at a higher level in the DOM, often directly under the body tag. Portals enable modals to overlay other content cleanly, maintain accessibility by trapping focus, and ensure correct event bubbling. They also facilitate layering and z-index management, enhancing UI behavior and user experience for floating elements.

23. How do React Suspense and Concurrent Mode work together?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Suspense and Concurrent Mode together provide a powerful approach to asynchronous rendering. Suspense allows components to “wait” for something like data or code to load by showing fallback content meanwhile. Concurrent Mode enhances this by enabling React to interrupt, pause, and resume rendering work without blocking the UI, thus providing smoother transitions and better responsiveness. Together, they help build apps that feel more fluid and performant by managing loading states and rendering priorities more effectively.

24. How do render props differ from HOCs?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Render props and Higher-Order Components (HOCs) both share component logic, but render props pass a function as a prop that returns UI, offering more explicit control over rendering. HOCs wrap components and inject props, abstracting behavior in a declarative way. Render props can reduce nesting and provide flexible, inline logic, while HOCs promote reusability but may increase complexity through wrapper layers. Each pattern has unique advantages for code reuse and composition.

25. How do render props work?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Render props work by passing a function as a child or prop to a component that returns JSX, allowing that component to control what is rendered based on internal state or logic. This technique enables sharing behavior without inheritance or wrapping, giving the consumer fine-grained control over UI composition. It enhances flexibility and code reuse, enabling dynamic rendering patterns that adjust to varying data or interactions.

26. How do screen readers interact with React apps?

Category: Accessibility
Screen readers interact with React apps by interpreting the rendered DOM and ARIA attributes to convey information audibly to users with visual impairments. React’s virtual DOM updates the real DOM dynamically, so it’s crucial to maintain semantic HTML and proper ARIA roles to ensure meaningful communication. Developers must manage focus, live regions, and keyboard navigation to provide seamless interaction. Accessibility in React requires attention to how components expose their state and structure to assistive technologies to enable effective navigation and content comprehension.

27. How do useState and useEffect work together?

Category: Hooks
useState and useEffect work together to manage state and side effects in functional components. useState allows storing and updating local component state, while useEffect lets you perform side effects like data fetching or subscriptions in response to state changes. Typically, an effect depends on certain pieces of state; when those state variables change, useEffect re-runs, keeping the component synchronized with external data or actions. This synergy forms the backbone of modern React functional component behavior.

28. How do you access DOM elements in React?

Category: Refs
Accessing DOM elements in React is primarily done using refs, which provide a way to reference a DOM node or React element directly. This access is necessary for scenarios such as managing focus, measuring dimensions, or performing imperative animations. React's ref system abstracts away the direct DOM manipulation, allowing components to maintain declarative code while interacting with the DOM when needed, thus balancing control and React’s reactive paradigm.

29. How do you access DOM elements using useRef?

Category: Hooks
useRef is a React Hook that creates a mutable ref object, which persists for the lifetime of the component. To access DOM elements, you assign the ref to a React element's ref attribute. This ref then holds the underlying DOM node, accessible via the current property. This technique enables direct manipulation or inspection of DOM nodes without triggering re-renders, fitting well into functional component architecture for imperative operations like focusing or scrolling.

30. How do you access native device features like camera and GPS?

Category: React Native
Accessing native device features such as the camera and GPS in React Native involves leveraging platform APIs through dedicated modules or community libraries. React Native bridges JavaScript code to native platform functionalities, allowing developers to request permissions, activate hardware, and handle asynchronous data from sensors. This approach provides a unified development experience across iOS and Android while harnessing device capabilities for rich mobile apps.

31. How do you access query parameters in React Router?

Category: Routing
In React Router, query parameters can be accessed using hooks that provide location information, such as useLocation. Developers parse the query string from the current URL to extract parameters, allowing components to respond dynamically based on URL state. This supports deep linking, filtering, and navigation features in single-page applications by integrating URL parameters with application state.

32. How do you apply styles in JSX?

Category: JSX
Styling in JSX can be done inline by passing JavaScript objects representing CSS properties directly to elements, or through className attributes referencing external stylesheets. Inline styles enable dynamic computation and scoped styles within components but lack full CSS capabilities such as pseudo-classes. Combining these approaches allows flexible, maintainable styling, leveraging both CSS power and React’s declarative syntax.

33. How do you avoid infinite loops with useEffect?

Category: Hooks
Infinite loops with useEffect occur when the effect updates state that triggers its own re-execution. To avoid this, dependencies must be carefully managed, ensuring the dependency array only includes variables that should cause the effect to re-run. Conditional logic inside the effect can prevent unnecessary updates. Proper understanding of dependencies and effect triggers is key to writing stable, performant hooks.

34. How do you avoid prop drilling in React applications?

Category: State Management
Prop drilling refers to passing props through many intermediate components that do not need them, which can lead to cumbersome code. Avoiding this involves using global or shared state management solutions like Context API, Redux, or Recoil, which allow components to access data directly. Breaking down components into more focused pieces and employing hooks for shared logic also reduces unnecessary prop passing, enhancing maintainability and clarity.

35. How do you avoid prop drilling?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Avoiding prop drilling involves architectural decisions such as lifting state up only as necessary and using React Context or other state management libraries to provide data where needed. This pattern keeps component hierarchies clean and focused on their responsibilities, preventing deeply nested prop chains that complicate data flow and debugging.

36. How do you avoid unnecessary re-renders in React?

Category: Performance
To avoid unnecessary re-renders, React developers optimize component updates by using memoization techniques like React.memo for functional components and shouldComponentUpdate in class components. Managing state immutably, avoiding anonymous functions in props, and carefully handling context updates also help reduce re-render frequency. Profiling tools assist in identifying and resolving performance bottlenecks.

37. How do you avoid unnecessary re-renders with Context API?

Category: Context API
Avoiding unnecessary re-renders with Context API involves minimizing context value changes by memoizing the context value and splitting contexts when different parts of the app consume unrelated data. Using useMemo or useCallback to stabilize context values and selectively subscribing components to contexts prevents excessive updates, improving performance.

38. How do you bind event handlers in class components?

Category: Events
In class components, event handlers must be bound explicitly to maintain the correct this context. This can be done in the constructor using bind(), with class fields syntax, or arrow functions. Proper binding ensures handlers correctly reference component instances, avoiding errors and enabling event-driven interactions.

39. How do you build and deploy a React Native app?

Category: React Native
Building and deploying a React Native app involves writing JavaScript and React code that targets native platforms, then using platform-specific tools such as Xcode for iOS and Android Studio for Android. The app is bundled and compiled, then distributed through app stores. Continuous integration, code signing, and environment setup are key steps in the deployment pipeline.

40. How do you cache GraphQL results?

Category: Data Fetching
Caching GraphQL results is often handled by client libraries like Apollo Client or Relay, which normalize and store query results in an in-memory cache. This reduces redundant network requests, improves performance, and enables offline capabilities. Cache policies can be configured for freshness and invalidation strategies to balance between speed and data accuracy.

41. How do you cancel an ongoing fetch request?

Category: Data Fetching
To cancel an ongoing fetch request, the AbortController API is commonly used. You create an AbortController instance and pass its signal property to the fetch call. Calling the abort() method on the controller cancels the request. This mechanism helps manage resource usage, prevent race conditions, and handle component unmounting scenarios gracefully in React applications.

42. How do you change the language dynamically in a React app?

Category: Internationalization
Dynamic language changes in React apps are often handled through internationalization libraries like react-i18next. These libraries provide context providers and hooks to switch languages at runtime, update translations dynamically, and persist user preferences. React’s reactive model allows seamless UI updates to reflect the new locale without reloading the page, improving user experience for multilingual audiences.

43. How do you clean up effects in useEffect?

Category: Hooks
useEffect cleanup is achieved by returning a function from the effect callback. This cleanup function runs before the effect runs again or when the component unmounts. It is essential for unsubscribing from events, cancelling timers, or aborting network requests to avoid memory leaks and unwanted side effects in React components.

44. How do you combine multiple reducers in Redux?

Category: State Management
Combining multiple reducers in Redux is commonly done using the combineReducers utility. It merges several reducers into one root reducer by delegating parts of the state tree to specific reducers. This modularizes state logic, keeps code organized, and simplifies state updates by isolating concerns per slice.

45. How do you combine multiple useEffect hooks?

Category: Hooks
You can combine multiple useEffect hooks by declaring them separately within the same component. React runs these effects independently in the order they appear. This approach keeps concerns isolated and the codebase more maintainable rather than mixing unrelated logic in a single effect.

46. How do you conditionally render components in React?

Category: Components
Conditional rendering in React is accomplished using JavaScript conditional statements or expressions inside JSX. Common patterns include using ternary operators, logical && operators, or if statements outside JSX to determine what components or elements to render based on application state or props.

47. How do you connect React components to Redux?

Category: State Management
React components connect to Redux using the connect function from react-redux or hooks like useSelector and useDispatch. connect wraps components, injecting state and dispatch as props, while hooks provide direct access to the store in functional components, enabling state reading and dispatching actions declaratively.

48. How do you consume context values in class and functional components?

Category: Context API
In functional components, context values are consumed via the useContext hook, which subscribes the component to context changes. In class components, context is accessed by defining a static contextType property or using a Consumer component to wrap JSX. Both methods provide access to shared data without prop drilling.

49. How do you create a catch-all 404 route in React Router?

Category: Routing
A catch-all 404 route in React Router is typically created by defining a Route without a path or using a wildcard path "*" that matches any unmatched routes. This route is placed last in the Switch or Routes to render a custom 404 page when no other routes match, providing a fallback UI for unknown paths.

50. How do you create a class component in React?

Category: Components
Class components are created by extending React.Component and implementing a render method that returns JSX. They manage state and lifecycle methods within the class body, enabling traditional object-oriented component definitions.

51. How do you create a context in React?

Category: Hooks
Creating a context in React is done via React.createContext(), which returns a Context object with Provider and Consumer components. The Provider component wraps parts of the app to supply a shared value, which can be accessed by any descendant component using Consumer or the useContext hook.

52. How do you create a controlled input component?

Category: Forms
Controlled input components have their value managed by React state. The input’s value attribute is bound to state, and changes are handled via onChange events that update this state. This approach provides full control over form elements, enabling validation, dynamic updates, and controlled submission.

53. How do you create a custom 404 page in Next.js?

Category: SSR / SSG
In Next.js, a custom 404 page is created by adding a pages/404.js file exporting a React component. Next.js automatically serves this page for all unmatched routes, allowing customized not-found UI consistent with the app’s styling and functionality.

54. How do you create a custom hook for form validation?

Category: Hooks
Custom hooks for form validation encapsulate validation logic in reusable functions that use React hooks internally. These hooks manage field values, validation status, error messages, and expose handlers for input changes, providing a clean, modular way to integrate validation in components.

55. How do you create a custom hook?

Category: Hooks
Creating a custom hook involves writing a JavaScript function whose name starts with "use" and which can use other hooks inside. This function encapsulates reusable stateful logic, allowing components to share behavior cleanly and improve code modularity.

56. How do you create a functional component in React?

Category: Components
Functional components are simple JavaScript functions that return JSX. They are stateless or use hooks for state and side effects, enabling simpler and more concise component definitions compared to class components.

57. How do you create a higher-order component (HOC) in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
A higher-order component is a function that takes a component and returns a new component with enhanced behavior or props. HOCs enable code reuse and abstraction by wrapping components and injecting additional logic, commonly used for cross-cutting concerns like logging, theming, or data fetching.

58. How do you create a layout with shared components using React Router?

Creating layouts with shared components in React Router involves defining routes that render common UI parts (like headers or sidebars) alongside nested routes. This approach allows consistent UI structure across multiple pages, improving navigation and user experience.

59. How do you create a middleware in Next.js?

Category: SSR / SSG
Middleware in Next.js is a function that runs before a request is completed, allowing tasks like authentication, redirects, or header manipulation. It is placed in the middleware.js file at the root and can intercept requests to add custom behavior server-side or at the edge.

60. How do you create a portal in React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React portals allow rendering children into a DOM node outside the parent component hierarchy. This is useful for modals, tooltips, and overlays that require different stacking contexts or to avoid CSS overflow issues while maintaining React’s declarative model.

61. How do you create a portal using ReactDOM.createPortal?

Category: Advanced Concepts
ReactDOM.createPortal is used to render a component or element outside of the main React DOM hierarchy, typically into a different DOM node such as a div outside the root app container. This is especially useful for modals, tooltips, or overlays, where isolation from parent CSS or DOM constraints is necessary, while still preserving React’s event system and lifecycle.

62. How do you create a React component?

Category: Basics
React components can be created as JavaScript functions (functional components) or ES6 classes (class components). Functional components return JSX and can use hooks for state and lifecycle, while class components extend React.Component and define a render method. Components encapsulate UI and behavior, promoting reusable and maintainable code.

63. How do you create a reusable modal component in React?

Category: Components
A reusable modal component in React is created by defining a component that manages its visibility via props or internal state and renders children as modal content. It often uses portals to overlay above other content, handles accessibility attributes like ARIA roles, and supports customizable styles and callbacks for actions like close or submit.

64. How do you create a selector using reselect?

Category: State Management
Reselect selectors are created using the createSelector function, which takes input selectors and an output function. They memoize computed state derived from Redux store slices, improving performance by recalculating only when relevant inputs change. This allows efficient and declarative state selection in complex applications.

65. How do you create a slice with Redux Toolkit?

Category: State Management
A slice in Redux Toolkit is created using createSlice, which combines reducer logic and action creators in one object. It defines initial state, reducers, and generates actions automatically, simplifying Redux code by reducing boilerplate and enabling immutable updates through Immer integration.

66. How do you create accessible React components?

Category: Accessibility
Accessible React components are built by following WAI-ARIA guidelines: adding appropriate roles, states, and properties; managing keyboard navigation; ensuring semantic HTML; providing visible focus indicators; and using tools like eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y. Testing with screen readers ensures a wide audience can interact effectively.

67. How do you create actions in Redux?

Category: State Management
Actions in Redux are plain JavaScript objects with a type property and optional payload. They describe changes in application state. You create action creators — functions that return these action objects — to encapsulate action construction, which are dispatched to update state via reducers.

68. How do you create an error boundary component?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Error boundaries are React class components that implement componentDidCatch and getDerivedStateFromError lifecycle methods to catch JavaScript errors in their child component tree. They allow rendering fallback UI when errors occur, preventing the entire app from crashing and improving resilience.

69. How do you create and use React components?

Category: Components
React components are created as either functional or class components and are used by importing and embedding them within JSX. Props are passed to customize behavior or appearance. This modular approach promotes code reuse and clearer UI structure.

70. How do you create compound components in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Compound components allow related components to share implicit state and behavior by nesting them inside a parent component. This pattern enables flexible and expressive APIs where children components communicate through context or props, simplifying complex UI compositions.

71. How do you create context and context providers in React?

Category: State Management
Context and context providers in React are created using React.createContext to generate a context object. The Provider component wraps parts of the component tree, supplying shared state or functions, which consumers access via useContext or Consumer components to avoid prop drilling.

72. How do you create context providers in React?

Category: State Management
Context providers in React are components that wrap their children with a Context.Provider, passing down the current context value. This setup allows descendant components to consume shared data or functions without explicitly receiving props at every level.

73. How do you create controlled forms in React?

Controlled forms in React are built by binding form input values to state and updating state on input changes. This pattern centralizes form data management in React, enabling validation, conditional rendering, and easy form submission handling.

Custom hooks are JavaScript functions prefixed with “use” that utilize built-in React hooks internally. They encapsulate reusable logic such as state management or side effects, promoting code reuse and modularity in functional components.

75. How do you create custom hooks to share logic?

Sharing logic with custom hooks involves creating reusable hook functions that encapsulate stateful logic or side effects. Components can then call these hooks to share behavior without duplicating code, fostering better separation of concerns.

76. How do you create custom hooks?

Category: Hooks
Creating custom hooks requires defining a function starting with “use” that calls React hooks inside it. These hooks package reusable logic for state, effects, or refs, making component code cleaner and easier to maintain.

77. How do you create custom React hooks?

Category: Hooks
Custom React hooks follow the same pattern as general custom hooks: functions starting with “use” that utilize React’s built-in hooks internally. They abstract stateful logic for reuse across components, enhancing modularity and readability.

80. How do you create Higher-Order Components (HOCs)?

This is similar to creating HOCs in React: defining a function that takes a component and returns a new component with extended functionality. HOCs abstract cross-cutting concerns like logging, theming, or data fetching to keep components clean.

81. How do you create Higher-Order Components?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Higher-Order Components (HOCs) are created by writing functions that take a component as input and return a new component with added functionality or props. This allows you to abstract repetitive logic and apply it across multiple components without modifying their internals.

87. How do you debounce events in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Debouncing events in React delays the processing of rapid, repetitive events by wrapping event handlers with debounce utilities (like lodash debounce). This reduces unnecessary renders or API calls, improving performance especially on inputs or scroll events.

88. How do you debounce input in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Debouncing input in React means postponing the processing of user input until the user stops typing for a specified delay, often done by using debounce functions in event handlers or custom hooks, to prevent excessive state updates or network requests.

89. How do you debounce input with hooks?

Category: Hooks
You can debounce input with hooks by creating a custom hook that uses useEffect and useState to delay updating a debounced value until after a specified timeout following the latest input change. This hook returns the debounced value for use in components.

90. How do you debounce or throttle events in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Debouncing delays event handling until a pause in events, while throttling limits event handling frequency. Both can be implemented using utilities (like lodash) or custom hooks in React to optimize performance during rapid events such as scroll, resize, or input.

91. How do you debounce user input in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Debouncing user input delays reacting to changes until the user stops typing, usually implemented by wrapping input change handlers with debounce functions or using debounced state updates via custom hooks to avoid excessive renders or API calls.

126. How do you forward refs to child components?

161. How do you handle error boundaries in React?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries in React are components that catch JavaScript errors anywhere in their child component tree, log those errors, and display a fallback UI instead of the component tree that crashed. They are implemented using class components with the lifecycle methods componentDidCatch and getDerivedStateFromError.

162. How do you handle error boundaries with fallback UI in React?

Category: Error Handling
To handle error boundaries with fallback UI, you define a fallback component or UI inside the error boundary's state. When an error occurs, the error boundary updates state and renders the fallback UI, providing a graceful degradation experience for users.

181. How do you handle file uploads in React?

When a file is selected, the change event handler accesses the file(s) via event.target.files. You can then upload these files to a server using FormData along with APIs like fetch or libraries such as Axios. Properly handling file size limits, types, and providing user feedback enhances the UX during uploads.

During form submission or on input changes, you validate the form data against this schema, capturing and displaying validation errors.

201. How do you handle forms with React Hook Form library?

202. How do you handle forms with React Hook Form?

The library promotes clean and efficient form handling with reduced re-renders.

213. How do you handle optimistic UI updates in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Optimistic UI updates immediately reflect changes in the UI before server confirmation to improve responsiveness. You update the local state first, then send the server request. If the request fails, you rollback the UI state and inform the user.

214. How do you handle optional props in TypeScript?

216. How do you handle redirects in Next.js?

218. How do you handle routing and navigation with React Router v6?

219. How do you handle routing guards in React Router?

Category: Routing
Routing guards protect routes based on authentication or permissions. In React Router, you can create wrapper components that check user status and either render the target component or redirect to login or error pages. This logic is often implemented using context or state.

220. How do you handle routing with React Router v6?

Category: Routing
React Router v6 manages routing through a nested route structure defined inside Routes. It supports dynamic routes, nested layouts, and declarative navigation with hooks like useNavigate. Routes are matched based on the current URL, enabling complex UI structures.

221. How do you handle server-side rendering with hydration in React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Server-side rendering (SSR) with hydration involves rendering React components to HTML on the server and sending this fully formed HTML to the client. When React loads on the client, it "hydrates" this HTML, attaching event listeners and making the app interactive without re-rendering the entire UI. This improves initial load performance and SEO. Frameworks like Next.js simplify this process by handling SSR and hydration seamlessly.

222. How do you handle side effects in React components?

Category: Hooks
Side effects such as data fetching, subscriptions, or manual DOM manipulations are handled in React functional components using the useEffect hook. This hook runs after render and can be configured to run conditionally based on dependency arrays, ensuring side effects execute correctly and cleanup when needed.

223. How do you handle side effects in React with useEffect?

Category: Hooks
The useEffect hook accepts a function that runs after the component renders. You can perform side effects like API calls or event subscriptions here. The hook also supports cleanup by returning a function to unsubscribe or clear timers. Dependency arrays control when the effect runs, optimizing performance.

224. How do you handle side effects in React?

Category: Hooks
Side effects in React are handled by placing imperative code inside useEffect or lifecycle methods in class components. This includes tasks like data fetching, subscriptions, timers, or manually changing the DOM. Proper cleanup and dependency management are critical for predictable behavior.

225. How do you handle side effects using useEffect hook in React?

Category: Hooks
Using useEffect, you write your side effect code inside the provided function argument. You can specify when it should re-run by listing dependencies. Cleanup functions ensure resources are freed or subscriptions are removed when the component unmounts or before the effect re-runs.

226. How do you handle side effects with the useEffect hook?

Category: Hooks
The useEffect hook handles side effects by running the effect function after render. Dependencies passed to the hook control when it re-executes. Cleanup functions prevent memory leaks by running before the next effect or unmounting.

227. How do you handle side effects with useEffect hook?

Category: Hooks
To handle side effects using useEffect, place the effect logic inside the hook’s callback function. Specify dependencies in an array to control when it runs. Return a cleanup function for unsubscribing or cleanup tasks.

228. How do you handle side effects with useEffect?

Category: Hooks
The useEffect hook allows you to run side effects after render, such as fetching data or setting up event listeners. Use the dependency array to optimize and prevent unnecessary runs. Always clean up subscriptions or timers to avoid leaks.

229. How do you handle state immutability in React?

Category: State Management
React relies on immutable state updates to detect changes efficiently. To handle state immutability, always create new objects or arrays instead of mutating existing ones. Use spread operators or helper functions like Array.map() or Object.assign(). This ensures predictable state updates and proper re-rendering.

230. How do you handle state lifting in React applications?

Category: State Management
State lifting involves moving shared state up to the closest common ancestor of components that need it. This allows child components to receive state and update functions as props, ensuring synchronized state management across components.

231. How do you handle touch and gestures in React Native?

232. How do you handle user events in React?

233. How do you implement a controlled form input in React?

234. How do you implement a custom hook in React?

Custom hooks are functions that use built-in React hooks to encapsulate reusable logic. They start with "use" and can manage state, side effects, or any logic. This promotes code reuse and cleaner components.

235. How do you implement a dark mode toggle in React?

236. How do you implement a HOC (Higher Order Component)?

Category: Advanced Patterns
A Higher Order Component is a function that takes a component and returns a new enhanced component. It’s used to share common logic like authorization, theming, or data fetching by wrapping the original component and injecting props or behavior.

237. How do you implement an error boundary?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries are React class components that implement componentDidCatch and getDerivedStateFromError lifecycle methods to catch errors in child components. They render fallback UI instead of crashing the whole app.

238. How do you implement authentication flows in React apps?

239. How do you implement authentication in React applications?

Implementing authentication in React involves creating login forms, managing auth tokens (usually in local storage or cookies), securing API calls with tokens, and conditionally rendering UI based on auth state. Libraries like Firebase Auth or Auth0 simplify this process.

240. How do you implement authentication in React apps?
