Authentication in React apps is implemented by integrating with backend auth services, managing tokens securely, and protecting routes. React Context or libraries like Redux can store authentication state, and React Router guards ensure only authorized users access protected routes.

241. How do you implement authentication in React?

Category: Security
Implementing authentication in React typically involves managing user sessions through tokens (like JWT), creating secure login and logout flows, and protecting routes based on user authentication state. Authentication state is often handled via React Context, Redux, or dedicated libraries, combined with API calls to backend services. You ensure secure storage of tokens (preferably HttpOnly cookies or secure storage) and use protected routes to restrict access to authorized users only.

242. How do you implement authentication with hooks?

Category: Hooks
Authentication with hooks usually involves creating custom hooks such as useAuth that encapsulate authentication logic including login, logout, and token management. These hooks leverage React’s state and effect hooks to manage user authentication state and side effects like token refresh, making it easy to consume authentication state across components.

243. How do you implement code splitting in React applications?

Category: Code Splitting
Code splitting in React is implemented to improve performance by loading parts of the app on demand rather than all at once. This is commonly done using dynamic import() statements combined with React.lazy and Suspense to lazily load components only when needed, reducing the initial bundle size.

244. How do you implement code splitting in React using dynamic import?

Category: Code Splitting
Dynamic import allows you to load modules asynchronously. In React, you use import() to dynamically load a component file, which returns a Promise. Combined with React.lazy, this allows React to defer loading the component until it’s rendered, optimizing bundle size.

245. How do you implement code splitting in React using React.lazy?

Category: Code Splitting
React.lazy allows you to define a component that is loaded lazily via dynamic import. When React encounters this component in the render tree, it suspends rendering until the module loads, which is handled with a Suspense fallback UI.

246. How do you implement code splitting in React?

Category: Code Splitting
Code splitting is implemented by dividing your app into chunks that can be loaded independently. Tools like Webpack do this automatically with dynamic imports, while React provides React.lazy and Suspense to integrate lazy loading seamlessly into your component tree.

247. How do you implement code splitting using React.lazy and Suspense?

Category: Code Splitting
You wrap lazy-loaded components created with React.lazy inside a Suspense component with a fallback UI. This fallback is displayed while the component is loading, providing a smooth user experience during code splitting.

248. How do you implement code splitting with dynamic imports?

Category: Code Splitting
Dynamic imports use the import() syntax to load modules asynchronously. In React, this is the foundation for lazy loading components, letting you split bundles and improve load time by fetching components only when required.

249. How do you implement code splitting with React Router?

Category: Routing
React Router supports code splitting by allowing routes to load components lazily using React.lazy. Each route component can be imported dynamically, and Suspense wraps the routes to show fallback UI while loading, improving initial load performance.

250. How do you implement code splitting with React.lazy and Suspense?

Category: Code Splitting
You declare your component with React.lazy using a dynamic import, then place it inside a Suspense component with a fallback UI. This pattern delays loading the component until it’s rendered, showing a loading state meanwhile.

251. How do you implement code splitting with React.lazy?

Category: Code Splitting
React.lazy lets you define components that load only when needed. You wrap the import in React.lazy and use Suspense to handle the loading state, effectively splitting your code into smaller bundles loaded on demand.

252. How do you implement compound components in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Compound components allow grouping related components together to share implicit state and logic, typically by using React Context. This pattern enables flexible APIs where child components can communicate with the parent and each other without explicit prop drilling.

253. How do you implement conditional rendering in React?

Category: Core Concepts
Conditional rendering in React can be achieved using JavaScript conditional expressions like if statements, ternary operators, and logical && operators inside JSX. This controls what elements render based on component state or props.

254. How do you implement context API for global state management?

Category: State Management
The Context API lets you create a context provider component to hold global state and pass it to any component via a context consumer or the useContext hook. This eliminates prop drilling and centralizes shared state.

255. How do you implement context in React?

Category: State Management
You create a context object with React.createContext(), then wrap components with a provider that supplies context value. Components use the context by consuming it via useContext or context consumers.

256. How do you implement context selectors for optimizing React Context?

Category: State Management
Context selectors allow components to subscribe to only parts of the context state, reducing unnecessary re-renders. This is achieved by creating selector functions and custom hooks that compare selected values and update components only when those values change.

257. How do you implement controlled inputs in React forms?

Category: Forms
Controlled inputs are form inputs whose value is controlled by React state. You bind the input's value to a state variable and update the state via onChange handlers, ensuring React fully controls the form data.

258. How do you implement custom hooks in React?

Category: Hooks
Custom hooks are functions that reuse hook logic for state, side effects, or context. They start with "use" and let you abstract logic from components, improving code reuse and readability.

259. How do you implement custom validations with React Hook Form?

Category: Forms
React Hook Form supports custom validations via the validate option in register. You provide functions that return true if valid or an error message string if invalid, allowing flexible and reusable validation rules.

260. How do you implement dark mode in React applications?

Category: Styling
Dark mode can be implemented by toggling theme state and applying corresponding CSS classes or CSS variables. Using React Context or state management libraries helps propagate the theme choice app-wide. CSS-in-JS libraries also offer dynamic style changes based on theme.

261. How do you implement dark mode in React Native?

Category: React Native
In React Native, dark mode implementation commonly involves detecting the system color scheme using the useColorScheme hook, which returns 'light' or 'dark'. You then conditionally apply styles or themes based on this value. Libraries like React Navigation and Styled Components support theming out of the box, making it easier to switch between dark and light themes dynamically.

262. How do you implement dark mode in React?

Category: Styling
Dark mode in React is typically implemented by managing a theme state—either with React Context or state management libraries—that toggles between light and dark themes. These themes define different CSS variables, classes, or styles. By conditionally applying these styles or using CSS-in-JS libraries, you provide users with a consistent dark or light appearance across your app.

263. How do you implement dark mode toggle in React?

Category: Styling
A dark mode toggle involves creating a user interface element (like a switch or button) that updates theme state on user interaction. This toggle changes the current theme between light and dark by updating state stored in React Context or a global store, triggering a re-render with the appropriate styles applied.

264. How do you implement dark mode using CSS-in-JS in React?

Category: Styling
CSS-in-JS libraries like Styled Components or Emotion support dynamic theming by allowing you to define theme objects and provide them via a ThemeProvider. You can switch between light and dark theme objects and styles update automatically. Components receive theme props that can be used to conditionally style elements based on the current mode.

265. How do you implement dynamic routing in React Router?

Category: Routing
Dynamic routing in React Router is achieved by defining routes with URL parameters (like :id) that allow routes to match dynamic segments. Components receive these parameters via hooks like useParams, enabling dynamic content rendering based on the URL.

266. How do you implement dynamic routing with React Router?

Category: Routing
By setting up routes with dynamic path segments and using React Router's hooks to extract route parameters, you can render different components or fetch data dynamically based on the URL. This lets you build flexible navigation systems that adapt to user input or data.

267. How do you implement dynamic theming in React?

Category: Styling
Dynamic theming means allowing your app's styles and appearance to change based on user preferences or system settings. This involves managing a theme state and passing theme variables via context or theming libraries, and applying styles that respond to theme changes in real-time.

268. How do you implement error boundaries in React?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries are React components that catch JavaScript errors anywhere in their child component tree, log those errors, and display a fallback UI instead of the crashed component tree. You create error boundaries by extending React.Component and implementing componentDidCatch and getDerivedStateFromError.

269. How do you implement error boundaries?

Category: Error Handling
To implement error boundaries, create a class component with state to track errors, override getDerivedStateFromError to update the state when an error occurs, and override componentDidCatch to log or report the error. Wrap the components you want to protect with this boundary.

270. How do you implement focus management in modals or dialogs?

Category: Accessibility
Proper focus management means trapping keyboard focus within the modal/dialog when open, restoring focus to the triggering element when closed, and setting initial focus inside the modal. This can be achieved using refs, event handlers, and focus-trap utilities, ensuring keyboard and screen reader users can navigate effectively.

271. How do you implement form validation in React?

Category: Forms
Form validation can be implemented using controlled inputs that validate input values on change or submit. You can build custom validation logic or use libraries like Yup or React Hook Form, which provide declarative validation schemas and error message handling.

272. How do you implement form validation with React Hook Form?

Category: Forms
React Hook Form simplifies validation by letting you register inputs and define validation rules inline or via schema validation with Yup. It automatically tracks errors and provides methods to show error messages and handle form state efficiently.

273. How do you implement global error handling in React?

Category: Error Handling
Global error handling often uses error boundaries for UI errors, combined with centralized error logging and reporting services. You can also use React Context or state management to track errors and show global notifications or fallback UI when errors occur.

274. How do you implement global state management with Redux Toolkit?

Category: State Management
Redux Toolkit simplifies Redux by providing functions to create slices, reducers, and actions automatically. You configure the store with configureStore, define slices with initial state and reducers, and use React-Redux hooks to connect components to the global state.

275. How do you implement global state management with Redux?

Category: State Management
Global state management with Redux involves creating a store, defining actions and reducers, and connecting components via the React-Redux library. State updates happen through dispatched actions, and components subscribe to store changes to render updated data.

276. How do you implement global state with Redux?

Category: State Management
The process is the same as with Redux in general—create a centralized store, define your state shape, and update it via dispatched actions processed by reducers. Use React-Redux hooks to read from and dispatch to the store globally.

277. How do you implement Higher-Order Components (HOCs) in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
HOCs are functions that take a component and return a new component with enhanced behavior. They allow reuse of component logic by wrapping the original component and injecting props or state. Common uses include injecting data, handling state, or adding lifecycle hooks.

278. How do you implement Higher-Order Components (HOCs)?

Category: Advanced Patterns
The implementation involves creating a function that returns a new component wrapping the input component, optionally enhancing it with additional props, state, or side effects, thus promoting code reuse and separation of concerns.

279. How do you implement Higher-Order Components?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Higher-Order Components wrap existing components to provide additional functionality without modifying their source. This pattern is useful for cross-cutting concerns like logging, theming, or data fetching by composing behavior.

280. How do you implement HOCs?

Category: Advanced Concepts
HOCs are functions returning enhanced components by wrapping the original one, allowing logic reuse, state management, or props injection in a declarative manner, supporting component composition and scalability.

281. How do you implement incremental static regeneration (ISR) in React frameworks like Next.js?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Incremental Static Regeneration (ISR) in Next.js allows you to update static pages after build time without rebuilding the entire site. You specify a revalidate interval in your page's getStaticProps function. After this time passes, the next request triggers a background regeneration of the page, serving stale content while regenerating, thus combining the performance of static pages with the freshness of server-side rendering.

282. How do you implement infinite scrolling in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Infinite scrolling loads content continuously as the user scrolls near the bottom of a list or page. In React, this is implemented by detecting scroll position, either with scroll event listeners or the Intersection Observer API. When the user reaches near the end, a new batch of data is fetched and appended, creating a seamless loading experience without pagination buttons.

283. How do you implement infinite scrolling with Intersection Observer in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Using the Intersection Observer API, you can observe a sentinel element placed at the bottom of your list. When this element comes into view, the observer triggers a callback that fetches more data and updates the list. This method is efficient, as it avoids expensive scroll event handlers and performs well across browsers.

284. How do you implement internationalization (i18n) in React applications?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Internationalization in React involves preparing your app to support multiple languages and regional formats. This is commonly done by using libraries such as react-intl or i18next. These tools provide mechanisms to load locale-specific messages, format dates/numbers, and switch languages dynamically. You typically structure translation files and use translation hooks or components throughout your app to render localized content.

285. How do you implement internationalization (i18n) in React apps?

Category: Advanced Patterns
The process includes setting up a translation framework, creating language resource files, wrapping your app with a provider that supplies the current language context, and using translation functions or components to display translated strings. Managing pluralization, formatting, and language switching is handled by the i18n library.

286. How do you implement internationalization (i18n) in React?

Category: Advanced Features
To implement i18n, choose a library that fits your needs, organize your translations by language and namespace, and provide a way for users to switch languages. Use hooks or higher-order components from the i18n library to inject translation functions and render localized UI elements.

287. How do you implement lazy loading components with React.lazy?

Category: Code Splitting
React.lazy enables you to dynamically import components only when they are needed. By wrapping a dynamic import with React.lazy and rendering it inside a Suspense component, React can split your code and reduce initial bundle size, improving performance.

288. How do you implement lazy loading for images in React?

Category: Code Splitting
Lazy loading images can be achieved by using the native loading="lazy" attribute on <img> tags or by leveraging Intersection Observer to detect when images enter the viewport, then triggering the loading of the image source dynamically.

289. How do you implement lazy loading for React components?

Category: Code Splitting
Use React.lazy with dynamic imports to defer loading components until they are rendered. Wrap lazy-loaded components with Suspense to provide a fallback UI during loading.

290. How do you implement lazy loading images in React?

Category: Code Splitting
Similar to previous answers, lazy loading images can be done via native browser support or custom Intersection Observer logic to load images only when about to scroll into view, saving bandwidth and improving perceived performance.

291. How do you implement lazy loading in React applications?

Category: Code Splitting
Lazy loading in React involves splitting code with React.lazy, dynamic imports, or third-party libraries, allowing parts of the UI or resources to load on demand rather than all at once, which improves initial load speed and user experience.

292. How do you implement lazy loading in React?

Category: Code Splitting
This is achieved through dynamic import syntax combined with React.lazy and Suspense. You can split your bundles by route, component, or feature, improving load times by only loading necessary code initially.

293. How do you implement lazy loading of images in React?

Category: Code Splitting
Techniques include using native loading="lazy", Intersection Observer API, or third-party libraries that detect when images enter the viewport and load them accordingly, reducing initial page load size.

294. How do you implement lazy loading with React Router?

Category: Code Splitting
React Router supports lazy loading by combining dynamic imports with React.lazy inside route definitions, so that route components are loaded only when the route matches, reducing the initial JavaScript bundle size.

295. How do you implement lazy loading with React.lazy and Suspense?

Category: Code Splitting
Wrap the dynamically imported component using React.lazy and embed it inside a Suspense component with a fallback UI to handle loading states gracefully during the asynchronous import.

296. How do you implement lazy loading with React.lazy?

Category: Code Splitting
Use React.lazy(() => import('path/to/component')) to defer component loading until render time, improving bundle efficiency by splitting code.

297. How do you implement memoization for expensive React computations?

Category: Performance Optimization
Memoization caches the result of expensive computations to avoid recalculating on every render. In React, use useMemo to memoize values or computations that depend on specific inputs, which optimizes rendering by recalculating only when dependencies change.

298. How do you implement memoization in React components?

Category: Performance Optimization
Use React hooks like useMemo to memoize computed values and React.memo to memoize functional components, preventing unnecessary renders when props haven't changed.

299. How do you implement navigation guards in React Router?

Category: Routing
Navigation guards in React Router are typically implemented by wrapping routes or route components with logic that checks for authentication or permissions. If the check fails, the user can be redirected or shown an error. This can be done with custom route components or hooks that run before rendering the route.

300. How do you implement optimistic UI updates in React applications?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Optimistic UI updates update the UI immediately in response to user actions before receiving server confirmation. In React, this involves updating state right away, then sending the server request. If the request fails, revert the UI state or show an error. This improves perceived performance and user experience.

301. How do you implement optimistic UI updates in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Optimistic UI updates in React involve updating the interface immediately in response to user interactions, assuming the backend operation will succeed. This is typically done by first updating local component or global state to reflect the user's action, then sending the request to the server. If the server confirms success, the UI remains as updated; if the request fails, the UI state is rolled back or adjusted to indicate the failure. This approach improves user experience by providing instant feedback, reducing perceived latency.

302. How do you implement optimistic UI updates?

Category: Advanced Patterns
The general pattern for optimistic UI updates involves:
  • Immediately updating the UI state to reflect the desired change.
  • Performing the asynchronous operation (e.g., API call).
  • If the operation succeeds, confirming the change.
  • If the operation fails, reverting the UI to the previous state or showing an error message.
This pattern requires careful management of state to avoid inconsistencies and providing user feedback in case of errors.

303. How do you implement optimistic updates in Redux?

Category: State Management
In Redux, optimistic updates are managed by dispatching an action to immediately update the state before the asynchronous API call completes. Middleware such as Redux Thunk or Redux Saga can handle the async logic. If the API call fails, another action is dispatched to rollback the optimistic update or handle errors. This ensures the UI stays responsive while maintaining consistency with backend state.

304. How do you implement polling in data fetching?

Category: Data Fetching
Polling is a technique to repeatedly fetch data from a server at fixed intervals to keep the UI updated with the latest information. This can be implemented by setting up a timer (e.g., using setInterval) that triggers a fetch function periodically, then updating state with the new data on each response. Care should be taken to clear the interval when the component unmounts to avoid memory leaks.

305. How do you implement polling with hooks?

Category: Hooks
Using React hooks, polling can be implemented with useEffect and setInterval. Inside useEffect, set an interval to fetch data at desired intervals. The effect cleanup function clears the interval when the component unmounts or dependencies change, preventing leaks or unwanted fetches.

306. How do you implement portal-based modals in React?

Category: Advanced Features
Portal-based modals use React Portals to render modal content outside the root DOM hierarchy, typically directly into a DOM node like document.body. This allows modals to overlay other content without CSS complications. React's createPortal function is used to specify the DOM node where the modal should be rendered.

307. How do you implement portals for modals in React?

Category: Advanced Features
To implement portals for modals, create a DOM element outside your app's root, then use React's createPortal to render modal content into this element. This helps in managing stacking context, z-index, and accessibility concerns, ensuring modals behave correctly across the app.

308. How do you implement portals in React?

Category: Advanced Features
React portals allow rendering components into DOM nodes outside the main React tree. Use ReactDOM.createPortal(children, container) to specify where in the DOM to mount the component subtree, which is useful for modals, tooltips, or overlays that must escape CSS overflow or z-index constraints.

309. How do you implement protected routes in React Router?

Category: Routing
Protected routes restrict access based on user authentication or roles. In React Router, this is done by wrapping the route component with a guard component that checks auth state. If unauthorized, it redirects users to a login page or error screen; if authorized, it renders the requested component.

310. How do you implement React Portals and when are they useful?

Category: Advanced Features
React Portals provide a way to render children into a DOM node outside the parent hierarchy. They are useful for UI elements that need to visually break out of container bounds, such as modals, tooltips, or dropdown menus, ensuring they appear above other content and behave correctly regardless of CSS stacking context.

311. How do you implement React Router for navigation?

Category: Routing
React Router provides declarative routing for React apps. Navigation is implemented by defining routes using <Route> components inside a <Router> and using components like <Link> or hooks like useNavigate to programmatically change routes, creating single-page app navigation without full page reloads.

312. How do you implement React Suspense for data fetching?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense can be combined with data fetching libraries that support Suspense (e.g., React Query, Relay). Suspense allows components to "wait" for asynchronous data by throwing a promise during rendering. React then shows fallback UI until the data is ready, simplifying loading states management.

313. How do you implement React.memo and when should you use it?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo is a higher-order component that memoizes functional components, preventing re-renders when props have not changed. Use it to optimize performance in components that render often with unchanged props, reducing unnecessary rendering cycles.

314. How do you implement React.memo for functional components?

Category: Performance Optimization
Wrap your functional component export with React.memo, optionally providing a custom comparison function. This tells React to skip rendering the component if its props haven't changed, enhancing render performance.

315. How do you implement route guards in React apps?

Category: Routing
Route guards control access to routes based on conditions like authentication. They are implemented by wrapping route components with guard logic that redirects or blocks unauthorized users, ensuring protected content is secure.

316. How do you implement routing guards with React Router?

Category: Routing
React Router routing guards are custom components that check user state before rendering routes. If the user is unauthorized, they redirect to login or error pages; if authorized, they render the requested route component. This pattern protects sensitive routes.

317. How do you implement routing in React applications?

Category: Routing
Routing in React apps is implemented by integrating React Router, which manages URL changes and maps paths to components. Developers define route paths and associated components within a Router context, enabling seamless single-page application navigation.

318. How do you implement routing in React apps?

Category: Routing
React apps implement routing using React Router by defining route components, linking pages using <Link> or navigation hooks, and handling URL changes to render corresponding components without full page reloads.

319. How do you implement routing in React?

Category: Routing
Routing is handled by using React Router, which listens to URL changes and renders the matching component. Developers set up BrowserRouter or HashRouter, then use Route components to specify paths and corresponding UI.

320. How do you implement routing using React Router v6?

Category: Routing
React Router v6 simplifies routing with a nested routes API and hooks. Use <Routes> to wrap <Route> components, and leverage hooks like useNavigate for programmatic navigation. Routes are matched exclusively and support layout nesting.

321. How do you implement routing using React Router?

Category: Routing
React Router is used by setting up a Router component wrapping the app, defining Route components for paths and components, and utilizing Link or navigation hooks for changing routes without reloads.

322. How do you implement routing with React Router v6?

Category: Routing
React Router v6 uses <Routes> for grouping <Route> components, supports nested routes, and exclusive matching. It encourages hooks like useNavigate for navigation and useParams for URL params.

323. How do you implement routing with React Router?

Category: Routing
Routing with React Router involves wrapping your app in a Router component, defining routes with Route, and navigating via Link or hooks, enabling declarative, client-side navigation.

324. How do you implement server-side rendering (SSR) in React apps?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Server-side rendering in React involves rendering components on the server to HTML markup before sending to the client. This improves SEO and performance by delivering fully rendered pages. Libraries like Next.js provide built-in SSR capabilities. SSR requires synchronizing server and client state, often involving hydration on the client to attach event handlers.

325. How do you implement server-side rendering (SSR) with Next.js?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Next.js simplifies SSR by automatically rendering pages on the server and serving HTML to clients. Developers use functions like getServerSideProps to fetch data at request time, enabling fully server-rendered pages with data hydration on the client. Next.js also supports hybrid static & server rendering.

326. How do you implement server-side rendering (SSR) with React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
To implement SSR with React, you render React components to HTML on the server using ReactDOMServer.renderToString(). The HTML is sent to the client, where React hydrates it to enable interactivity. You also handle data fetching on the server, passing the data as props to components to avoid loading states on initial render.

327. How do you implement server-side rendering with hydration?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Hydration is the process of attaching React's event listeners to server-rendered HTML. After rendering on the server and sending markup to the client, React uses ReactDOM.hydrate() to bind the React app to existing DOM nodes, enabling full interactivity without re-rendering the entire UI.

328. How do you implement server-side rendering with Next.js?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Next.js automatically performs SSR for pages unless configured otherwise. Developers fetch data via server-side functions like getServerSideProps, returning props to the page component. Next.js renders the page to HTML on the server and sends it to the client, where hydration attaches React functionality.

329. How do you implement server-side rendering with React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
SSR with React requires rendering React components to static HTML markup on the server and sending it to clients. Then React hydrates this markup client-side for dynamic behavior. This involves coordinating data fetching on the server, routing, and managing state consistency.

330. How do you implement side effects in React?

Category: Hooks
Side effects, such as data fetching, subscriptions, or DOM mutations, are implemented using the useEffect hook. This hook runs after render and can return a cleanup function to handle resource disposal. Dependencies control when the effect runs, allowing optimized and controlled side effects.

331. How do you implement state management using Redux Toolkit?

Category: State Management
Redux Toolkit simplifies Redux by providing utilities to create slices of state with reducers and actions in one place. You define a slice with createSlice, configure the store with configureStore, and use hooks like useDispatch and useSelector for accessing and updating state in components, making Redux easier and more efficient.

332. How do you implement state management with MobX in React?

Category: State Management
MobX manages state by using observable state containers and reactions. In React, observable stores are created, and components are wrapped with observer to reactively update when observables change. MobX uses transparent reactive programming, making state management simpler and automatic.

333. How do you implement state management with Redux Toolkit?

Category: State Management
Redux Toolkit provides standardized patterns for Redux setup. Create slices with createSlice combining reducers and actions, configure store with configureStore, and leverage thunk middleware for async logic, making state management streamlined and less boilerplate-heavy.

334. How do you implement suspense for data fetching in React?

Category: Code Splitting
Suspense for data fetching involves using React's Suspense boundary to suspend rendering until data is ready. Data fetching libraries integrate by throwing promises during render, triggering Suspense to display fallback content until the data resolves, simplifying asynchronous UI logic.

335. How do you implement Suspense in React?

Category: Code Splitting
Suspense is implemented by wrapping parts of the UI in a <Suspense fallback="..."/> component, which renders fallback content while the child components are loading asynchronously. It works well with code-splitting and data fetching.

336. How do you initialize state in a class component?

Category: State
In class components, state is initialized in the constructor by assigning an object to this.state. Alternatively, state can be initialized as a class field without a constructor, setting initial values for component data.

337. How do you initialize state lazily with useState?

Category: Hooks
To lazily initialize state with useState, pass a function to useState that returns the initial state. This function is called only once during the initial render, improving performance if initial state calculation is expensive.

338. How do you integrate React with Redux Toolkit?

Category: State Management
React integrates with Redux Toolkit by wrapping the app with <Provider> from react-redux, passing the configured store. Components use hooks like useSelector to read state and useDispatch to dispatch actions, connecting React to the Redux state flow.

339. How do you integrate React with TypeScript?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React with TypeScript involves typing component props, state, and hooks to enable static type checking. Functional components use React.FC or typed function signatures, while hooks get type annotations, enhancing developer experience and code safety.

340. How do you integrate Redux with React Router?

Category: State Management
Integrating Redux with React Router involves coordinating application state with routing state to maintain synchronization between UI and URL changes. Typically, you wrap your app with Redux’s <Provider> to supply the Redux store, and React Router’s <BrowserRouter> (or other router) for routing. To connect navigation events and state updates, you can listen to routing changes inside Redux middleware or use libraries like connected-react-router (deprecated, but still relevant in some apps) or modern alternatives that allow dispatching navigation actions as Redux actions. This integration enables time-travel debugging, dispatching navigation programmatically, and syncing URL changes with Redux state.

341. How do you lazy-load routes in React Router?

Category: Routing
Lazy-loading routes in React Router is achieved by combining React’s React.lazy and Suspense with dynamic imports. Instead of importing route components statically, you import them lazily which defers loading until the route is matched. Wrap the route components inside a <Suspense fallback="..."> to show a fallback UI during loading. This approach reduces the initial bundle size and improves app performance by loading only the necessary code when needed.

342. How do you lift state up in a component hierarchy?

Category: State Management
Lifting state up means moving state to the closest common ancestor of components that need to share it. Instead of keeping local state in individual child components, you move the state up to a parent component and pass it down via props. This allows multiple components to stay in sync and share data consistently. The parent also handles updating the state, usually through callbacks passed down to children. This pattern avoids duplication and helps maintain a single source of truth for shared state.

343. How do you lift state up in React?

Category: State Management
In React, lifting state up involves moving the state from a child component to its nearest common ancestor that requires access to that state. The parent component maintains the state and passes the data and update handlers as props to children, allowing coordinated interaction. This approach is a core pattern for managing shared state without external libraries and promotes clear data flow.

344. How do you make a React app accessible?

Category: Accessibility
Making a React app accessible involves following web accessibility standards such as WCAG. This includes using semantic HTML elements, providing appropriate ARIA attributes, ensuring keyboard navigability, managing focus states, and providing screen reader support. Use tools like ESLint plugins for accessibility, React-specific libraries (like react-aria), and test with screen readers and keyboard-only navigation. Proper contrast, clear focus indicators, and descriptive labels also improve accessibility.

345. How do you manage accessibility in React applications?

Category: Accessibility
Managing accessibility in React apps requires integrating accessibility considerations throughout development. Use semantic HTML, ensure interactive elements have keyboard focus and ARIA roles, provide meaningful alt text for images, and handle dynamic content updates for screen readers. Utilize React libraries like react-aria or reach-ui for accessible components. Regularly audit with automated tools and manual testing, and keep accessibility in mind during design and development.

346. How do you manage accessibility in React apps?

Category: Accessibility
Accessibility management in React involves consistent use of semantic markup, proper ARIA attributes, and ensuring all UI elements are reachable and usable via keyboard and assistive technologies. Developers should also manage focus, handle announcements for screen readers, and provide accessible forms and error messages. Testing with tools like Axe, Lighthouse, and manual keyboard navigation is essential to catch issues early.

347. How do you manage accessibility in React components?

Category: Accessibility
Managing accessibility within React components means designing components with built-in support for keyboard navigation, proper ARIA roles, and semantic HTML. Components should handle focus management, provide labels, and support screen readers. Reusable components should expose props for accessibility features and follow best practices to ensure they can be used accessibly throughout the app.

Animations in React are managed using CSS transitions or animations, JavaScript libraries like react-spring or framer-motion, or CSS-in-JS solutions. React’s component lifecycle or hooks like useEffect can trigger animation states. For complex animations, libraries provide declarative APIs for smooth, performant animations that integrate well with React’s rendering cycle.

349. How do you manage authentication in React apps?

Category: Security
Authentication management in React involves handling user login state, token storage, and access control. Commonly, apps use libraries like firebase-auth, Auth0, or custom backend APIs. Tokens (like JWTs) are stored securely (e.g., HttpOnly cookies or secure storage) and included in API requests. React context or state management libraries hold authentication status, and protected routes restrict access accordingly.

350. How do you manage authentication state in React apps?

Category: Security
Authentication state is managed using React Context, Redux, or other global state tools. This state tracks if a user is logged in, user details, and tokens. It persists across sessions using mechanisms like localStorage, cookies, or secure storage. Components subscribe to this state to render conditionally, and actions like login/logout update the state accordingly to control access and UI.

351. How do you manage complex state with useReducer?

Category: Hooks
The useReducer hook is ideal for managing complex state logic in React components. It follows a reducer pattern similar to Redux, where state transitions are driven by dispatched actions. You define a reducer function that takes the current state and an action object, returning the new state. This approach is particularly useful when state changes involve multiple sub-values or when the next state depends on the previous one. It helps improve readability and maintainability in large components.

352. How do you manage component lifecycle in functional components?

Category: Hooks
In React functional components, lifecycle management is handled using the useEffect hook. This hook lets you perform side effects like data fetching, subscriptions, or manual DOM manipulations. You can simulate different lifecycle phases: componentDidMount by passing an empty dependency array, componentDidUpdate by including dependencies, and componentWillUnmount by returning a cleanup function. This provides a flexible and declarative way to hook into lifecycle events.

353. How do you manage component lifecycle in React functional components?

Category: Hooks
React functional components manage lifecycle events using the useEffect hook. With this hook, you can run logic after render (mounting), on updates to specific dependencies, and during unmounting via a cleanup function. This allows developers to replicate class component lifecycle methods like componentDidMount, componentDidUpdate, and componentWillUnmount in a more declarative and functional style.

354. How do you manage component state with useReducer?

Category: Hooks
The useReducer hook is a powerful alternative to useState for managing component state in React. It's particularly effective for state that has complex transitions or when multiple sub-values need to be updated together. You define a reducer function to describe how state updates should occur based on actions. The hook returns the current state and a dispatch function, enabling a more structured and predictable state update flow.

355. How do you manage component state with Zustand or Recoil?

Zustand and Recoil offer modern state management alternatives to Redux. Zustand provides a minimalistic, hook-based API for global state, allowing you to define state and actions in a single store. Recoil introduces atoms and selectors for fine-grained, reactive state, ideal for component-level subscriptions. Both libraries integrate seamlessly with React and are suitable for scalable applications that need flexible and performant state management solutions.

356. How do you manage focus for accessibility in React?

Managing focus for accessibility in React involves programmatically setting focus to relevant elements based on user interaction or navigation. For accessibility, always ensure focus is visible and logical, especially for modals, form submissions, and dynamic content updates. 357. How do you manage focus in React applications?

In React applications, focus management is typically handled using the ref and useEffect hooks. Libraries like focus-trap-react or React Aria can help automate complex focus behavior.

358. How do you manage focus in React apps?

Managing focus in React apps is crucial for accessibility and user experience. Use ref to gain direct access to DOM nodes and element.focus() to programmatically set focus. Focus should follow logical UI flow, such as returning focus to a trigger after closing a modal. Maintain focus visibility and consider edge cases like keyboard-only users or screen readers for a complete solution.

359. How do you manage focus in React components for accessibility?

In React components, focus for accessibility is managed by assigning ref to DOM elements and controlling focus with useEffect. For example, when a modal opens, you can set focus to the first interactive element. Make sure to also return focus when the component unmounts. This helps users using screen readers or keyboard navigation maintain context and usability throughout their interaction.

360. How do you manage focus in React for accessibility?

To manage focus in React for accessibility, use ref to access DOM nodes and apply focus() based on user interactions or component lifecycle. 361. How do you manage focus with hooks?

Category: Hooks
You can manage focus in React using hooks by combining useRef with useEffect. Create a reference to a DOM element with useRef and set focus using ref.current.focus() inside useEffect. This is useful for setting initial focus on inputs or restoring focus after UI updates. Custom hooks can also encapsulate focus logic for reusable accessibility patterns.

Zustand and Recoil offer simpler and more modern APIs with minimal boilerplate.

375. How do you manage global state in React?

376. How do you manage global state using Context API?

Category: State Management
The Context API allows you to create a shared state accessible throughout your React component tree without prop drilling. You create a context with React.createContext(), provide it via a Context.Provider, and consume it using useContext. It’s ideal for global data like themes or user authentication, though not optimal for high-frequency updates or deeply nested state trees.

377. How do you manage global state using Recoil?

Category: State Management
Recoil manages global state through atoms and selectors. Atoms are units of state that components can subscribe to using the useRecoilState or useRecoilValue hooks. Selectors derive computed state. Recoil enables fine-grained state updates, is efficient in large apps, and supports asynchronous data flows, making it suitable for scalable global state management.

378. How do you manage global state using Redux?

Category: State Management
Redux manages global state through a centralized store, actions, and reducers. Components dispatch actions to update the store, and use selectors or useSelector to access state. Middleware like Redux Thunk or Saga handle side effects. Redux provides a robust and predictable structure, making it ideal for large-scale applications with complex state logic.

379. How do you manage global state using Zustand?

Category: State Management
Zustand provides a minimal and flexible approach to global state using hooks. You define a store using a function, and access or update state with custom hooks created via Zustand. It avoids boilerplate and supports persistence, middleware, and subscription-based updates. Zustand is suitable for small to medium apps requiring simplicity and performance.

380. How do you manage global state with Context API?

Managing global state with the Context API involves wrapping components with a context provider and using useContext to consume shared data. It's best for low-frequency updates such as theme toggling or user roles. For more dynamic state needs, consider combining Context with useReducer or moving to a dedicated state library.

While effective for small to medium apps, performance may degrade with deeply nested components or frequent updates.

Recoil supports async selectors for data fetching and offers a simple API for modular and scalable global state management.

383. How do you manage global state with Recoil?

384. How do you manage global state with Redux Toolkit?

385. How do you manage global state with Redux?

386. How do you manage global state with Zustand?

Zustand uses a functional store model where you define state and actions in a single store hook. You access or mutate state with selectors and functions returned from that hook. Zustand supports middleware, persistence, and scalable architecture with minimal setup, making it a strong choice for modern apps requiring fast and flexible state solutions.

387. How do you manage multiple form inputs in React?

388. How do you manage side effects in functional components?

Category: Hooks
In functional components, side effects are managed using the useEffect hook. This hook allows you to perform operations like data fetching, subscriptions, or DOM manipulation after the component renders. You can control when the effect runs by specifying dependencies, and return a cleanup function to handle unmounting or updates.

389. How do you manage side effects in React with useEffect?

Category: Hooks
You manage side effects in React with the useEffect hook by placing effect logic inside the hook’s callback. It runs after the render phase and can be configured to run only on mount, on dependency change, or on unmount with a cleanup function. This makes it useful for API calls, event listeners, or timers.

390. How do you manage side effects in React?

Category: Hooks
React manages side effects primarily with the useEffect hook, allowing you to synchronize your component with external systems. You can execute asynchronous tasks like API requests, subscriptions, or DOM updates after a render. Cleanup logic can be returned from the effect to handle unmounting or re-runs when dependencies change.

391. How do you manage side effects using React Query?

Category: State Management
React Query abstracts and simplifies managing side effects related to asynchronous data fetching, caching, and synchronization with the server. It provides hooks like useQuery and useMutation that handle background fetching, automatic retries, caching, and update synchronization without manually writing effects. This leads to cleaner components and improved performance by reducing unnecessary fetches.

392. How do you manage side effects using the useEffect hook?

Category: Hooks
You manage side effects with the useEffect hook by defining the side effect logic inside the hook’s callback function. You specify dependencies to control when the effect runs, such as on component mount or when specific values change. Cleanup functions returned from the effect handle tasks like unsubscribing or clearing timers when the component unmounts or updates.

393. How do you manage side effects using useEffect hook?

Category: Hooks
Managing side effects with the useEffect hook involves placing the effect logic inside the hook's callback, with an optional dependency array to control its execution. This allows for effects like data fetching or event subscriptions to run at appropriate times. Cleanup is done by returning a function from the effect to clean resources when necessary.

394. How do you manage side effects using useEffect?

Category: Hooks
The useEffect hook manages side effects by running after the render phase. You provide a function that contains the side effect, and optionally a dependency array to control when it runs. Common uses include data fetching, event listeners, and DOM updates, with cleanup functions to avoid memory leaks.

395. How do you manage side effects with custom hooks?

Category: Hooks
Custom hooks encapsulate side effect logic using useEffect and other hooks, allowing reuse across components. By abstracting side effects such as data fetching, subscriptions, or timers into custom hooks, you keep components clean and promote modularity, testability, and consistency.

396. How do you manage side effects with React Query?

Category: State Management
React Query manages side effects related to asynchronous data fetching by automating cache management, background updates, retries, and stale data synchronization. Its hooks like useQuery trigger fetches and manage loading and error states, abstracting away boilerplate and providing a declarative way to handle data side effects efficiently.

397. How do you manage side effects with Redux Saga?

Redux Saga manages side effects by using generator functions called sagas that listen for dispatched Redux actions and perform asynchronous tasks like API calls or complex workflows. It enables declarative effects, cancellation, and concurrency control, making side effect management more manageable and testable in Redux applications.

398. How do you manage side effects with the useEffect hook?

The useEffect hook is the primary way to handle side effects in functional React components. Place your side effect code inside its callback, define dependencies to optimize execution, and provide a cleanup function if needed to clean resources on unmount or before re-running the effect.

399. How do you manage side effects with useEffect?

Category: Hooks
Use useEffect to manage side effects by specifying a function that runs after rendering. This function can perform asynchronous operations, event subscriptions, or manual DOM updates. Optionally return a cleanup function to handle teardown, and use the dependency array to control when the effect runs.

400. How do you manage state lifting in React?

Category: State Management
State lifting involves moving shared state up to the nearest common ancestor of components that need to access or modify it. This allows multiple components to synchronize their behavior based on the same state. Typically, the ancestor passes state and setters down via props, facilitating controlled component interactions.

401. How do you manage state using the useState hook?

Category: Hooks
The useState hook provides a simple way to add state to functional components by returning a state variable and a setter function. You initialize the state with a value, and React ensures updates trigger re-renders. It’s best suited for local, simple state and supports lazy initialization.

402. How do you manage state with Recoil?

Category: State Management
Recoil manages state using atoms for state units and selectors for derived/computed state. Components subscribe to atoms via hooks, ensuring updates trigger efficient re-renders only where needed. 403. How do you manage state with useReducer instead of useState?

You use useReducer instead of useState when state logic is complex or involves multiple sub-values. useReducer lets you manage state with a reducer function and dispatch actions, providing better organization and predictable state transitions, especially useful for forms or multi-step interactions.

404. How do you manage state with useReducer?

Category: Hooks
useReducer manages state by accepting a reducer function and initial state. You dispatch actions to this reducer, which returns new state based on the action type. This pattern centralizes state updates, improves clarity for complex state, and enables easier debugging compared to multiple useState calls.

405. How do you manage state with Zustand?

407. How do you memoize callback functions in React?

Category: Hooks
Memoizing callback functions in React is achieved using the useCallback hook, which returns a memoized version of the callback that only changes if its dependencies change. This optimization prevents unnecessary re-creations of functions, helping reduce re-renders in child components relying on stable callbacks.

408. How do you memoize components and functions?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Memoizing components involves wrapping them with React.memo to avoid re-renders when props don’t change, while memoizing functions uses hooks like useCallback or useMemo to cache function or computed value instances. Together, they optimize rendering performance by minimizing unnecessary updates.

409. How do you memoize components with React.memo?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo wraps a functional component to memoize its rendered output, preventing re-renders if props are shallowly equal. It is useful for pure components where rendering depends solely on props, boosting performance by skipping unnecessary rendering cycles.

410. How do you memoize components?

Category: Performance Optimization
Components are memoized by wrapping them in React.memo to cache their output unless props change. Additionally, hooks like useMemo and useCallback can memoize values and functions used inside components to avoid expensive recalculations and re-renders.

411. How do you memoize expensive calculations in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Expensive calculations are memoized using the useMemo hook, which caches the result of a computation until its dependencies change. This prevents costly recalculations on every render and improves component performance when dealing with heavy computations or derived data.

412. How do you memoize functions with useCallback?

Category: Hooks
useCallback memoizes functions by returning a stable function reference that only changes if specified dependencies change. This prevents unnecessary recreations of functions, which is especially useful when passing callbacks to optimized child components that rely on reference equality.

413. How do you memoize React components?

Category: Performance Optimization
React components are memoized by wrapping them in React.memo, which caches the rendered output and skips re-rendering when props remain shallowly equal. This optimizes rendering by reducing unnecessary updates in components that render the same output for identical props.

414. How do you memoize values with useMemo?

Category: Hooks
useMemo memoizes computed values by recalculating them only when dependencies change. This is ideal for avoiding expensive recalculations or preventing new object/array references that could trigger unnecessary re-renders or effect executions.

415. How do you mimic componentDidMount behavior with hooks?

Category: Hooks
To mimic componentDidMount with hooks, use useEffect with an empty dependency array. This ensures the effect runs only once after the initial render, perfect for initializing data or subscriptions similar to class component mounting behavior.

416. How do you mimic componentDidUpdate using hooks?

Category: Hooks
You mimic componentDidUpdate by using useEffect with specific dependencies. The effect runs after renders when any dependency changes, allowing you to react to updates similarly to class components’ update lifecycle method.

417. How do you mock API calls in React tests?

This ensures tests are deterministic, fast, and independent of real backend services.

418. How do you mock API calls in tests?

This allows tests to simulate various API responses, handle error cases, and isolate components from external dependencies for consistent test outcomes.

419. How do you mock API calls in unit tests?

This isolation helps verify component logic without relying on real network activity, using Jest mocks or similar testing utilities.

420. How do you mock context in React tests?

427. How do you optimize list rendering performance in React?

428. How do you optimize list rendering with virtualization?

429. How do you optimize list rendering?

433. How do you optimize performance with React.memo?

Using React.memo in React apps helps optimize performance by memoizing components and preventing unnecessary re-renders when props don’t change, thus reducing CPU load and improving rendering efficiency.

437. How do you optimize React app performance with useMemo and useCallback?

Category: Performance Optimization
Combining useMemo and useCallback optimizes React app performance by memoizing expensive calculations (useMemo) and stable callback functions (useCallback), reducing unnecessary recalculations and re-renders, especially when passing callbacks to memoized child components.

438. How do you optimize React app performance with virtualization?

Category: Performance Optimization
Virtualization optimizes React app performance by rendering only visible items in a long list or grid, rather than the entire dataset. Libraries like react-window and react-virtualized implement this technique, significantly improving rendering speed and reducing memory usage.

439. How do you optimize React app performance?

To optimize React app performance, use techniques like memoization (React.memo, useMemo, useCallback), code splitting, lazy loading, virtualization for large lists, minimizing state updates, and reducing unnecessary re-renders by structuring components efficiently.

Optimizing React apps for mobile focuses on reducing bundle size, minimizing memory consumption, and improving rendering speed. Techniques include using React Native’s FlatList for efficient list rendering, memoizing components with React.memo, debouncing inputs, lazy loading components, and optimizing images and assets to suit mobile performance constraints.

443. How do you optimize React component performance with useMemo?

Category: Performance Optimization
useMemo optimizes React component performance by memoizing the results of expensive computations so they only re-run when dependencies change. This reduces unnecessary recalculations on every render, improving efficiency in components with complex calculations or data transformations.

444. How do you optimize React component rendering with React.memo?

Category: Performance Optimization
Using React.memo wraps a component to memoize its output, preventing re-renders unless its props change. This is particularly helpful for functional components that receive the same props repeatedly, reducing CPU usage and speeding up rendering.

445. How do you optimize React component rendering?

446. How do you optimize React context to avoid unnecessary renders?

Category: State Management
To optimize React context and avoid unnecessary renders, structure context values to minimize changes, split context into smaller focused providers, memoize context values with useMemo, and consume context selectively to prevent broad re-renders when unrelated parts of the context update.

447. How do you optimize React performance using React.memo and useCallback?

Category: Performance Optimization
Combining React.memo and useCallback optimizes React performance by memoizing components and stabilizing callback functions passed as props. This avoids unnecessary re-renders and function re-creations, especially beneficial in components with frequent updates or deep component trees.

448. How do you optimize React performance using virtualization?

Virtualization optimizes React performance by rendering only a subset of items visible in a scrollable viewport rather than the entire list. Using libraries like react-window or react-virtualized, it reduces DOM nodes, improves scroll performance, and lowers memory usage.

449. How do you optimize React performance with React.memo and useCallback?

Using React.memo to memoize components and useCallback to memoize functions prevents unnecessary re-renders and function recreations. This is key to optimizing performance, especially when passing callbacks to child components that rely on referential equality.

450. How do you optimize React performance with virtualization?

Additionally, code-splitting and virtualization can help keep rendering efficient in large applications.

456. How do you optimize rendering performance using React.memo?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo optimizes rendering by memoizing a component’s rendered output, allowing React to skip re-rendering the component when its props have not changed. This prevents unnecessary updates and improves performance, especially for functional components that receive stable props.

457. How do you optimize rendering performance with useMemo and useCallback?

Category: Performance Optimization
Using useMemo and useCallback together optimizes rendering by memoizing both expensive calculations and function references. This reduces unnecessary recalculations and prevents child components from re-rendering due to new function props, resulting in smoother and more efficient updates.

458. How do you optimize rendering using useMemo and useCallback?

459. How do you optimize rendering with React.memo?

462. How do you pass parameters to a custom hook?

Category: Hooks
You pass parameters to a custom hook just like a regular function by defining arguments in the hook signature. These parameters can then be used internally within the hook to customize behavior, such as fetching data based on an ID or configuring options for reusable logic.

463. How do you pass parameters to routes in React Router?

Category: Routing
Parameters are passed to routes in React Router via URL segments defined as route params (e.g., /users/:id) and accessed in components using hooks like useParams(). You can also pass state or query parameters for additional data transfer.

464. How do you pass props to a child component?

Category: Props
Props are passed to a child component by including them as attributes in the JSX tag of the child. For example, <ChildComponent someProp={value} /> passes someProp to the child, which can then access it via its props object.

465. How do you pass props to a component?

Category: Basics
Props are passed to a component by adding them as attributes in JSX when rendering the component. These props provide data or callbacks to the component, enabling dynamic rendering and interaction.

466. How do you perform a mutation using Apollo Client?

Category: Data Fetching
To perform a mutation using Apollo Client, you use the useMutation hook with a GraphQL mutation query. Invoking the returned mutation function sends the mutation request, and you can handle loading, error, and response states accordingly.

467. How do you perform code splitting using React.lazy?

Category: Code Splitting
React.lazy allows dynamic import of components, enabling code splitting by loading components only when they are rendered. Combined with Suspense for fallback UI, it reduces initial bundle size and improves load time.

468. How do you perform side effects with useEffect?

Category: Hooks
Side effects such as data fetching, subscriptions, or manual DOM manipulation are handled inside the useEffect hook. By specifying dependencies, you control when the effect runs, including on mount, update, or unmount.

469. How do you persist data in React Native?

Category: React Native
In React Native, data persistence is commonly achieved using AsyncStorage for simple key-value storage, or more advanced solutions like SQLite, Realm, or SecureStore. These allow storing user preferences, authentication tokens, or offline data securely and reliably.

470. How do you persist form data between sessions?

Category: Forms
To persist form data between sessions, you can save the form state to persistent storage such as localStorage or sessionStorage in the browser. On form input changes, update the stored data, and on component mount, retrieve and prefill the form with this data. Libraries like Formik or React Hook Form can be extended to integrate this persistence seamlessly.

471. How do you persist Redux state across sessions?

Category: State Management
Persisting Redux state across sessions is typically done using middleware like redux-persist, which automatically saves the Redux store to persistent storage such as localStorage and rehydrates the store on app initialization. This ensures that the application state is maintained even after page reloads or browser restarts.

472. How do you preload React components?

Category: Performance
Preloading React components can be achieved by using dynamic imports with React.lazy combined with techniques like import() prefetching or preloading via link tags in HTML. This allows loading components ahead of time, reducing wait times when the component is needed and improving perceived performance.

473. How do you prevent component re-renders in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Preventing unnecessary component re-renders can be done using React.memo to memoize functional components, using useMemo and useCallback to memoize values and functions, minimizing state changes, avoiding inline function and object creations in render, and properly managing context and props to avoid triggering updates.

474. How do you prevent form submission on Enter key?

Category: Forms
To prevent form submission on the Enter key, you can handle the onKeyDown or onSubmit event on the form or input fields and call event.preventDefault() when the Enter key is detected. This stops the default submission behavior, allowing more controlled handling of form inputs.

475. How do you prevent memory leaks in React components?

Category: Performance Optimization
Prevent memory leaks by cleaning up side effects in useEffect cleanup functions, such as aborting async requests, removing event listeners, cancelling subscriptions, and clearing timers. Proper cleanup ensures components do not hold onto resources after unmounting.

476. How do you prevent prop drilling in large React apps?

Category: State Management

This simplifies passing data to deeply nested components without intermediate props.

477. How do you prevent prop drilling in React applications?

Category: State Management
In React applications, prop drilling is prevented by leveraging React Context to share state across components, or by adopting external state management solutions that allow direct access to state without passing props down multiple levels.

478. How do you prevent prop drilling using Context API?

Category: State Management
Using the Context API, you create a context provider at a high level and consume the context value in any descendant component without passing props manually through every intermediate component. This avoids prop drilling by centralizing shared data access.

479. How do you prevent prop drilling using React context?

React Context prevents prop drilling by allowing you to pass data via a context provider and consume it with hooks like useContext anywhere in the component tree, bypassing the need to pass props through every nested component.

483. How do you prevent unnecessary renders in React?

484. How do you prevent unnecessary renders using React.memo?

485. How do you prevent unnecessary renders with hooks?

486. How do you prevent unnecessary renders?

488. How do you prevent XSS attacks in React?

React mitigates XSS attacks by default through automatic escaping of values in JSX. Avoid using dangerouslySetInnerHTML unless absolutely necessary, and always sanitize any external HTML content before injecting it. Validate and encode user input on both client and server.

490. How do you programmatically navigate using React Router?

491. How do you protect routes in React based on user roles?

Category: Security
Protect routes by creating a wrapper component that checks the user’s role from context or state. If the role matches the allowed access, render the route; otherwise, redirect or render a "403 Forbidden" page. Combine this with React Router and role-based logic to secure access.

492. How do you protect routes using authentication in React Router?

Category: Routing
Use a protected route component that checks authentication status via context or a global state. If the user is authenticated, render the component; otherwise, redirect to the login page. This approach ensures that only logged-in users can access specific routes.

493. How do you provide and consume a context?

496. How do you refetch data when a dependency changes?

Use the useEffect hook with the dependency in its dependency array. When the dependency changes, the effect re-runs and triggers a new fetch. In libraries like React Query, specify dependencies in query keys to handle refetching automatically.

497. How do you render a list of elements in React?

498. How do you reset a form programmatically?

500. How do you reset Redux state on logout?

513. How do you stop event propagation in React?

517. How do you style React components using CSS modules?

518. How do you style React components using CSS-in-JS libraries?

520. How do you style React components with CSS modules?

525. How do you test a custom hook?

526. How do you test asynchronous actions in React components?

527. How do you test asynchronous behavior in React components?

528. How do you test asynchronous code in React components?

Wrap assertions inside waitFor or use async queries like findByText to test asynchronous code. These helpers retry assertions until they pass or time out, accommodating asynchronous state changes or rendering.

529. How do you test asynchronous logic in React components?

530. How do you test asynchronous React components?

533. How do you test for accessibility in React?

549. How do you type context values in TypeScript?

Category: TypeScript
Define a TypeScript interface or type for your context value, then use it when creating and consuming the context. Example: const MyContext = React.createContext<MyType | undefined>(undefined) and use useContext<MyType>().

550. How do you type custom hooks in TypeScript?

Category: TypeScript
Declare the return type and any parameter types explicitly. For example, a hook returning state and a setter might be typed as: const useMyHook = (): [number, () => void] => {...}. Use generic types if the hook works with flexible data.

551. How do you type forwardRef components in TypeScript?

Category: TypeScript
Use the forwardRef generic type. Example: const MyComponent = React.forwardRef<HTMLDivElement, MyProps>((props, ref) => ...). This ensures both the props and ref are correctly typed, supporting IntelliSense and type safety.

552. How do you type props for class components in TypeScript?

Category: TypeScript
Extend React.Component with your props type: class MyComponent extends React.Component<MyProps>. Define MyProps as an interface describing the expected prop types for clarity and IntelliSense support.

553. How do you type props in a functional component using TypeScript?

Category: TypeScript
Define a props interface and use it in the function declaration: interface Props { name: string } const MyComponent: React.FC<Props> = ({ name }) => {...}. Alternatively, type props inline without using React.FC.

554. How do you type React.memo components with props?

Category: TypeScript
Define a props interface and apply it when wrapping with React.memo. Example: const MyComponent = React.memo<MyProps>((props) => ...). This ensures the component’s props are correctly inferred and validated by TypeScript.

555. How do you update context values?

Category: State Management
To update context values, provide both the state and a setter function in the context provider. Components consuming the context can call the setter to update values. This is typically done using useState or useReducer inside the provider.

556. How do you update state based on previous state in useState?

Category: Hooks
Use the functional form of setState: setCount(prev => prev + 1). This ensures that updates are based on the most recent state value, avoiding issues with stale state in asynchronous updates.

557. How do you update state immutably in Redux reducers?

Category: State Management
Always return a new object using spread syntax or utility functions like immer. For example, to update a property: return { ...state, value: newValue }. Never mutate the existing state directly inside reducers.

558. How do you update the state in a component?

Category: Basics
Use the setter function from useState to update state in functional components. In class components, use this.setState(). State updates trigger a re-render with the new value.

559. How do you use a ternary operator in JSX?

Category: JSX
Use the ternary operator inside curly braces: {isLoggedIn ? <Logout /> : <Login />}. This allows conditional rendering of elements or components based on boolean logic.

560. How do you use Axios to fetch data in React?

Category: Data Fetching
Import Axios and call it inside useEffect to perform a fetch on mount: axios.get(url).then(response => setData(response.data)). Handle errors using catch and optionally use async/await for cleaner syntax.

561. How do you use context API to manage application state?

Category: State Management
Create a context with createContext() and wrap your app with its provider. Store state and updater functions in the context provider and access them in child components using useContext() for shared application state.

562. How do you use createAsyncThunk in Redux Toolkit?

Category: State Management
Use createAsyncThunk to define an asynchronous action. It returns a thunk that dispatches pending, fulfilled, and rejected actions. Handle each state in your slice using extraReducers to manage loading, success, and error states.

563. How do you use Expo for fast development?

Category: React Native
Install the Expo CLI and create a project using npx create-expo-app. Use the Expo Go app to preview instantly on a mobile device. Expo provides a rich set of APIs and simplifies building and deploying React Native apps.

564. How do you use Formik in React?

Category: Forms
Import useFormik or use the <Formik> component to manage form state, validation, and submission. Define initial values and a submit handler, and bind form fields using formik.handleChange, formik.values, and formik.handleSubmit.

565. How do you use Formik with Yup for validation?

Category: Forms
Define a validation schema using Yup and pass it to Formik via the validationSchema prop. Yup provides declarative schema-based validation, and Formik integrates with it to automatically validate fields and display errors.

566. How do you use fragments to avoid extra nodes?

Category: Components
Use <></> or <React.Fragment></React.Fragment> to group multiple children without adding extra DOM elements. This helps keep the DOM clean and avoids unnecessary wrapper elements.

567. How do you use GraphQL with React?

Category: Data Fetching
Use a library like Apollo Client. Wrap your app with ApolloProvider, define queries using gql, and use hooks like useQuery or useMutation to interact with your GraphQL API from components.

568. How do you use higher-order components (HOCs)?

Category: Design Patterns
A higher-order component is a function that takes a component and returns a new component with additional behavior or props. Example: const Enhanced = withLogger(MyComponent). Use HOCs to reuse logic across multiple components.
