569. How do you use hooks in class

Category: Hooks
You cannot use hooks inside class components. Hooks are exclusive to functional components. For class components, use lifecycle methods and state instead of useState, useEffect, and other hooks.

570. How do you use hooks in React?

Category: Hooks
Import hooks from React like useState, useEffect, and useContext, then call them at the top level of functional components. Hooks let you manage state, side effects, context, refs, and more without using classes.

571. How do you use inline styles in React?

Category: Styling
Use a JavaScript object with camelCased properties. For example: <div style={{ backgroundColor: 'blue', padding: '10px' }}>. This allows for dynamic and scoped styling directly in your JSX.

572. How do you use multiple contexts in a component?

Category: Context API
Call useContext separately for each context you want to consume: const auth = useContext(AuthContext), const theme = useContext(ThemeContext). This lets a single component access multiple providers' values.

573. How do you use portals to render children outside the DOM hierarchy?

Category: Advanced Features
Use ReactDOM.createPortal(child, container) to render a child component into a different DOM node than its parent. This is useful for modals, tooltips, or overlays that need to break out of the component tree structure.

574. How do you use React Context API for global state management?

Category: State Management
Create a context and wrap your app in a provider that holds state using useState or useReducer. Components can then access or update the shared state using useContext, enabling global state sharing without prop drilling.

575. How do you use React Profiler for performance tuning?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Profiler helps with performance tuning by visualizing component render timings and identifying unnecessary renders. Use the Profiler tab in React DevTools to explore commit durations, re-render counts, and interactions, enabling you to optimize component structures and hook usage.

576. How do you use React Profiler to analyze component rendering?

Category: Debugging
React Profiler lets you analyze how often and how long components render. In React DevTools, start profiling and interact with the app. The profiler visualizes renders, helping you identify components that re-render too often or take too long, which guides optimization efforts.

577. How do you use React Profiler to identify performance issues?

Category: Debugging
To identify performance issues, use the Profiler tab in React DevTools. It highlights slow renders and high-frequency re-renders. Look for components with long render durations or those updating frequently without prop/state changes. Optimize with React.memo, useMemo, or useCallback.

578. How do you use React Profiler to improve performance?

Category: Debugging
Use the React Profiler to find performance bottlenecks by measuring render time and frequency. Based on the insights, optimize performance by memoizing components, reducing unnecessary state changes, or restructuring component trees to minimize re-renders.

579. How do you use React Profiler to measure performance?

Category: Debugging
React Profiler records metrics like render duration and update frequency. Enable the Profiler in React DevTools, interact with the app, and view render graphs and timing data. This helps you track performance trends and target slow-rendering components for optimization.

580. How do you use React Query to cache server responses?

Category: State Management
React Query caches server responses automatically. Use the useQuery hook to fetch data, and it stores results in a client-side cache. You can control cache duration, refresh intervals, and use background refetching to keep data up to date with minimal re-fetching.

581. How do you use React Router for navigation?

Category: Routing
React Router enables navigation with components like <Routes> and <Route>. Use Link or useNavigate for navigating programmatically. It handles client-side routing efficiently and updates the UI based on the current path.

582. How do you use React.lazy for code splitting?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Use React.lazy to dynamically import components, which enables code splitting. Wrap the lazy-loaded component with <Suspense> to provide a fallback UI while the component loads. This reduces the initial bundle size and speeds up load times.

583. How do you use React.memo to improve performance?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo wraps functional components to prevent re-renders when props haven’t changed. It performs a shallow comparison of props and only re-renders if a prop value changes, helping to optimize performance for pure components.

584. How do you use React.memo to optimize component rendering?

Category: Performance Optimization
Wrap components in React.memo to memoize them. If the props are the same between renders, the component is skipped. This is especially useful for presentational or list item components where renders are expensive or unnecessary.

585. How do you use React.memo to prevent unnecessary re-renders?

Category: Performance Optimization
Use React.memo to wrap a functional component, so it only re-renders when its props change. This prevents unnecessary updates, especially in components with expensive render logic or stable props across renders.

586. How do you use React.memo with TypeScript?

Category: TypeScript
In TypeScript, define prop types using an interface or type alias, then use React.memo on the component: const MyComponent = React.memo((props: MyProps) => ...). Type safety is preserved and prop types are correctly inferred by TypeScript.

587. How do you use React’s Profiler to detect performance bottlenecks?

Category: Debugging
React’s Profiler highlights which components take longer to render and how often they update. By recording interactions and analyzing timings in React DevTools, you can detect inefficient renders and refactor them using memoization or state management optimizations.

588. How do you use React’s useTransition hook?

Category: Hooks
The useTransition hook allows you to mark state updates as non-urgent, improving UI responsiveness during concurrent rendering. Use it by calling const [isPending, startTransition] = useTransition(). Wrap slow updates in startTransition(() => ...) so the UI can remain interactive while deferring less critical updates.

589. How do you use react-intl for localization?

Category: Internationalization
To localize your app with react-intl, wrap your app in <IntlProvider> with a locale and messages. Use components like <FormattedMessage> or the useIntl hook to render localized text. This library supports pluralization, formatting, and translations from JSON files.

590. How do you use refs in React?

Category: Refs
Refs are created using useRef() and allow you to access DOM elements or persist values across renders without causing re-renders. Attach a ref to an element using the ref attribute and access the element via ref.current.

591. How do you use route parameters in React Router?

Category: Routing
In React Router, define parameters in the route path like /user/:id. In the component, use useParams() to access them: const { id } = useParams(). This is useful for dynamic routing based on URL segments.

592. How do you use ScrollView and FlatList in React Native?

Category: React Native
Use ScrollView for rendering a vertical scrollable area with static content. Use FlatList for performance-optimized rendering of long lists, as it supports lazy loading and efficient re-rendering. Provide data and renderItem props to FlatList to define its content.

593. How do you use React.memo to prevent unnecessary re-renders?

Category: Performance Optimization
Use React.memo to memoize functional components. It prevents re-renders if props haven't changed by doing a shallow comparison. Wrap the component like export default React.memo(MyComponent) to optimize rendering performance.

594. How do you use React.memo with TypeScript?

Category: TypeScript
When using React.memo with TypeScript, define the prop types using an interface and pass the component to React.memo. Example: const MyComponent: React.FC<MyProps> = React.memo((props) => ...). Type safety is maintained while improving render performance.

595. How do you use React’s Profiler to detect performance bottlenecks?

Category: Debugging
Use the Profiler component from React to wrap parts of your component tree. It accepts an onRender callback that logs render timings and reasons. This helps identify which components are rendering frequently or taking too long.

596. How do you use StyleSheet in React Native?

Category: React Native
In React Native, use StyleSheet.create() to define styles in a structured and optimized way. Example: const styles = StyleSheet.create({ container: { flex: 1 } }). Apply the style using the style prop.

597. How do you use the useState hook in a functional component?

Category: Hooks
The useState hook is used to add local state to a functional component. Call const [state, setState] = useState(initialValue). Update the state using setState. This causes a re-render of the component with the new value.

598. How do you use the Context API with TypeScript?

Category: TypeScript
Define the context shape using a TypeScript interface and use React.createContext<Type>(). Provide the value using Context.Provider and consume it with useContext. This ensures type safety throughout the app.

599. How do you use the Link component for navigation?

Category: Routing
The Link component from React Router is used for client-side navigation. Provide the target path via the to prop: <Link to="/about">About</Link>. This enables navigation without full page reloads.

600. How do you use the React Profiler API for performance monitoring?

Category: Debugging
The React Profiler API allows you to track component rendering performance. Use the Profiler component with an onRender function to get timing and render details. Analyze this data to spot bottlenecks and improve performance.

601. How do you use the unstable_useTransition hook?

Category: Advanced Concepts
The unstable_useTransition hook is an experimental version of useTransition that allows you to mark UI updates as non-urgent. Use it to improve perceived performance by deferring state updates. It's useful for transitions like tab switches or content filters.

602. How do you use the useContext hook?

Category: Hooks
The useContext hook allows you to consume a context value inside a functional component. Pass your context object to useContext, like const value = useContext(MyContext), to access shared data across the component tree.

603. How do you use the useDebugValue hook?

Category: Hooks
useDebugValue is used to display custom labels for custom hooks in React DevTools. Use it within a custom hook: useDebugValue(someValue). It helps with debugging by making internal hook states more transparent.

604. How do you use the useDeferredValue hook in React?

Category: Hooks
useDeferredValue lets you defer re-rendering of a non-urgent part of the UI. It returns a deferred version of a value that updates less urgently. This is helpful when rendering large lists or performing heavy computations after state changes.

605. How do you use the useEffect hook?

Category: Hooks
useEffect lets you run side effects like data fetching, subscriptions, or DOM manipulation after render. Syntax: useEffect(() => { /* effect */ }, [dependencies]). The effect runs when dependencies change, and cleanup can be returned from the function.

606. How do you use the useImperativeHandle hook?

Category: Refs
useImperativeHandle customizes the instance value exposed when using ref with forwardRef. It's used to expose a controlled API from a child to a parent. Use it to define what methods or properties the parent can access via the ref.

607. How do you use the useLayoutEffect hook and when should you use it?

Category: Hooks
useLayoutEffect works like useEffect but runs synchronously after all DOM mutations and before the browser paints. It's ideal for reading layout or synchronously triggering reflows, such as measuring DOM nodes or updating scroll positions.

608. How do you use the useLayoutEffect hook effectively?

Category: Hooks
Use useLayoutEffect for operations that require DOM measurement before paint, like animations or syncing scroll positions. Avoid heavy computations to prevent UI blocking. Always clean up effects properly to avoid memory leaks.

609. How do you use the useLayoutEffect hook?

Category: Hooks
The syntax is identical to useEffect: useLayoutEffect(() => { /* logic */ }, [dependencies]). It executes the function after all DOM changes but before the browser paints. Prefer useEffect unless precise DOM measurements are needed.

610. How do you use the useLocation hook?

Category: Routing
The useLocation hook from React Router gives access to the current location object, including pathname, search, and hash. It's useful for reading URL information and responding to route changes inside a component.

611. How do you use the useReducer hook for complex state management?

Category: Hooks
useReducer is ideal for complex state logic involving multiple sub-values or when the next state depends on the previous one. It takes a reducer function and an initial state and returns the current state with a dispatch function. It's a good alternative to useState for managing structured or interrelated state.

612. How do you use the useReducer hook for complex state?

Category: Hooks
To manage complex state with useReducer, define a reducer that handles state transitions and dispatch actions to update the state. This approach centralizes updates, improves readability, and simplifies debugging in components with complex or conditional state logic.

613. How do you use the useReducer hook in React?

Category: Hooks
Use useReducer by providing it a reducer function and an initial state. The hook returns a state value and a dispatch function. Dispatching actions allows state transitions based on action types, making it suitable for scenarios like form management or toggling multiple UI states.

614. How do you use the useReducer hook?

Category: Hooks
The useReducer syntax is: const [state, dispatch] = useReducer(reducer, initialState). It's beneficial for managing state that changes in complex ways. The reducer function handles different action types and returns the new state based on the dispatched action.

615. How do you use the useRef hook in React?

Category: Refs
useRef creates a mutable ref object that persists for the component's lifetime. Use it to access DOM elements directly or to hold a mutable value that doesn’t trigger a re-render. It’s commonly used with ref={ref} on elements or to store values across renders.

616. How do you use the useReducer hook for complex state management?

Category: Hooks
The useReducer hook is useful when dealing with complex state logic that involves multiple values or intricate updates. Define a reducer function that describes how the state should change based on an action. Then use useReducer(reducer, initialState) to get the current state and a dispatch function. Dispatch actions to update the state based on your logic.

617. How do you use the useReducer hook for complex state?

Category: Hooks
For complex state, useReducer lets you encapsulate logic in a reducer function, avoiding deeply nested useState calls. You define actions to describe state changes and dispatch them as needed. This structure is especially helpful for forms, toggles, or multi-step flows.

618. How do you use the useReducer hook in React?

Category: Hooks
In React, the useReducer hook is used by passing in a reducer function and an initial state. It returns a state and dispatch pair. Actions dispatched to the reducer allow you to manage transitions clearly and consistently, making it great for complex or interdependent state.

619. How do you use the useReducer hook?

Category: Hooks
Use useReducer by first creating a reducer function: a pure function that takes current state and an action, and returns the new state. Then call const [state, dispatch] = useReducer(reducer, initialState). Trigger state updates using dispatch({ type: 'ACTION_TYPE', payload }).

620. How do you use the useRef hook in React?

Category: Refs
The useRef hook returns a mutable object with a current property. It is commonly used to access DOM elements directly or to store persistent values between renders without causing re-renders. Use it by calling const ref = useRef() and then attaching it to a DOM element via the ref attribute.

621. How do you use the useState hook to manage state in functional components?

Category: Hooks
To manage state in functional components, use the useState hook by calling const [state, setState] = useState(initialValue). This returns a state variable and a function to update it. When you call setState, React re-renders the component with the new state value.

622. How do you use the useSyncExternalStore hook?

Category: Hooks
The useSyncExternalStore hook is used to subscribe to external stores in a way that works with concurrent rendering. Provide a subscribe function, a getSnapshot function for client rendering, and optionally a getServerSnapshot for SSR. React uses these to keep the UI in sync with the external store.

623. How do you use TypeScript with Redux Toolkit?

Category: TypeScript
With Redux Toolkit and TypeScript, type your slices using PayloadAction for actions and define RootState and AppDispatch in your store setup. Use useSelector and useDispatch with appropriate TypeScript types by creating custom hooks like useAppSelector and useAppDispatch.

624. How do you use useCallback to memoize functions?

Category: Performance
Use useCallback to memoize functions and prevent unnecessary re-creations on every render. It returns a memoized version of the function that only changes if its dependencies change: const memoizedFn = useCallback(() => {...}, [deps]). This improves performance especially when passing callbacks to child components.

625. How do you use useEffect to manage side effects?

Category: Hooks
The useEffect hook lets you handle side effects like data fetching, subscriptions, or manual DOM changes in functional components. Pass it a function that executes after render, and optionally provide a dependency array to control when the effect runs: useEffect(() => { ... }, [deps]).

626. How do you use useEffect to perform side effects?

Category: Hooks
To perform side effects such as fetching data, setting up subscriptions, or manually updating the DOM, use the useEffect hook. You pass it a function that runs after the component renders. Include a dependency array to specify when the effect should re-run. For cleanup, return a function from inside useEffect.

627. How do you use useForm from React Hook Form?

Category: Forms
To use useForm from React Hook Form, call it at the top of your component: const { register, handleSubmit, formState } = useForm(). Use register to bind inputs, handleSubmit to handle form submission, and access validation state through formState. This provides efficient, declarative form handling.

628. How do you use useImperativeHandle with forwardRef?

Category: Hooks
Use useImperativeHandle with forwardRef to expose specific methods or values from a child component to a parent. Wrap the component with forwardRef, then call useImperativeHandle(ref, () => ({ method })) inside. This pattern is useful for exposing imperative APIs like focus or reset.

629. How do you use useNavigate in React Router?

Category: Routing
In React Router v6, useNavigate is a hook that lets you navigate programmatically. Call const navigate = useNavigate(), then use navigate('/path') to redirect the user. You can also pass state or use navigate(-1) to go back in history.

630. How do you use useReducer for complex state management?

Category: Hooks
The useReducer hook is ideal for managing complex state logic. Define a reducer function that handles actions, and initialize the hook like this: const [state, dispatch] = useReducer(reducer, initialState). Dispatch actions to update state based on predefined logic in the reducer.

631. How do you use useReducer for state management?

Category: Hooks
Use useReducer to manage component state when the state logic is complex or involves multiple sub-values. Define a reducer function that receives the current state and an action, then returns the new state. Initialize it with const [state, dispatch] = useReducer(reducer, initialState). Dispatch actions to update state predictably.

632. How do you use useRef to access DOM elements?

Category: Refs
To access DOM elements, call const myRef = useRef(null) and attach it to a JSX element using ref={myRef}. After the component mounts, myRef.current will point to the DOM node, allowing you to interact with it directly, such as focusing an input or measuring dimensions.

633. How do you use useRef to persist values?

Category: Hooks
useRef can be used to persist values across renders without triggering a re-render. Initialize it with const countRef = useRef(0). Update its value using countRef.current = newValue. This is useful for storing mutable data like timers or previous state without affecting the UI.

634. How do you use Webpack to optimize React apps?

Category: Advanced Concepts
To optimize React apps using Webpack, enable production mode, use code splitting with dynamic import(), enable tree shaking, minify code with TerserPlugin, and cache assets using content hashes. Use loaders like babel-loader for JS and css-loader for styles, and optimize images with appropriate plugins.

635. How do you validate a form in React?

Category: Forms
Validate forms in React by managing form state with useState or libraries like Formik or React Hook Form. Check field values on input or form submit, and display errors conditionally. You can also use validation schemas like Yup for declarative rules.

636. How do you validate form input in React?

Category: Forms
In React, you validate form input by tracking input values using component state and applying validation rules either on change or on form submission. You can use controlled components and manually implement validation logic, or leverage libraries like Formik or React Hook Form for more complex forms. For example, check if a field is required, matches a pattern, or satisfies a minimum length, then update the UI with error messages accordingly.

637. How do you validate forms in React?

Category: Forms
To validate forms in React, use controlled components to manage form data and apply validation logic during input changes or before submission. Validation can be simple JavaScript functions or complex schema-based validation using libraries like Yup with Formik or built-in validation rules in React Hook Form. Display validation messages dynamically based on validation results to enhance user experience.

638. How do you write a GraphQL query in a React component?

Category: Data Fetching
You can write a GraphQL query in a React component using client libraries like Apollo Client or Relay. With Apollo, define the query using the gql template literal and use the useQuery hook to execute it. The hook returns the data, loading, and error state, which you can use to render your component conditionally based on the query result.

639. How do you write a Higher-Order Component?

Category: Advanced Patterns
A Higher-Order Component (HOC) is a function that takes a component and returns a new component with additional props or behavior. To write one, define a function that accepts a component, wraps it with extra logic (e.g., conditional rendering, data fetching, auth checks), and returns a new enhanced component. HOCs are useful for code reuse across components.

640. How do you write unit tests for components?

Category: Testing
To write unit tests for React components, use testing libraries like Jest with React Testing Library. Render the component using render(), then simulate user interactions with fireEvent or userEvent. Use assertions to check if elements are rendered, props are passed correctly, or event handlers are called as expected. Mock functions and external modules to isolate component logic.

641. How does Concurrent Mode improve UI responsiveness?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Concurrent Mode improves UI responsiveness by allowing React to interrupt and pause rendering work, prioritizing important updates over less critical ones. It enables features like useTransition, which makes updates feel more fluid by keeping the UI responsive during intensive operations. This helps avoid blocking the main thread and ensures better user experience on slower devices.

642. How does Next.js enable server-side rendering?

Category: Advanced
Next.js enables server-side rendering (SSR) by pre-rendering pages on the server before sending HTML to the client. Using functions like getServerSideProps, you can fetch data at request time and render the component with fresh content. This improves performance and SEO compared to client-only rendering strategies.

643. How does Next.js enable SSR?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Next.js implements SSR by executing React components on the server with dynamic data fetched using getServerSideProps. When a request comes in, it renders the HTML on the server and sends it to the client. This results in faster initial load times and better indexing by search engines. SSR is ideal for dynamic content that needs to be up-to-date on each request.

644. How does React batch state updates?

Category: State
React batches state updates automatically inside event handlers, hooks, and lifecycle methods to optimize performance. Instead of re-rendering the component after each setState call, React groups them together and renders only once. In modern versions (starting from React 18), batching is also supported for asynchronous operations like timeouts and promises through automatic batching.

645. How does React compare to other frontend frameworks like Angular or Vue?

Category: Core Concepts
React is a library for building user interfaces, whereas Angular is a full-fledged MVC framework and Vue is a progressive framework. React emphasizes unidirectional data flow and uses JSX for templating. Angular uses TypeScript and two-way data binding, while Vue provides a more structured approach with directives and single-file components. React offers more flexibility but requires integrating with additional libraries for routing and state management.

646. How does React differ from traditional DOM manipulation?

Category: Core Concepts
React abstracts away direct DOM manipulation by using a virtual DOM. Instead of manually updating the DOM with imperative commands (e.g., document.getElementById), you describe what the UI should look like, and React efficiently updates the real DOM using a diffing algorithm. This approach leads to more predictable code, easier maintenance, and better performance in complex UIs.

647. How does React Fiber improve performance?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber is a complete rewrite of React’s core algorithm, designed to enable incremental rendering. It allows React to pause work, reuse previously rendered content, and prioritize updates based on urgency. This fine-grained control over rendering tasks leads to smoother user experiences, especially in concurrent rendering scenarios.

648. How does React handle cleanup in useEffect?

Category: Hooks
React handles cleanup in useEffect by allowing you to return a cleanup function from the effect. This function is executed before the component unmounts or before the effect runs again due to changes in dependencies. It’s commonly used to clean up subscriptions, timers, or event listeners to prevent memory leaks and ensure proper teardown.

649. How does React handle errors in components?

Category: Error Handling
React handles errors in components using Error Boundaries. These are special components that catch JavaScript errors in their child component tree during rendering, in lifecycle methods, and in constructors. You create an error boundary by defining a class component with componentDidCatch and getDerivedStateFromError methods to display fallback UI instead of breaking the app.

650. How does React handle event pooling?

Category: Event Handling
In earlier versions, React used event pooling to improve performance by reusing SyntheticEvent objects. However, this meant that event properties were cleared after the event callback completed. To persist the event, you had to call event.persist(). As of React 17, event pooling is removed, so SyntheticEvents remain usable after the handler has run.

651. How does React handle events differently than the DOM?

Category: Core Concepts
React uses a SyntheticEvent system that normalizes events across browsers and wraps native events to provide consistent behavior. Unlike the native DOM, React attaches a single event listener to the document and delegates events through this system. This improves performance and compatibility but means that React event handlers don’t bubble the same way as in the raw DOM.

652. How does React handle keys during reconciliation?

Category: Advanced
During reconciliation, React uses keys to identify which items have changed, been added, or removed. A stable and unique key allows React to avoid unnecessary re-renders by reusing existing elements instead of recreating them. Without keys or with unstable ones like array indexes, React may misinterpret changes, leading to rendering bugs or performance issues.

653. How does React handle reconciliation for lists with keys?

Category: Lists & Keys
For lists, React compares elements by their keys during reconciliation. If the key matches an old element, React updates it with new props. If not, React unmounts the old element and mounts a new one. This makes updates predictable and efficient. Using meaningful keys (like IDs) ensures minimal DOM manipulation and preserves component state across renders.

654. How does React handle reconciliation?

Category: Core Concepts
React’s reconciliation algorithm compares the new virtual DOM tree with the previous one to determine the minimal set of changes needed to update the actual DOM. It uses keys to match elements and optimizes updates by reusing components when possible. React assumes component structure is stable and prioritizes performance through heuristic rules.

655. How does React handle state management compared to Redux?

Category: State Management
React handles state locally within components using the useState and useReducer hooks. This is ideal for isolated state logic. Redux, on the other hand, offers a centralized store for global state management, better suited for large applications with complex state sharing across components. While React provides context for limited global sharing, Redux gives a predictable, structured architecture for state flow.

656. How does React Hook Form differ from Formik?

Category: Forms
React Hook Form is more performant and lightweight compared to Formik. It leverages uncontrolled components and native form behavior, minimizing re-renders. Formik, on the other hand, uses controlled components and internal state management, which can lead to more re-renders. React Hook Form provides better performance for large forms, while Formik offers more out-of-the-box abstractions and a simpler API for beginners.

657. How does React manage component lifecycle with hooks?

Category: Hooks
React manages component lifecycle with hooks by providing specific functions that align with different phases of a component's life. For instance, useEffect replaces lifecycle methods like componentDidMount, componentDidUpdate, and componentWillUnmount. You can control when the effect runs by specifying dependencies, allowing you to synchronize with external systems, manage subscriptions, or perform cleanup tasks.

658. How does React Native differ from React?

Category: React Native
React Native is a framework for building native mobile apps using React, but instead of targeting the browser's DOM, it renders native UI components on iOS and Android. While it shares React's component model and hooks, React Native uses components like View and Text instead of div and span. It also uses a different styling system and bridges JavaScript with native APIs.

659. How does React support localization and internationalization (i18n)?

Category: Internationalization
React supports localization and internationalization through third-party libraries such as react-intl, i18next, and formatjs. These tools help manage translations, formatting for dates and numbers, and language switching. Components or hooks provided by these libraries allow you to define messages in different locales and render them dynamically based on the user’s language settings.

660. How does React update the UI?

Category: Core Concepts
React updates the UI by re-rendering components when their state or props change. It uses a virtual DOM to compute the difference between the previous and current trees. This diff is used to efficiently update only the changed parts of the real DOM, minimizing reflows and repaints for better performance.

661. How does React.lazy help in code splitting?

Category: Code Splitting
React.lazy helps in code splitting by allowing you to dynamically import components only when they are needed. This reduces the initial bundle size and improves load time. Combined with Suspense, it lets you display fallback UI while the lazy component is being loaded. This strategy improves the perceived performance and scalability of your application.

662. How does React.lazy work?

Category: Code Splitting
React.lazy works by returning a Promise that resolves to a module containing a React component. You pass a dynamic import function to React.lazy, and React defers loading the component until it's actually needed in the UI. The component must be rendered within a <Suspense> boundary so that React can show fallback content while loading.

663. How does React’s context API work?

Category: State Management
React’s Context API provides a way to pass data through the component tree without having to pass props manually at every level. You create a context with React.createContext(), wrap your components with a Provider, and consume the value using useContext. It’s ideal for sharing global state like themes, user info, or locale across the app.

664. How does React’s diffing algorithm work?

Category: Core Concepts
React’s diffing algorithm, also known as the reconciliation process, compares the previous and current virtual DOM trees. It identifies differences and calculates the minimum number of changes needed to update the real DOM. It optimizes by assuming that components of the same type will have similar structure and by using keys to match list items efficiently.

665. How does React’s event system differ from native DOM events?

Category: Event Handling
React uses a SyntheticEvent system that wraps native DOM events to provide cross-browser consistency and efficiency. Unlike native events that attach directly to DOM nodes, React delegates all events to a root container using a single event listener. This reduces memory usage and ensures a uniform API regardless of the browser.

666. How does React’s reconciliation algorithm optimize updates?

Category: Core Concepts
React’s reconciliation algorithm optimizes updates by reusing DOM elements where possible. It assumes components of the same type can be updated in place and uses keys to identify changes in lists. It avoids full re-renders by comparing the current and previous virtual DOMs and applying only the minimal set of changes to the real DOM.

667. How does React’s reconciliation algorithm work internally?

Category: React Internals
Internally, React’s reconciliation algorithm walks through the virtual DOM tree using a depth-first approach. It checks if elements can be reused by comparing their type and key. If they differ, it unmounts the old and mounts a new one. The Fiber architecture allows React to pause, interrupt, or prioritize these tasks, enabling more responsive rendering.

668. How does React’s reconciliation algorithm work?

Category: Core Concepts
React’s reconciliation algorithm works by comparing the new and previous virtual DOM trees to identify changes. It updates only what has changed, minimizing DOM operations. For components with the same type, it updates the props; for different types, it re-creates the component. In lists, it uses keys to detect moved or removed items efficiently.

669. How does React's reconciliation algorithm work?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React's reconciliation algorithm is the process React uses to efficiently update the UI by comparing the previous and new virtual DOM trees. It employs heuristics to minimize DOM operations by reusing existing elements when possible. Key strategies include comparing element types, using keys for list elements to track movement or removal, and prioritizing updates through the Fiber architecture. This algorithm enables React to batch and schedule updates, improving responsiveness and performance.

670. How does Redux DevTools help in development?

Category: State Management
Redux DevTools provides an interface for developers to inspect Redux store state changes over time. It allows time-travel debugging, action replay, and state snapshotting, which helps trace bugs, understand application behavior, and debug complex state transitions. The tool also supports middleware integration and can monitor dispatched actions, making state management transparent and easier to debug.

671. How does SSR improve performance and SEO?

Category: SSR / SSG
Server-Side Rendering (SSR) improves performance by sending fully rendered HTML from the server, allowing browsers to display content faster without waiting for JavaScript to load and execute. It enhances SEO since search engines can index content more easily from server-rendered HTML rather than client-rendered JavaScript. SSR also provides better initial page load experiences, especially for slow connections or devices.

672. How does Suspense help with data fetching?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React's Suspense allows components to "wait" for asynchronous data (like fetching from APIs) before rendering. When used with libraries that support Suspense, such as React Query or Relay, it enables declarative loading states by suspending rendering until data is ready, showing fallback UI meanwhile. This simplifies handling loading states and improves user experience by avoiding flickering or partial renders.

673. How does the useEffect hook work in React?

Category: Hooks
The useEffect hook lets you perform side effects in functional components, such as data fetching, subscriptions, or manually changing the DOM. It runs after every completed render by default but can be customized to run only when certain dependencies change by passing them in a dependency array. Cleanup functions can be returned to manage resource disposal when effects are re-run or components unmount.

674. How does the useEffect hook work?

Category: Hooks
The useEffect hook executes a function after a component renders. It helps synchronize side effects like fetching data or interacting with external APIs with the component lifecycle. You can specify when the effect should run by providing a dependency array. If the array is empty, the effect runs once after the initial render. Cleanup logic can be returned for unmounting or effect re-execution.

675. How does the useImperativeHandle hook work?

Category: Refs
The useImperativeHandle hook customizes the instance value that is exposed to parent components when using forwardRef. It allows a child component to expose specific methods or properties to its parent, controlling what operations the parent can perform on the child’s ref. This encapsulates implementation details while allowing imperative manipulation when necessary.

676. How does the virtual DOM improve performance?

Category: Basics
The virtual DOM improves performance by minimizing direct manipulations to the real DOM, which are costly operations. React creates an in-memory representation of the UI, and when state changes occur, it calculates the difference between previous and new virtual DOM trees. Only the necessary changes are applied to the real DOM, reducing reflows, repaints, and improving overall responsiveness.

677. How does tree shaking affect React app size?

Category: Performance
Tree shaking is a build optimization that removes unused code from the final bundle. In React apps, it eliminates unused components, functions, and libraries, reducing app size. This leads to faster load times and better performance, especially important for large-scale applications where dependency bloat can be significant.

678. How does useCallback help with unnecessary re-renders?

Category: Hooks
The useCallback hook memoizes a function, returning a stable reference unless its dependencies change. This prevents functions from being re-created on every render, which is important when passing callbacks to child components wrapped in React.memo. By keeping the same function instance, it helps avoid unnecessary re-renders triggered by changing function props.

679. How does useLayoutEffect differ from useEffect?

Category: Hooks
useLayoutEffect runs synchronously after all DOM mutations but before the browser paints, making it ideal for reading layout or making DOM measurements. In contrast, useEffect runs asynchronously after the browser has painted. Using useLayoutEffect prevents flickering and ensures visual consistency when effects must happen before the UI is visible.

680. How does useMemo help optimize performance?

Category: Performance
The useMemo hook memoizes the result of a computation, recomputing it only when its dependencies change. This optimization avoids expensive recalculations on every render, improving performance especially when rendering complex components or performing heavy calculations.

681. How does Webpack bundle a React application?

Category: Tooling
Webpack bundles a React application by starting from the entry point and recursively resolving dependencies, including JavaScript, CSS, images, and more. It applies loaders to transform code (e.g., Babel for JSX), and plugins for optimization tasks like minification and code splitting. The result is one or more optimized bundles that can be efficiently loaded by browsers.

682. How is useReducer different from useState?

Category: Hooks
While useState manages simple state values directly, useReducer is designed for managing more complex state logic involving multiple sub-values or when the next state depends on the previous one. useReducer uses a reducer function to compute the new state from the current state and an action, making it ideal for complex state transitions and centralized state updates.

683. How is useRef different from state?

Category: Hooks
The useRef hook provides a mutable container whose .current property persists across renders without causing re-renders when changed. It’s typically used to hold references to DOM elements or mutable values. In contrast, state managed by useState triggers component re-renders when updated, making it suitable for data that influences rendering.

684. How to handle errors in functional components?

Category: Error Handling
In functional components, errors can be handled using React’s ErrorBoundary components, which catch rendering errors in their child tree. Although error boundaries must be class components, hooks like useErrorHandler from libraries can help. Additionally, try-catch blocks inside event handlers and conditional rendering for fallback UI improve robustness.

685. How to manage forms with Formik or React Hook Form?

Category: Forms
Formik and React Hook Form provide abstractions to simplify form state management, validation, and submission. Formik uses controlled components and integrates with Yup for schema validation, offering declarative APIs. React Hook Form leverages uncontrolled inputs with refs for better performance, and uses built-in or custom validation. Both help manage form data efficiently and provide validation feedback.

686. How to test React components with Jest and React Testing Library?

Category: Testing
Jest is a testing framework used for running tests and assertions, while React Testing Library focuses on testing React components from a user perspective by querying and interacting with rendered UI. Writing tests involves rendering components, simulating user events, and asserting expected outcomes. This approach ensures components behave correctly under different scenarios.

687. What are accessibility best practices in React?

Category: Accessibility
Accessibility best practices in React include using semantic HTML elements, managing keyboard navigation, providing ARIA roles and attributes where necessary, ensuring color contrast, and using accessible labels. React developers should also test with screen readers and use tools like axe-core to identify accessibility issues early.

688. What are action creators in Redux?

Category: State Management
Action creators are functions that return action objects in Redux. They encapsulate the creation of action objects with a specific type and payload, promoting consistency and reusability. Using action creators simplifies dispatching actions and makes the codebase easier to maintain.

689. What are actions and reducers in Redux?

Category: State Management
Actions are plain objects describing a change in state, containing a type and optionally a payload. Reducers are pure functions that take the current state and an action, and return a new state based on the action type. This pattern centralizes state updates and ensures predictable state transitions.

690. What are ARIA roles and how are they used in React?

Category: Accessibility
ARIA (Accessible Rich Internet Applications) roles define the purpose of UI elements for assistive technologies. In React, developers add ARIA roles as attributes (e.g., role="button") to improve accessibility for screen readers. Proper use of ARIA roles ensures interactive elements are announced and understood correctly.

691. What are best practices for accessibility in React?

Category: Accessibility
Best practices include using semantic HTML, ensuring keyboard navigability, providing meaningful alt text, managing focus appropriately, using ARIA attributes thoughtfully, testing with accessibility tools, and avoiding reliance on color alone for conveying information. React developers should also ensure dynamic content updates are announced properly.

692. What are best practices for React component design?

Category: Best Practices
Best practices include creating small, reusable components, using props and state thoughtfully, avoiding side effects in render, using hooks effectively, maintaining separation of concerns, and documenting component APIs. Also, consider performance optimizations like memoization and avoiding unnecessary re-renders.

693. What are common causes of performance issues in React?

Category: Performance
Common causes include unnecessary re-renders, heavy computations during render, excessive use of inline functions or objects causing memoization failures, large lists without virtualization, blocking the main thread with synchronous code, and improper use of state or props leading to inefficient updates.

694. What are common security risks in React apps?

Category: Security
Common security risks include Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) from unsafe HTML injection, improper handling of authentication tokens, exposure of sensitive data in client code, insecure dependencies, and lack of input validation. React’s default escaping helps mitigate XSS, but developers must still be vigilant about third-party libraries and APIs.

695. What are components in React Native?

Category: React Native
Components in React Native are the building blocks of the UI, similar to React but tailored for mobile platforms. They include core elements like View, Text, and Image that map to native UI components on iOS and Android. React Native components support styling, layout, and gestures, enabling the creation of performant mobile applications.

696. What are compound components and how do you build them?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Compound components are a design pattern in React that allow multiple components to work together by sharing an implicit state. This pattern enables a parent component to manage state and pass it down to nested child components, providing a flexible and expressive API. To build them, you typically create a parent component that holds the shared state and several child components that access this state via React context or by explicitly passing props. This approach allows users of the compound component to compose the UI declaratively, while the internal state and logic remain centralized and encapsulated.

697. What are compound components and how do you create them?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Compound components in React are groups of components designed to work together as a unit, sharing state and behavior without requiring explicit prop drilling. To create them, you typically use React context to provide shared state and callbacks, allowing child components to communicate seamlessly with the parent. This design enhances composability and flexibility, letting developers compose complex UIs with clear and manageable code structure.

698. What are compound components in React and how do you build them?

Category: Advanced Patterns
In React, compound components are a pattern where a set of related components share state and behavior via a common parent, allowing them to function as a cohesive unit. To build them, you define a parent component that manages shared state and provides context, while child components consume this context to render UI or trigger actions. This pattern promotes separation of concerns, reusable code, and cleaner APIs, improving maintainability and usability.

699. What are compound components?

Category: Design Patterns
Compound components are a React design pattern where multiple components are combined to work as a single logical unit. They share state and logic typically through context, allowing for flexible and declarative composition of complex UI elements. This pattern avoids prop drilling and enables cleaner APIs by encapsulating shared behavior in a parent component while exposing composable children.

700. What are controlled and uncontrolled components in forms?

Category: Forms
Controlled components are form inputs whose value is controlled by React state, meaning React is the single source of truth for the input’s data. Uncontrolled components manage their own internal state, and React accesses the value via refs when needed. Controlled components offer greater control and validation but require more boilerplate, while uncontrolled components simplify code but offer less direct control.

701. What are controlled and uncontrolled components in React?

Category: Forms
Controlled components in React receive their current value and update callbacks via props, making React state the source of truth for the form input’s value. Uncontrolled components, however, manage their own state internally, with React accessing values through refs when needed. Controlled components facilitate validation and dynamic behavior; uncontrolled components are simpler but less flexible.

702. What are controlled and uncontrolled components?

Category: Forms
Controlled components are form inputs managed entirely by React state, allowing precise control over user input and validation. Uncontrolled components maintain their own internal state and expose values via refs. The choice depends on the complexity of form interactions and the level of control needed.

703. What are controlled components and how do you use them?

Category: Forms
Controlled components in React are inputs whose values are driven by React state. You use them by setting the input’s value prop to a state variable and updating the state on user input via event handlers. This approach allows for real-time validation, conditionally enabling buttons, and other interactive behaviors linked directly to user input.

704. What are controlled components and uncontrolled components?

Category: Forms
Controlled components rely on React state to hold and update their values, giving full control over the form inputs. Uncontrolled components store their own state internally and expose values through refs, reducing the need for event handlers but limiting control. Both have use cases depending on complexity and developer preference.

705. What are controlled components in React forms?

Category: Forms
Controlled components in React forms are inputs whose displayed values come from React state, and every change triggers a state update. This pattern enables React to fully control the form inputs, facilitating validation, conditional rendering, and integration with other UI elements.

706. What are controlled components in React?

Category: Forms
Controlled components are React components whose form elements’ values are controlled by React state. The input’s value is set via props, and user input updates the state through event handlers, making React the authoritative source for the input’s data.

707. What are controlled components?

Category: Forms
Controlled components are form elements in React that do not maintain their own internal state; instead, their value is controlled by React through state and props. This allows developers to synchronize input values with application state for better control and validation.

708. What are controlled vs uncontrolled components in React forms?

Category: Forms
Controlled components in React forms have their value bound to React state, enabling controlled updates and validation, while uncontrolled components manage their own state internally and expose values via refs. Controlled components are preferred when form data needs to be tightly integrated with app logic, whereas uncontrolled components can simplify code for basic use cases.

709. What are controlled vs uncontrolled components?

Category: Forms
Controlled components in React have their form element values managed by React state, allowing for explicit control over the input’s value, validation, and behavior. Uncontrolled components manage their own internal state and expose values using refs, reducing the need for state management but offering less control. The choice depends on the complexity of the form and requirements for validation or interaction.

710. What are custom hooks and why create them?

Category: Hooks
Custom hooks are reusable functions in React that encapsulate common stateful logic using built-in hooks like useState or useEffect. They help to abstract and share logic across components without repeating code. Creating custom hooks improves code maintainability, readability, and reduces duplication by isolating complex behaviors or side effects into reusable units.

711. What are custom hooks in React?

Category: Hooks
Custom hooks are JavaScript functions in React prefixed with "use" that combine built-in hooks to encapsulate reusable stateful logic. They allow developers to extract component logic into shared functions, improving code reuse and separation of concerns without affecting component hierarchy or behavior.

712. What are default props?

Category: Props
Default props in React are values assigned to component props to provide fallback values when no prop is passed by the parent component. They ensure that components have necessary data to function properly and help prevent undefined or null values during rendering.

713. What are error boundaries and how are they implemented?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries are React components that catch JavaScript errors anywhere in their child component tree, log those errors, and display a fallback UI instead of crashing the entire app. They are implemented by creating class components that define the lifecycle methods static getDerivedStateFromError(error) and componentDidCatch(error, info). This allows graceful handling of rendering errors and improves app stability.

714. What are error boundaries and how do they function?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries are React components that catch errors in their child components during rendering, lifecycle methods, and constructors. When an error is caught, the boundary updates its state to render a fallback UI instead of crashing the entire application, enhancing user experience by isolating errors.

715. What are error boundaries and how do they help?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries help by preventing a JavaScript error in one part of the React component tree from breaking the entire app. They catch errors and display fallback UIs, improving reliability and allowing developers to handle errors gracefully without affecting unrelated parts of the UI.

716. What are error boundaries and how do they work in React?

Category: Error Handling
In React, error boundaries are special class components that catch runtime errors in their descendants. They implement static getDerivedStateFromError() to update state and componentDidCatch() to log errors. When an error occurs, they render fallback UI instead of letting the error propagate and crash the app.

717. What are error boundaries and how do they work?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries are React components that catch JavaScript errors in their child components during rendering, lifecycle, and constructors. They prevent the entire component tree from unmounting by rendering fallback UI and optionally logging the error, thereby improving application resilience.

718. What are error boundaries and how do you use them?

Category: Error Handling
To use error boundaries, you create a class component that implements static getDerivedStateFromError() to update the state when an error is caught and componentDidCatch() to log error details. Then you wrap parts of your app with this boundary component to catch errors locally and display fallback UI instead of a broken interface.

719. What are error boundaries in React and how do they work?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries are React class components that catch errors thrown in any of their child components. They use lifecycle methods to update state and render a fallback UI, preventing the entire app from crashing. They provide a way to gracefully handle unexpected runtime errors during rendering.

720. What are error boundaries in React and how do you create them?

Category: Error Handling
You create error boundaries in React by defining a class component that implements the lifecycle methods static getDerivedStateFromError(error) to update state and componentDidCatch(error, info) to log error details. This component then wraps children components and renders fallback UI if an error occurs.

721. What are error boundaries in React?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries are special React class components designed to catch JavaScript errors anywhere in their child component tree, log them, and display fallback UI to prevent the app from crashing. They improve app robustness by isolating errors.

722. What are error boundaries?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries are React components that catch errors during rendering, lifecycle methods, and constructors in their child components. They allow rendering fallback UI and logging errors instead of unmounting the whole component tree, enhancing error resilience.

723. What are Expo and React Native CLI?

Category: React Native
Expo and React Native CLI are two tools for building React Native apps. Expo is a managed workflow offering easy setup, over-the-air updates, and a suite of built-in APIs without native code. React Native CLI offers more flexibility, enabling direct native code access, custom native modules, and advanced configuration for complex apps.

724. What are fallback components in Suspense?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Fallback components in React Suspense are UI placeholders displayed while waiting for asynchronous operations, such as lazy-loaded components or data fetching, to complete. They provide a graceful loading state by showing temporary content like spinners or messages, enhancing user experience during delays.

725. What are Flexbox layouts in React Native?

Category: React Native
Flexbox layouts in React Native use the CSS Flexbox system to arrange components in rows or columns with flexible sizing and alignment. It allows building responsive, adaptive UIs by controlling direction, wrapping, alignment, and distribution of space within containers, which is essential for diverse screen sizes in mobile apps.

726. What are fragments in React and why are they useful?

Category: Components
Fragments in React let you group multiple elements without adding extra nodes to the DOM. They are useful because they avoid unnecessary wrappers, which can simplify the HTML structure, improve performance, and prevent styling or layout issues caused by extra elements.

727. What are fragments in React?

Category: JSX
React fragments are a syntax feature that allows returning multiple JSX elements grouped together without wrapping them in a parent DOM element. They can be written as <React.Fragment> or using the shorthand <></>.

728. What are Higher-Order Components (HOCs) and how are they used?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Higher-Order Components (HOCs) are functions that take a component and return a new component with enhanced behavior. They are used to reuse component logic such as authentication, data fetching, or theming, allowing separation of concerns without modifying the original component.

729. What are Higher-Order Components (HOCs) and how do you create them?

Category: Advanced Patterns
You create a Higher-Order Component by defining a function that accepts a component as an argument and returns a new component. The returned component wraps the original one, injecting additional props, state, or side effects to extend its behavior without altering its implementation.

730. What are Higher-Order Components (HOCs) in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
In React, Higher-Order Components are patterns for reusing component logic by creating functions that wrap existing components and return enhanced versions, allowing code sharing and abstraction without duplication.

731. What are higher-order components (HOCs)?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Higher-order components are React design patterns where a function takes a component and returns a new component with added functionality. They enable logic reuse by wrapping components without modifying them directly.

732. What are hook dependency arrays and why are they important?

Category: Hooks
Hook dependency arrays specify the variables that React watches to decide when to re-run hooks like useEffect or useCallback. They are important to optimize performance and prevent unwanted side effects by ensuring hooks run only when necessary dependencies change.

733. What are hooks and how do they relate to patterns?

Category: Design Patterns
Hooks are functions that let you use React features such as state and lifecycle inside functional components. They relate to design patterns by enabling reusable logic encapsulation, such as custom hooks for abstracting behavior, leading to cleaner and more maintainable code.

734. What are hooks rules and why are they important?

Category: Hooks
Hooks rules, such as only calling hooks at the top level and only inside React functional components or custom hooks, are important to ensure consistent behavior and prevent bugs. Violating these rules can lead to unpredictable rendering and state inconsistencies.

735. What are hooks rules you must follow?

Category: Hooks Rules
The key rules for hooks are: only call hooks at the top level of your component or custom hook, never inside loops, conditions, or nested functions; and only call hooks from React function components or custom hooks. These ensure hooks work predictably and avoid state or effect mismatches.

736. What are lifecycle methods in class components?

Category: Lifecycle
Lifecycle methods are special functions in React class components that run at specific stages, such as mounting, updating, and unmounting. Examples include componentDidMount, shouldComponentUpdate, and componentWillUnmount. They enable side effects, cleanup, and optimization during component lifecycles.

737. What are portals in React and when would you use them?

Category: Advanced Features
Portals let you render React components outside the main DOM hierarchy, useful for modals, tooltips, or overlays that need to appear above other content without disrupting layout or styling. They provide a way to escape normal parent-child DOM nesting.

738. What are portals in React?

Category: Advanced Features
Portals are a React feature that allows rendering a component’s children into a DOM node outside of the parent component’s DOM hierarchy, typically used to create UI elements like modals or tooltips that visually break out of their containers.

739. What are prop types and how do you use them?

Category: Props
Prop types are a way to validate the types of props passed to React components. Using the prop-types library, you can specify expected types for each prop, helping catch bugs early by warning if props of incorrect types are provided.

740. What are props in React and how do you pass them?

Category: Props
Props are inputs to React components passed as attributes in JSX. You pass them by adding attributes to the component tag, and inside the component, props are accessible as an object, allowing data to flow from parent to child components.

741. What are props in React?

Category: Props
Props are short for “properties” and represent data passed from parent to child components in React. They allow components to be dynamic and reusable by configuring their behavior or display based on external input.

742. What are pure components in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Pure components in React are components that implement a shallow comparison of props and state to decide if they should re-render. This optimization avoids unnecessary renders by reusing the previous output when inputs haven’t changed, leading to improved performance in complex UIs.

743. What are React error boundaries and how do they work?

Category: Error Handling
React error boundaries are special components that catch JavaScript errors anywhere in their child component tree, log those errors, and display a fallback UI instead of the component tree that crashed. They prevent entire app crashes and improve user experience by handling runtime errors gracefully.

744. What are React error boundaries and how do you implement them?

Category: Error Handling
You implement React error boundaries by creating a class component that defines either or both static getDerivedStateFromError(error) and componentDidCatch(error, info) lifecycle methods. These methods update state to show fallback UI and perform side effects like logging errors.

745. What are React error boundaries and how do you use them?

Category: Error Handling
You use error boundaries by wrapping parts of your React component tree with an error boundary component. This ensures that if any child component throws an error during rendering or lifecycle methods, the boundary will catch it and display a fallback UI without breaking the entire app.

746. What are React error boundaries limitations?

Category: Error Handling
React error boundaries do not catch errors inside event handlers, asynchronous code, server-side rendering, or errors thrown in the error boundary itself. Also, they must be class components, as hooks cannot currently be used to create error boundaries.

747. What are React error boundaries?

Category: Error Handling
React error boundaries are components designed to catch runtime errors in their child component trees, preventing UI crashes by rendering fallback content and allowing developers to handle errors gracefully.

748. What are React fibers?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Fiber is the reimplementation of React’s core reconciliation algorithm. It breaks rendering work into small units that can be paused and resumed, enabling smoother user experiences by prioritizing updates, handling interruptions, and improving responsiveness.

749. What are React fragments and how do they help in rendering?

Category: Components
React fragments help in rendering by allowing multiple child elements to be grouped without adding extra nodes to the DOM. This results in cleaner markup, better performance, and avoids unnecessary wrappers that could interfere with styles or layout.

750. What are React fragments and when should you use them?

Category: Components
Use React fragments when you need to return multiple elements from a component without adding an extra DOM element, such as in lists, table rows, or grouped content, to keep the DOM tree minimal and semantically correct.

751. What are React fragments and why are they useful?

Category: Components
React fragments are useful because they allow grouping multiple elements without creating unnecessary DOM nodes, which can reduce complexity and improve performance, especially when working with lists or returning multiple children.

752. What are React fragments and why use them?

Category: Components
Use React fragments to avoid adding extra HTML elements to the DOM when a component returns multiple elements. This keeps the DOM clean and avoids potential styling or layout issues from unnecessary wrappers.

753. What are React Higher-Order Components (HOCs)?

Category: Advanced Patterns
React Higher-Order Components are functions that accept a component and return a new component with added behavior or data. They promote reusability by abstracting common functionality, allowing components to be enhanced without modifying their original code.

754. What are React hooks and why are they useful?

Category: Hooks
React hooks are functions that enable state and lifecycle features in functional components, making them more powerful and expressive. They simplify code by removing the need for class components and allow easy reuse of stateful logic across components.

755. What are React hooks and why were they introduced?

Category: Hooks
React hooks were introduced to enable functional components to have state and lifecycle methods, which were previously only available in class components. This leads to simpler, cleaner code and better ways to share logic between components.

756. What are React hooks rules and best practices?

Category: Hooks
React hooks rules include only calling hooks at the top level of your components or custom hooks, never inside loops or conditions, and only calling them from React function components or custom hooks. Best practices involve keeping hooks pure, minimizing dependencies, and using them to organize logic cleanly.

757. What are React Hooks rules and why must they be followed?

Category: Hooks Rules
React Hooks rules must be followed to ensure predictable behavior and avoid bugs. Calling hooks conditionally or inside loops breaks the rules and can cause inconsistent state or lifecycle behavior, leading to difficult-to-debug errors.

758. What are React hooks rules you must follow?

Category: Hooks Rules
The key rules for React hooks are: call hooks only at the top level, never inside loops, conditions, or nested functions; call hooks only from React function components or custom hooks; and always manage hook dependencies carefully to avoid stale data or unwanted re-renders.

759. What are React Hooks?

Category: Hooks
React Hooks are special functions that let you “hook into” React features such as state and lifecycle methods from functional components. Introduced to simplify component logic, hooks like useState, useEffect, and useContext allow functional components to be more powerful and reusable without relying on classes.

760. What are React portals and how are they used?

Category: Advanced Features
React portals provide a way to render children into a DOM node that exists outside the parent component's DOM hierarchy. This is useful for modals, tooltips, or dropdowns that need to visually break out of container boundaries, ensuring proper layering and interaction without breaking component structure.

761. What are React portals and how do they work?

Category: Advanced Features
React portals work by using the ReactDOM.createPortal API to render React children into a different DOM subtree. Even though the portal content is rendered outside the parent hierarchy, events propagate as if the portal were still part of the React tree, preserving event handling behavior.

762. What are React portals and how do you use them?

Category: Advanced Features
You use React portals by calling ReactDOM.createPortal(children, container), where children is the React node you want to render and container is the DOM element outside the current component hierarchy. This allows rendering UI elements like modals or tooltips outside normal DOM flow.

763. What are React portals and when should you use them?

Category: Advanced Features
Use React portals when you need to render components outside of their parent container in the DOM for styling or layout reasons — such as modals, tooltips, dropdowns, or popovers — especially when those components must overlay other elements or escape CSS overflow rules.

764. What are React portals and why use them?

Category: Advanced Features
React portals are used to break out of the parent DOM hierarchy, allowing components to render in different parts of the DOM while maintaining React’s declarative model and event propagation. This solves layout and layering challenges without losing React's event system benefits.

765. What are React portals used for?

Category: Advanced Features
React portals are primarily used for UI elements like modals, tooltips, dropdown menus, and overlays that need to visually escape the boundaries of their parent components to avoid clipping, stacking context issues, or CSS overflow constraints.

766. What are React portals?

Category: Advanced Features
React portals are a feature that allows rendering a subtree of React components into a DOM node outside the main DOM hierarchy of the parent component, enabling better control over layout and z-index stacking without breaking React's event system.

767. What are React Server Components and how do they differ from client components?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Server Components run on the server and send rendered HTML to the client without including the JavaScript for UI logic, reducing bundle size and improving performance. Client components run entirely on the client, managing UI interactions. Server components can't handle state or events but can fetch data directly.

768. What are React Server Components and how do they differ from traditional components?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Traditional React components run on the client and include UI logic and interactivity, while React Server Components execute on the server and send fully rendered UI to the client, which improves load times and reduces client bundle size by offloading work to the server.

769. What are React Server Components and their advantages?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Server Components improve performance by reducing JavaScript sent to the client, enabling faster initial load, allowing direct server data fetching without extra API calls, and simplifying state management since they don’t handle client-side interactivity.

770. What are React Server Components and their benefits?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Benefits of React Server Components include smaller client bundles, improved SEO and performance through server-side rendering, simplified data fetching by accessing server resources directly, and reduced complexity in managing client state.

771. What are React Server Components?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Server Components are components rendered on the server that send HTML and serialized state to the client. They enable apps to offload rendering work from the client, improving performance by reducing JavaScript bundle sizes and allowing seamless integration with server-side data.

772. What are React Suspense boundaries?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense boundaries are components that specify loading fallbacks while waiting for asynchronous operations like lazy-loaded components or data fetching. They enable declarative, smooth UI transitions during code or data loading without blocking the entire UI.

773. What are React synthetic events?

Category: Event Handling
React synthetic events are cross-browser wrappers around native browser events, providing a consistent API regardless of the environment. This abstraction improves performance by pooling events and ensures consistent event behavior across different browsers.

774. What are refs used for?

Category: Refs
Refs provide a way to access and interact directly with DOM elements or React components. They are commonly used for managing focus, triggering imperative animations, integrating with third-party DOM libraries, or storing mutable values that persist across renders without causing re-renders.

775. What are render props and when should you use them?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Render props are a technique where a component accepts a function as a prop to control what it renders. This pattern allows sharing logic between components in a flexible way and is useful when different rendering or behavior needs to be injected dynamically.

776. What are render props in React?

Category: Advanced Patterns
In React, render props refer to a pattern where a component uses a prop whose value is a function to render content. This technique promotes reusability by letting components share behavior while customizing the UI output.

777. What are render props?

Category: Advanced Patterns
Render props are a React pattern where a component’s child is a function that returns React elements. This enables components to share functionality while allowing flexible rendering controlled by the consumer of the component.

778. What are RTK Query benefits and use cases?

Category: State Management
RTK Query, part of Redux Toolkit, simplifies data fetching and caching in Redux apps. It reduces boilerplate, handles caching, automatic refetching, and provides easy integration with Redux state, making it ideal for complex state management involving server data.

779. What are rules of hooks violations?

Category: Hooks
Rules of hooks violations occur when hooks are used incorrectly, such as calling hooks conditionally, inside loops, or outside React functional components. Breaking these rules can cause inconsistent hook calls, leading to bugs and unexpected behavior because React relies on hook call order to manage state and effects.

780. What are selectors in Redux?

Category: State Management
Selectors in Redux are functions that extract specific pieces of state from the Redux store. They help encapsulate and memoize state selection logic, improving code readability and performance by preventing unnecessary component re-renders when unrelated state changes.
