781. What are semantic HTML elements and why are they important in React?

Category: Accessibility
Semantic HTML elements clearly describe their meaning to browsers and assistive technologies, improving accessibility and SEO. Using elements like <header>, <main>, and <nav> in React helps users with disabilities navigate and understand web content more effectively.

782. What are side effects in React?

Category: Hooks
Side effects in React refer to operations that affect something outside the scope of the component, such as data fetching, subscriptions, or manual DOM manipulation. The useEffect hook is designed to handle side effects safely within functional components.

783. What are slices in Redux Toolkit?

Category: State Management
Slices in Redux Toolkit represent a modular piece of the Redux state and its logic, encapsulating reducers, actions, and initial state together. This abstraction simplifies Redux setup and helps organize state management by feature or domain.

784. What are snapshot tests in React?

Category: Testing
Snapshot tests capture the rendered output of React components at a point in time, storing it in a snapshot file. Future test runs compare the current output to this snapshot to detect unintended UI changes, providing a simple way to ensure UI consistency.

785. What are snapshot tests?

Category: Testing
Snapshot tests automatically save a component’s rendered output and compare it against future renders. They help catch unexpected changes in UI by flagging differences, useful for regression testing in React applications.

786. What are some best practices for styling React components?

Category: Styling
Best practices for styling React components include using CSS Modules or styled-components for scoped styles, keeping styles modular and reusable, favoring CSS-in-JS for dynamic styling, and maintaining consistent naming conventions. Avoid global styles that can cause conflicts and leverage theme providers for design consistency.

787. What are some common pitfalls when using hooks?

Category: Hooks
Common pitfalls include violating rules of hooks (calling hooks conditionally or inside loops), missing dependencies in useEffect, causing infinite loops, overusing hooks leading to complex code, and misunderstanding when to use state versus refs. These mistakes can cause bugs and poor performance.

788. What are some common use cases for React?

Category: Core Concepts
React is commonly used for building dynamic web apps, single-page applications, component libraries, dashboards, and mobile apps via React Native. It excels in managing complex UIs with reusable components and efficient state updates.

789. What are some core components in React Native?

Category: React Native
Core React Native components include View for containers, Text for text display, Image for images, ScrollView for scrollable containers, and TextInput for user input, providing the foundation for building native mobile UIs.

790. What are some strategies for testing React hooks?

Category: Testing
Strategies include using React Testing Library’s renderHook utility for isolated hook testing, mocking dependencies, testing state changes and effects, and ensuring hooks behave correctly in different scenarios. Testing hooks independently helps maintain reliable and reusable logic.

791. What are some use cases for portals?

Category: Advanced Features
Portals are ideal for rendering modals, tooltips, dropdowns, context menus, and overlays that must escape their parent container’s CSS overflow or z-index constraints, enabling better UI layering and positioning without breaking component structure.

792. What are subscriptions in GraphQL?

Category: Data Fetching
GraphQL subscriptions enable real-time updates by maintaining a persistent connection (usually via WebSockets) between client and server. When data changes, the server pushes updates to subscribed clients, ideal for live feeds, notifications, or real-time collaboration.

793. What are Suspense and Concurrent Mode in React 18?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Suspense allows components to wait for asynchronous data or code to load before rendering, improving UX with fallback UIs. Concurrent Mode enables React to interrupt and prioritize rendering tasks, resulting in smoother, more responsive apps by rendering in chunks and avoiding UI blocking.

794. What are synthetic events and how are they different from native events?

Category: Event Handling
Synthetic events in React are wrappers around native browser events, providing a consistent cross-browser API. Unlike native events, they are pooled and managed by React for performance and uniform behavior, abstracting away browser inconsistencies.

795. What are synthetic events in React and how are they managed?

Category: Event Handling
React manages synthetic events using an event delegation system attached to the root DOM container. Events are pooled and reused to reduce memory overhead. React normalizes event properties across browsers for consistent behavior.

796. What are synthetic events in React and how do they differ from native events?

Category: Event Handling
Synthetic events differ from native events in that they provide a unified interface, pooling, and normalization to handle browser inconsistencies. Native events are direct browser events, while synthetic events are React’s abstraction layer for cross-browser compatibility and improved performance.

797. What are synthetic events in React and how do they work?

Category: Event Handling
Synthetic events in React wrap native browser events to provide consistent properties and behavior across browsers. React pools these events for efficiency, releasing them after the event callback to minimize memory usage.

798. What are synthetic events in React?

Category: Event Handling
React synthetic events are its cross-browser event system that wraps native browser events, ensuring consistent behavior, better performance, and simplified event management by pooling and normalizing event objects.

799. What are the advantages and disadvantages of Redux compared to React Context?

Category: State Management
Redux offers a structured, predictable state container with tools like middleware and DevTools, ideal for large or complex apps. React Context is simpler and built-in, suited for low-frequency state sharing. Redux has more boilerplate but better debugging; Context can cause unnecessary re-renders and lacks advanced features.

800. What are the advantages and disadvantages of render props?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Advantages of render props include flexible code reuse and dynamic UI composition by passing functions as props. However, they can lead to verbose syntax, nested components (callback hell), and harder-to-follow code compared to hooks or higher-order components.

801. What are the advantages of functional components over class components?

Category: Components
Functional components offer a simpler syntax and are easier to read and test compared to class components. They avoid the complexity of the this keyword and lifecycle methods, making state management more straightforward through hooks. Functional components typically have better performance optimizations due to React's internal handling and can leverage modern React features like hooks and concurrent mode more naturally.

802. What are the advantages of using TypeScript with React?

Category: TypeScript
TypeScript adds static typing to React, which helps catch errors at compile time rather than runtime. It improves code quality, enhances developer productivity with better tooling and autocompletion, and makes refactoring safer. TypeScript also improves documentation and onboarding by explicitly defining component props and state shapes.

803. What are the benefits of custom hooks?

Category: Hooks
Custom hooks enable code reuse and abstraction of complex logic related to stateful behavior or side effects. They help keep components clean and focused on rendering while encapsulating reusable behavior, improving maintainability and testability across your React application.

804. What are the benefits of React Server Components?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Server Components allow rendering components on the server without sending their code to the client. This reduces bundle size, improves initial load performance, and enables efficient data fetching since server components can access server resources directly. They help build faster, more scalable apps with seamless server-client integration.

805. What are the benefits of React.memo for functional components?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo is a higher-order component that memoizes functional components, preventing unnecessary re-renders when props have not changed. This optimization improves performance by reducing the workload for React, especially in large component trees or expensive render operations.

806. What are the benefits of SSR?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Server-Side Rendering (SSR) improves initial page load performance and SEO by rendering React components on the server as HTML before sending to the client. It enables faster perceived loading times and better indexing by search engines, enhancing user experience and discoverability.

807. What are the benefits of static rendering in web apps?

Category: SSR / SSG
Static rendering generates HTML files at build time, allowing extremely fast loading since pages can be served from a CDN without server computation. It improves scalability, security, and SEO, especially for content-heavy or marketing sites where data doesn’t change frequently.

808. What are the benefits of using functional components over class components?

Category: Components
Functional components offer concise syntax, easier debugging, better performance optimizations, and direct access to hooks for managing state and side effects. They avoid the complexity of classes and enable cleaner, more maintainable code aligned with modern React best practices.

809. What are the benefits of using React functional components over class components?

Category: Core Concepts
Functional components simplify component logic, improve readability, and leverage hooks for state and lifecycle management. They remove the need for binding this and reduce boilerplate, resulting in cleaner and more performant React codebases.

810. What are the benefits of using React Router?

Category: Routing
React Router enables declarative, client-side routing in React apps, providing seamless navigation without full page reloads. It supports nested routes, dynamic URL parameters, and programmatic navigation, enhancing user experience and app structure.

811. What are the benefits of using React Server Components?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Server Components reduce client-side bundle size by rendering components on the server, improving load times. They allow direct data fetching on the server, reduce client complexity, and integrate seamlessly with client components for hybrid rendering.

812. What are the benefits of using React StrictMode?

Category: Advanced Features
React StrictMode helps identify potential problems by activating additional checks and warnings during development. It detects deprecated APIs, side effects, and unsafe lifecycles, encouraging best practices and more resilient code.

813. What are the benefits of using React.memo?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo improves performance by memoizing functional components and preventing unnecessary re-renders when props have not changed, reducing computational overhead in complex UIs.

814. What are the benefits of using useCallback vs inline functions?

Category: Performance Optimization
useCallback memoizes callback functions, preventing unnecessary re-creation on every render, which helps avoid unnecessary child re-renders or effect executions. Inline functions create new references on each render, possibly causing performance issues.

815. What are the best practices for accessibility in React applications?

Category: Accessibility
Best practices include using semantic HTML, managing focus correctly, providing ARIA attributes where needed, ensuring keyboard navigability, using meaningful alt text for images, and testing with screen readers to create inclusive React apps.

816. What are the best practices for optimizing React applications?

Category: Performance
Key practices include minimizing unnecessary re-renders using memoization, code-splitting with Suspense and lazy loading, efficient state management, avoiding inline functions, and optimizing list rendering with keys and virtualization.

817. What are the best practices for structuring React projects?

Category: Architecture
Organize files by feature or domain, keep components small and reusable, separate concerns (components, hooks, utilities), and use consistent naming conventions. Modular design aids maintainability and scalability.

818. What are the best practices for styling React components?

Category: Styling
Use CSS Modules, styled-components, or emotion for scoped styling, keep styles modular and reusable, prefer declarative styles over inline, and maintain consistency with design tokens or themes.

819. What are the best practices for using React context?

Category: State Management
Use context for global or app-wide state, avoid overusing it for frequent updates to prevent unnecessary re-renders, memoize context values, and combine with reducers or other state management patterns for complex state.

820. What are the common accessibility features in React?

Category: Accessibility
Common features include semantic elements, ARIA roles and attributes, keyboard navigation support, focus management, alt text for images, and screen reader compatibility to ensure inclusivity.

821. What are the common pitfalls in React performance optimization?

Category: Performance Optimization
Pitfalls include unnecessary re-renders due to non-memoized props or state, overusing context for frequently changing data, heavy computations on the main thread, and not leveraging code splitting or lazy loading effectively.

822. What are the core principles of Redux?

Category: State Management
Redux principles are a single source of truth for state, state is read-only and changes via actions, and changes are made with pure reducer functions. This predictable state management makes apps easier to debug and test.

823. What are the dependencies in useEffect used for?

Category: Hooks
The dependency array tells React when to re-run the effect. Effects run only when dependencies change, optimizing performance by avoiding unnecessary effect executions.

824. What are the differences between class components and functional components?

Category: Components
Class components use ES6 classes, lifecycle methods, and this keyword, while functional components are simpler functions that use hooks for state and side effects. Functional components generally have less boilerplate and are easier to test and optimize.

825. What are the differences between client-side and server-side rendering?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Client-side rendering generates UI in the browser using JavaScript after the initial HTML is loaded, leading to faster subsequent interactions but slower initial load. Server-side rendering produces full HTML on the server, improving initial load time and SEO, but requires server resources.

826. What are the differences between controlled and uncontrolled components?

Category: Forms
Controlled components have their state managed by React through props and callbacks, offering full control over input values. Uncontrolled components store their own state internally in the DOM, accessed via refs, leading to simpler but less controllable forms.

827. What are the differences between React and Angular?

Category: Comparison
React is a library focusing on UI building with a virtual DOM and unidirectional data flow, whereas Angular is a full-fledged framework with a comprehensive ecosystem including routing, form management, and dependency injection. React uses JSX and encourages component-based architecture, while Angular uses TypeScript and templates.

828. What are the differences between React and Vue?

Category: Comparison
React is a UI library focused on JSX and functional programming with hooks, while Vue is a progressive framework offering templates, reactivity, and a gentle learning curve. Vue provides more built-in features like directives and state management patterns, whereas React relies more on external libraries.

829. What are the differences between React class components and functional components?

Category: Core Concepts
Class components use lifecycle methods and the this keyword, whereas functional components leverage hooks for managing state and effects. Functional components tend to have cleaner syntax and are preferred in modern React development.

830. What are the differences between React Router v5 and v6?

Category: Routing
React Router v6 introduces simplified route definitions with nested routes, better route matching algorithms, automatic route ranking, and hooks like useNavigate. It drops support for some deprecated features from v5 and offers improved performance and developer experience.

831. What are the differences between Redux and Context API?

Category: State Management
Redux is a predictable state container for JavaScript apps designed to manage global state with a strict unidirectional data flow, middleware support, and powerful developer tools. It is suitable for complex state logic and large applications. The Context API, on the other hand, is a React built-in feature that provides a way to pass data through the component tree without prop drilling. While Context API is simpler and great for theming or lightweight state sharing, it does not provide the advanced capabilities of Redux such as middleware, time-travel debugging, or fine-grained control over updates. Redux focuses on immutable updates and centralizing state, whereas Context API is more about dependency injection and less about state management complexity.

832. What are the differences between useEffect and useInsertionEffect?

Category: Hooks
useEffect is a React hook that runs after the render is committed to the screen, suitable for side effects like data fetching, subscriptions, or manual DOM mutations. It does not block the browser paint and is asynchronous with respect to rendering. useInsertionEffect is a newer hook designed for injecting styles or making mutations before the browser paints, ensuring styles or changes are applied synchronously and avoiding visual inconsistencies or flickers. It runs synchronously after all DOM mutations but before the browser paints, making it ideal for CSS-in-JS libraries to inject styles efficiently. Unlike useEffect, useInsertionEffect is very rarely needed and should be used with caution as improper use can cause blocking behavior.

833. What are the differences between useEffect cleanup and componentWillUnmount?

Category: Hooks
The cleanup function returned by useEffect acts as a combination of componentWillUnmount and some aspects of componentDidUpdate lifecycle methods in class components. It runs before the component unmounts or before the effect re-runs due to dependencies changing, allowing you to clean up subscriptions, timers, or event listeners. componentWillUnmount, on the other hand, only runs once before a class component is removed from the DOM. useEffect cleanup is more flexible since it handles both unmounting and updates, while componentWillUnmount is only for unmount. The hook-based cleanup promotes declarative side effect management within functional components.

834. What are the limitations of React error boundaries?

Category: Error Handling
React error boundaries can catch errors during rendering, in lifecycle methods, and in constructors of the whole tree below them, but they cannot catch errors inside event handlers, asynchronous code (e.g., setTimeout callbacks), server-side rendering, or errors thrown in the error boundary itself. Also, they do not recover the component’s previous state but instead provide a fallback UI, requiring manual recovery strategies. Error boundaries work only with class components, limiting their use in functional components unless wrapped appropriately. They also do not solve all error handling but are useful for preventing the entire React component tree from crashing.

835. What are the limitations of React?

Category: Core Concepts
React focuses primarily on the UI layer and does not provide built-in solutions for routing, state management, or form handling, requiring third-party libraries. It also uses a virtual DOM abstraction that may lead to some performance overhead in very complex apps or with large lists, unless optimized carefully. React’s JSX syntax can be a barrier for beginners, and server-side rendering adds complexity. React enforces a unidirectional data flow which can make some patterns less intuitive. Additionally, React’s frequent updates and evolving API require developers to continuously learn and adapt.

836. What are the main features of React?

Category: Basics
React is a declarative, component-based JavaScript library for building user interfaces. Its main features include the use of JSX to write components, the virtual DOM for efficient updates, unidirectional data flow, and the ability to compose reusable UI components. React supports hooks, enabling functional components to manage state and side effects. It also offers features like context for prop drilling avoidance, concurrent mode (experimental), and server-side rendering capabilities, making it highly versatile for building modern web applications.

837. What are the pros and cons of server-side rendering?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Server-side rendering (SSR) improves initial load performance and SEO by rendering the React components to HTML on the server, delivering fully rendered pages to clients. This results in faster perceived load times and better crawlability by search engines. However, SSR adds complexity to the build and deployment process, increases server load, and can complicate state hydration on the client. It may also delay interactivity until the JavaScript bundles are loaded and hydrated. Debugging SSR issues can be more challenging, and SSR requires more careful handling of side effects and asynchronous data fetching.

838. What are the rules of hooks in React?

Category: Hooks
The rules of hooks ensure consistent behavior and predictable state management in React functional components. They include: only call hooks at the top level of your component or custom hooks (not inside loops, conditions, or nested functions), only call hooks from React function components or custom hooks (not regular JavaScript functions), and always use hooks in the same order on every render. Following these rules prevents bugs related to inconsistent hook state and maintains React’s internal hook state management integrity.

839. What are the rules of hooks?

Category: Hooks Rules
The rules of hooks are fundamental guidelines for using React hooks correctly: hooks must be called at the top level of a React function component or custom hook; hooks must never be called inside loops, conditions, or nested functions; and hooks must only be called from React function components or custom hooks. These rules ensure hooks run consistently on every render, preserving state order and preventing unexpected behaviors.

840. What are the rules of JSX syntax?

Category: JSX
JSX syntax rules require that every JSX element must have one parent element, and expressions inside JSX should be wrapped in curly braces. Attributes must be camelCased (e.g., className instead of class), and self-closing tags must include the slash (e.g., <input />). JavaScript expressions, not statements, can be used within JSX, and comments inside JSX require curly braces with JavaScript comment syntax. JSX elements must return a single expression, and conditional rendering typically uses ternary operators or logical operators. These rules ensure JSX compiles correctly to JavaScript and maintains clarity in React components.

841. What are the rules of using Hooks?

Category: Hooks
Using hooks correctly requires following specific rules to ensure React can track the component's state and lifecycle properly. Hooks must only be called at the top level of a React function component or custom hook and never inside loops, conditions, or nested functions. This maintains a consistent hook call order across renders. Hooks can only be called from React function components or custom hooks, not from regular JavaScript functions. These rules ensure the internal hook state remains synchronized with the component lifecycle and avoids unpredictable behavior.

842. What are the three main phases of the React lifecycle?

Category: Lifecycle
The three main phases of the React lifecycle are Mounting, Updating, and Unmounting. Mounting occurs when a component is created and inserted into the DOM. Updating happens when a component’s props or state change, triggering a re-render. Unmounting is when a component is removed from the DOM, allowing cleanup of resources. Each phase provides specific lifecycle methods or hooks (e.g., useEffect) for developers to hook into and perform side effects accordingly.

843. What are the trade-offs of using Redux vs Context API?

Category: State Management
Redux offers a powerful, scalable state management solution with strict rules, middleware support, time-travel debugging, and a global immutable store. It’s well-suited for complex applications with large and deeply nested state. However, Redux can introduce boilerplate code and has a steeper learning curve. The Context API is simpler, built into React, and great for passing data without prop drilling in smaller or medium apps. Its trade-off is less control over updates and no built-in middleware or developer tools, which can lead to performance issues with frequent context updates. Choosing between them depends on app complexity, performance needs, and developer preference.

844. What are the ways to style React components?

Category: Styling
React components can be styled using various methods, including traditional CSS files, CSS Modules for scoped styles, inline styles via the style attribute, styled-components or other CSS-in-JS libraries for component-level styling with dynamic props, and utility-first frameworks like Tailwind CSS. Each approach offers trade-offs between maintainability, performance, and developer experience. Global CSS is simple but prone to conflicts, CSS Modules localize styles, and CSS-in-JS provides dynamic styling capabilities with JavaScript logic integration.

845. What are uncontrolled components?

Category: Forms
Uncontrolled components are form elements in React where form data is handled by the DOM itself rather than by React state. Their values are accessed using refs rather than controlled via state and onChange handlers. This approach is simpler for basic use cases or integrating with non-React code but offers less control and validation capability compared to controlled components. Uncontrolled components rely on the browser to manage input state internally.

846. What are uncontrolled inputs and when to use them?

Category: Forms
Uncontrolled inputs are form fields whose value is managed by the DOM instead of React state. They are typically accessed via refs to read the current value when needed. They are useful when integrating React with legacy code, when form data doesn’t need to update the UI on every change, or for simpler forms where full control is unnecessary. However, they lack the fine-grained control and instant validation available with controlled inputs.

847. What are Webpack and Babel in the React toolchain?

Category: Tooling
Webpack is a module bundler that processes and bundles JavaScript files and assets into optimized static files for the browser. It handles dependencies, code splitting, and asset management. Babel is a JavaScript compiler that transforms modern ES6+ and JSX syntax into backwards-compatible JavaScript that browsers can execute. Together, they enable React developers to write modern code and deliver performant apps compatible with various environments.

848. What happens if you call useState inside a loop?

Category: Hooks
Calling useState inside a loop violates the rules of hooks because hooks must be called in the same order on every render. This can cause React to lose track of state hooks, leading to bugs or runtime errors. Hooks need to be called unconditionally at the top level of a component or custom hook to maintain consistent hook call order and state integrity.

849. What happens if you omit the dependency array in useEffect?

Category: Hooks
If the dependency array is omitted in useEffect, the effect runs after every render, causing the effect to execute repeatedly. This can lead to performance issues or infinite loops if the effect updates state that triggers a re-render. Including dependencies ensures the effect runs only when specified values change.

850. What happens if you pass an empty array to useEffect?

Category: Hooks
Passing an empty dependency array to useEffect causes the effect to run only once after the initial render, mimicking componentDidMount behavior. The effect will not re-run on updates, making it useful for initialization logic or fetching data when the component mounts.

851. What happens when state changes in a component?

Category: State
When state changes in a React component, React schedules a re-render of that component and its children. During re-rendering, the component recalculates its UI based on the new state. React then efficiently updates the DOM by comparing the previous and new virtual DOM representations, applying only necessary changes to keep the UI in sync.

852. What happens when you call setState?

Category: Basics
Calling setState in a class component or using the updater function from useState in a functional component queues a state update. React schedules a re-render with the new state values, merges or replaces the existing state, and updates the UI accordingly. State updates are asynchronous and may be batched for performance optimization.

853. What is a controlled component?

Category: Forms
A controlled component is a form element whose value is controlled by React state. The component’s input value is set via props and updated through event handlers like onChange, ensuring React has full control over the form data. This allows for instant validation, conditionally enabling/disabling inputs, and synchronizing UI with application state.

854. What is a controlled vs uncontrolled pattern in UI components?

Category: Design Patterns
The controlled vs uncontrolled pattern in UI components refers to how component state and data flow are managed. Controlled components rely on React state as the single source of truth, where component data is passed via props and updated through callbacks, giving React full control over the component. Uncontrolled components delegate state management to the DOM, using refs to access values when needed. Controlled patterns offer more predictable data flow and easier validation, while uncontrolled can simplify integration with legacy code or reduce complexity in simple forms.

855. What is a custom hook and why would you write one?

Category: Hooks
A custom hook is a reusable function that encapsulates common React hook logic to share functionality across multiple components. It starts with "use" and can manage state, side effects, or any React hook-related behavior. Writing custom hooks promotes code reuse, abstraction, and separation of concerns, enabling cleaner and more maintainable component code by extracting repeated logic into standalone, testable units.

856. What is a layout in Next.js?

Category: SSR / SSG
In Next.js, a layout is a React component that wraps around pages to provide a consistent structure, styling, or UI elements like headers, footers, or navigation across multiple pages. Layouts help avoid code duplication by centralizing shared UI parts. Next.js supports defining layouts per page or globally, enabling flexible and maintainable UI composition.

857. What is a loader in Webpack?

Category: Tooling
A loader in Webpack is a module that transforms files as they are loaded, enabling Webpack to process non-JavaScript assets like CSS, images, or transpile JavaScript using Babel. Loaders operate as chained transformations to convert source files into modules that Webpack can bundle. They are essential for extending Webpack’s capability to handle various file types.

858. What is a nested route in React Router?

Category: Routing
A nested route in React Router allows defining routes inside other routes, enabling hierarchical URL structures and UI layouts. This means a parent route renders child routes within its component, allowing complex page compositions with shared UI components like sidebars or headers. Nested routing enhances code organization and user navigation flow.

859. What is a plugin in Webpack?

Category: Tooling
A plugin in Webpack is a powerful extension that can tap into the build process to perform tasks like bundle optimization, asset management, environment variable injection, and more. Unlike loaders that transform files, plugins enable more complex functionalities by hooking into Webpack’s lifecycle events and modifying output or behavior.

860. What is a portal in React?

Category: Advanced
A portal in React provides a way to render children into a DOM node outside the parent component’s hierarchy. This is useful for modals, tooltips, or overlays where the rendered element needs to visually break out of its parent container but still maintain React’s event handling and lifecycle. Portals help avoid CSS overflow or z-index issues while keeping React's declarative model.

861. What is a reducer and how does it work?

Category: State Management
A reducer is a pure function that takes the current state and an action, then returns a new state based on that action. It follows the principle of immutability by not modifying the existing state but producing a new one. Reducers are commonly used in Redux and React’s useReducer hook to manage complex state transitions in a predictable and testable manner.

862. What is a unit test in React?

Category: Testing
A unit test in React verifies the behavior of a small, isolated part of the application such as a component or function. It ensures that the component renders correctly given specific props, handles user interactions as expected, and produces the correct output. Unit tests improve code reliability and facilitate refactoring by catching regressions early.

863. What is an error boundary in React?

Category: Error Handling
An error boundary is a React component that catches JavaScript errors anywhere in its child component tree, logs those errors, and displays a fallback UI instead of the component tree that crashed. Error boundaries improve user experience by preventing the entire app from crashing due to errors in parts of the UI.

864. What is an uncontrolled component?

Category: Forms
An uncontrolled component is a form input element that manages its own internal state instead of relying on React state. Its value is accessed using refs rather than controlled via React props and onChange handlers. This pattern is simpler but less powerful for validation and control compared to controlled components.

865. What is Apollo Client and how is it used?

Category: Data Fetching
Apollo Client is a comprehensive state management library for JavaScript that enables fetching, caching, and managing data from a GraphQL API. In React, it integrates seamlessly with hooks and components to simplify data querying, mutation, and subscription, providing powerful caching and optimistic UI updates.

866. What is client-side navigation in Next.js?

Category: SSR / SSG
Client-side navigation in Next.js refers to transitioning between pages in the browser without a full page reload. It leverages the Next.js router to fetch and render new pages dynamically, preserving app state and enabling faster, smoother navigation experiences compared to traditional server-side page loads.

867. What is code splitting and how do you implement it?

Category: Code Splitting
Code splitting is a performance optimization technique that breaks up large JavaScript bundles into smaller chunks that can be loaded on demand. In React, it can be implemented using dynamic import() syntax with React.lazy and Suspense, or by leveraging tools like Webpack’s code splitting features. This reduces initial load time by loading only the necessary code for the current view.

868. What is code splitting and how is it implemented?

Category: Performance Optimization
Code splitting is implemented by dividing application code into smaller chunks loaded asynchronously as needed rather than all at once. In React, it’s commonly done using React.lazy and Suspense for components, or by configuring Webpack with dynamic imports. This improves performance by reducing bundle size and speeding up initial load.

869. What is code splitting and why is it important?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Code splitting is important because it improves application performance by minimizing initial bundle size, reducing load times, and improving responsiveness. It allows users to load only the necessary code for their current view, decreasing bandwidth usage and improving the perceived speed of the app, which is crucial for user experience especially on slow networks.

870. What is code splitting in React?

Category: Performance
Code splitting in React is the technique of breaking down the app’s JavaScript bundle into smaller pieces that load on demand, rather than loading everything upfront. React supports this via React.lazy for lazy loading components and Suspense to handle loading states. This approach optimizes performance by reducing the amount of code sent to the browser initially.

871. What is component composition in React?

Category: Design Patterns
Component composition in React is the pattern of building complex UIs by combining simpler, reusable components. Instead of creating large monolithic components, composition encourages breaking the UI into smaller, manageable parts that are nested and composed together. This promotes modularity, reusability, and easier maintenance by leveraging props and children to customize behavior and appearance.

872. What is componentDidMount used for?

Category: Lifecycle
componentDidMount is a lifecycle method in React class components that runs once immediately after a component is mounted (inserted into the DOM). It is commonly used for performing side effects such as fetching data, setting up subscriptions, or manipulating the DOM. It’s an ideal place to initiate asynchronous operations or integrations.

873. What is componentDidUpdate used for?

Category: Lifecycle
componentDidUpdate is a lifecycle method called after a component updates due to changes in props or state. It is used to react to those changes by performing side effects like network requests or DOM updates that depend on the updated data. Care must be taken to avoid infinite loops by comparing previous and current props/state.

874. What is componentWillUnmount used for?

Category: Lifecycle
componentWillUnmount is a lifecycle method called just before a component is removed from the DOM. It is used for cleanup tasks such as invalidating timers, canceling network requests, or removing event listeners to prevent memory leaks and unexpected behavior.

875. What is Concurrent Mode in React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Concurrent Mode is an experimental React feature designed to improve UI responsiveness by allowing React to interrupt and pause rendering work to prioritize urgent updates. It enables better handling of asynchronous rendering, avoiding blocking the main thread, and improving perceived performance and user experience by making rendering more interruptible and concurrent.

876. What is conditional rendering in React?

Category: Rendering
Conditional rendering in React is the technique of rendering components or elements based on conditions, usually using JavaScript operators like if statements or ternary expressions. This allows components to dynamically display different UI parts depending on application state, user interactions, or data.

877. What is context and how does it solve prop drilling?

Category: State Management
Context is a React feature that provides a way to pass data through the component tree without having to pass props manually at every level. It helps solve prop drilling, where intermediate components must pass props they don’t use to deeply nested children, by enabling direct access to shared data anywhere in the tree via a Context Provider and Consumer or useContext hook.

878. What is Context API and when to use it?

Category: Context API
Context API is a React built-in feature that enables sharing state or functions across many components without passing props explicitly. It is ideal for global data like themes, user authentication, or localization. Use it when multiple components at different levels need access to the same data, avoiding cumbersome prop drilling.

879. What is context-based i18n and how is it implemented?

Category: Internationalization
Context-based internationalization (i18n) in React uses Context API to provide translation data and locale information to components throughout the app. It is implemented by creating a context for language and messages, then wrapping the app with a provider that supplies the current locale and translations. Components consume this context to display localized text dynamically.

880. What is Create React App (CRA)?

Category: Tooling
Create React App (CRA) is an officially supported React toolchain that sets up a modern React development environment with no configuration. It provides a preconfigured build system with Webpack, Babel, development server, and scripts for building, testing, and running React apps, enabling developers to focus on writing code quickly.

881. What is end-to-end (E2E) testing?

Category: Testing
End-to-end (E2E) testing is a testing methodology that verifies an application’s functionality from the user's perspective by simulating real user scenarios from start to finish. It ensures that all integrated parts of the system work together correctly by testing the entire flow through the UI, APIs, and databases.

882. What is error boundary and how to implement it?

Category: Error Handling
An error boundary is a React component that catches JavaScript errors in its child component tree and displays a fallback UI. To implement it, create a class component with the lifecycle methods componentDidCatch and static getDerivedStateFromError to catch errors and update state. The fallback UI renders when an error occurs, preventing the entire app from crashing.

883. What is error boundary in React?

Category: Error Handling
An error boundary is a React component designed to catch runtime errors in its child component tree, log them, and display a fallback UI instead of crashing the whole app. Error boundaries improve stability by isolating errors and handling them gracefully.

884. What is event delegation in React?

Category: Event Handling
Event delegation in React is the practice where React attaches a single event listener at the root of the document instead of individual elements. When an event occurs, React uses event bubbling to determine which component should respond, improving performance and memory efficiency by minimizing event listeners.

885. What is Formik and how does it help with form management?

Category: Forms
Formik is a popular React library for building and managing forms. It simplifies form state management, validation, and submission handling by providing declarative APIs, reducing boilerplate, and improving developer productivity. Formik supports controlled components and integrates well with validation libraries like Yup.

886. What is Formik and why is it used?

Category: Forms
Formik is used in React applications to handle complex form logic by managing form state, validation, and submission. It abstracts away common form management tasks to reduce code complexity, making it easier to build scalable and maintainable forms.

887. What is getDerivedStateFromProps and when is it used?

Category: Lifecycle
getDerivedStateFromProps is a static lifecycle method in React called right before rendering, both on mount and update. It allows the component to update its internal state based on changes in props. It is used sparingly to synchronize state with props but can lead to complex logic if overused.

888. What is getServerSideProps in Next.js?

Category: SSR / SSG
getServerSideProps is a Next.js data fetching method that runs on the server for every request. It allows fetching data dynamically and passing it as props to the page component, enabling server-side rendering with up-to-date data on each request.

889. What is getSnapshotBeforeUpdate used for?

Category: Lifecycle
getSnapshotBeforeUpdate is a lifecycle method called right before the DOM is updated. It captures some information (a snapshot) from the DOM, such as scroll position, which can then be used in componentDidUpdate to make adjustments after the update.

890. What is getStaticPaths in Next.js?

Category: SSR / SSG
getStaticPaths is a Next.js function used with dynamic routes to specify which paths should be statically generated at build time. It returns a list of paths and fallback behavior, enabling static generation of dynamic pages for improved performance and SEO.

891. What is getStaticProps in Next.js?

Category: SSR / SSG
getStaticProps is a Next.js data-fetching method used for static site generation (SSG). It runs at build time and fetches data that is passed as props to a page component, allowing pages to be pre-rendered with dynamic data. This improves performance by serving static HTML with the necessary data embedded.

892. What is hydration in React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Hydration in React refers to the process where React attaches event listeners and reactivates a server-rendered HTML markup on the client side. After static HTML is loaded in the browser, React “hydrates” it by converting it into a fully interactive React app, enabling seamless transitions between server-side and client-side rendering.

893. What is inversion of control in React components?

Category: Design Patterns
Inversion of control in React components is a design pattern where a parent component controls the flow of data and behavior by passing callbacks or render props to child components, instead of children managing their own state or logic. This enables greater flexibility and reuse by delegating control to higher-level components.

894. What is Jest and how is it used in React projects?

Category: Testing
Jest is a popular JavaScript testing framework often used in React projects for unit, integration, and snapshot testing. It provides an easy-to-use API, built-in assertions, mocking capabilities, and fast test execution. Jest helps ensure React components and logic behave as expected.

895. What is Jotai and how does it handle state management?

Category: State Management
Jotai is a minimalistic state management library for React that uses atoms to represent individual pieces of state. It offers simple and flexible APIs for reading and writing atoms, enabling fine-grained reactivity and avoiding unnecessary re-renders by isolating state slices.

896. What is JSX and how does it differ from HTML?

Category: Core Concepts
JSX is a syntax extension for JavaScript that looks similar to HTML but allows embedding React components and JavaScript expressions within markup. Unlike HTML, JSX is not a string but transpiled to React.createElement calls, enabling dynamic UI construction with JavaScript logic.

897. What is JSX and how does it work in React?

Category: Core Concepts
JSX works by allowing developers to write XML-like code in JavaScript files which is then compiled to React.createElement calls during build time. This process creates a tree of React elements that React uses to build the virtual DOM, enabling efficient updates and rendering.

898. What is JSX and how does it work?

Category: Core Concepts
JSX is a declarative syntax that combines JavaScript and XML-like markup. It is transformed by tools like Babel into JavaScript function calls creating React elements. This allows developers to describe UI structure declaratively while enabling dynamic rendering through JavaScript expressions.

899. What is JSX and why is it used in React?

Category: Core Concepts
JSX is used in React to simplify UI creation by allowing developers to write markup-like syntax directly in JavaScript. This improves readability and makes component structure intuitive while still enabling powerful dynamic behavior via embedded JavaScript.

900. What is JSX and why is it used?

Category: Core Concepts
JSX is used to describe the UI in a declarative way that combines HTML-like syntax with JavaScript logic. It improves developer experience by providing a clear, concise way to define component layouts and behaviors, making React code more maintainable and expressive.

901. What is JSX in React?

Category: Core Concepts
JSX in React is a syntax extension that lets developers write markup-like code directly within JavaScript. It is transpiled into React.createElement calls which produce React elements, facilitating a declarative and efficient way to define UI components.

902. What is JSX?

Category: JSX
JSX is a syntax extension for JavaScript that resembles HTML and is used with React to describe the structure of UI components. It combines markup and JavaScript expressions, allowing a seamless way to build interactive user interfaces declaratively.

903. What is lazy loading and code splitting?

Category: Code Splitting
Lazy loading and code splitting are performance optimization techniques where application code is divided into smaller chunks that load only when needed. This reduces initial load time and improves responsiveness by loading components or modules asynchronously.

904. What is lazy loading in React?

Category: Code Splitting
Lazy loading in React refers to deferring the loading of components or modules until they are actually needed in the UI. It is commonly implemented with React.lazy and Suspense to dynamically import components, improving the app’s performance by reducing initial bundle size.

905. What is lifting state up in React?

Category: State Management
Lifting state up in React is the technique of moving shared state from child components to their closest common ancestor. This allows multiple components to access and synchronize state through props, promoting better data flow and coordination.

906. What is memoization and how is it used in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Memoization is an optimization technique where expensive function calls or component renders are cached based on input parameters to avoid unnecessary recalculations. In React, memoization is used via React.memo, useMemo, or useCallback to improve performance by reducing redundant renders.

907. What is memoization in React?

Category: Performance Optimization
Memoization in React refers to caching the results of functions or component outputs based on inputs, so subsequent renders or calculations can reuse cached results. This reduces unnecessary work, enhancing rendering efficiency and application performance.

908. What is middleware in Redux?

Category: State Management
Middleware in Redux is a layer that intercepts dispatched actions before they reach reducers, allowing additional processing like logging, asynchronous calls, or modifying actions. It extends Redux's capabilities by enabling side effects and complex workflows within the state management flow.

909. What is mocking in testing and how is it done?

Category: Testing
Mocking in testing is the practice of creating simulated versions of functions, modules, or components to isolate and control behavior during tests. It is done by replacing real implementations with mocks that return predefined responses, allowing focused testing without side effects or dependencies.

910. What is Next.js and how does it support SSR and SSG?

Category: SSR / SSG
Next.js is a React framework that supports Server-Side Rendering (SSR) and Static Site Generation (SSG) to improve performance and SEO. It offers built-in methods like getServerSideProps for SSR and getStaticProps for SSG, enabling pages to be pre-rendered on the server or at build time.

911. What is optimistic UI in data fetching?

Category: Data Fetching
Optimistic UI is a technique where the user interface updates immediately in anticipation of a successful server response, improving perceived performance and user experience. If the server later responds with an error, the UI state is reverted or corrected accordingly.

912. What is prop drilling and how can Context API help?

Category: State Management
Prop drilling occurs when props are passed through multiple layers of components just to reach deeply nested components, which can lead to cluttered code and maintenance challenges. Context API helps by providing a way to share data globally across the component tree without explicit prop passing.

913. What is prop drilling and how can it be avoided?

Category: State Management
Prop drilling is the process of passing props down through many component layers unnecessarily. It can be avoided using Context API, state management libraries like Redux, or component composition techniques that provide shared state more directly and cleanly.

914. What is prop drilling and how can you avoid it?

Category: State Management
To avoid prop drilling, developers can leverage Context API to share data at higher levels, use global state management tools like Redux or MobX, or refactor components to reduce unnecessary intermediate prop passing, improving code clarity and maintainability.

915. What is prop drilling and how do you avoid it?

Category: State Management
Avoiding prop drilling involves using Context API to provide state or functions globally, employing state management libraries, or restructuring components to minimize deep prop chains, thus keeping component interfaces simpler and easier to maintain.

916. What is prop drilling and how do you prevent it?

Category: State Management
Prop drilling is prevented by avoiding excessive passing of props through multiple component layers. Solutions include using React Context for shared state, adopting centralized state management libraries, or utilizing render props and hooks to access data where needed.

917. What is prop drilling and how do you solve it?

Category: State Management
Prop drilling is solved by introducing Context API to provide data at a higher level, using state management solutions, or redesigning components to avoid passing props through many layers, thereby simplifying the data flow and improving maintainability.

918. What is prop drilling and how to avoid it?

Category: State Management
Prop drilling can be avoided by utilizing React's Context API to share data without passing props manually through each component, using external state management libraries, or adopting composition patterns to provide data more efficiently.

919. What is prop drilling?

Category: State Management
Prop drilling is the process where data or functions are passed down multiple nested components via props, even when intermediate components do not need them, which can make the code verbose and harder to maintain.

920. What is React and what problem does it solve?

Category: Core Concepts
React is a JavaScript library for building user interfaces by efficiently rendering components and managing UI state. It solves the problem of complex, dynamic UI updates by using a virtual DOM and declarative programming, improving performance and developer experience.

921. What is React and why is it used?

Category: Basics
React is used to build interactive and reusable UI components declaratively. It simplifies the development of complex user interfaces with efficient rendering, component-based architecture, and strong community support.

922. What is React Concurrent Mode and why is it important?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Concurrent Mode is an experimental set of features that allows React to prepare multiple UI updates simultaneously without blocking the main thread. It improves user experience by making apps more responsive and fluid, especially during heavy rendering or asynchronous tasks.

923. What is React Concurrent Mode?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React Concurrent Mode enables interruptible rendering and prioritization of updates, allowing React to pause, resume, or abandon rendering work to maintain a responsive UI. It represents a shift towards asynchronous rendering in React applications.

924. What is React Context and how do you use it?

Category: State Management
React Context is a feature that allows sharing state or functions globally across a component tree without passing props manually. It is used by creating a context with React.createContext and consuming it with useContext or Context.Consumer in child components.

925. What is React Context and how does it work?

Category: State Management
React Context works by providing a way to pass data through the component tree without manually threading props. A context provider wraps components and supplies the data, which consumers access via hooks or render props, enabling centralized and efficient state sharing.

926. What is React Context and when should you use it?

Category: State Management
React Context should be used to share data that needs to be accessed by many components at different nesting levels, such as theming, authentication status, or locale settings, to avoid prop drilling and simplify state management.

927. What is React Context API and how do you use it?

Category: State Management
React Context API is an interface for creating and using context in React applications. It involves creating a context object, wrapping components with a Provider to supply values, and consuming those values in child components using useContext or Context.Consumer.

928. What is React Context API and how is it used?

Category: State Management
The React Context API is used to share data globally across components without the need to pass props manually at every level. It is implemented by creating a context with React.createContext(), wrapping components with a Provider that holds the state or data, and accessing that data in nested components via the useContext hook or Context.Consumer.

929. What is React Context API and when should it be used?

Category: State Management
React Context API should be used when multiple components at various levels in the component tree need access to the same data or state, such as user authentication info, theme settings, or locale preferences. It helps avoid prop drilling and makes state sharing more efficient and organized.

930. What is React Context API and when should you use it?

Category: State Management
You should use React Context API when you want to provide global data or functionality to many components without passing props explicitly through every level. It's ideal for theming, user settings, or any data that needs to be accessed by many parts of the app, simplifying the state management.

931. What is React Fiber and how does it affect rendering?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber is a complete rewrite of React's core reconciliation algorithm aimed at improving rendering performance and responsiveness. It breaks rendering work into smaller units that can be paused and resumed, allowing React to prioritize important updates and keep the UI responsive.

932. What is React Fiber and how does it enhance rendering?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber enhances rendering by enabling incremental rendering and interruption of work, so React can prioritize urgent updates like user interactions while deferring less critical updates. This results in smoother animations and a more responsive interface.

933. What is React Fiber and how does it impact rendering?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber impacts rendering by allowing React to break rendering tasks into smaller chunks that can be processed asynchronously. This approach prevents blocking the main thread, improving app responsiveness especially in complex UI updates.

934. What is React Fiber and how does it improve performance?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber improves performance by enabling interruptible rendering, prioritizing tasks, and managing rendering work efficiently. This prevents long blocking operations, reduces UI jank, and enables React to deliver a smoother user experience.

935. What is React Fiber and how does it improve rendering?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber improves rendering by splitting work into units that can be paused and resumed, allowing React to handle high-priority updates immediately and postpone less critical rendering, enhancing overall UI responsiveness.

936. What is React Fiber and how does it work?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber works by creating a Fiber tree, a linked list of units of work representing the component tree. It processes these units incrementally, allowing React to pause, resume, or abort rendering tasks, prioritizing updates based on their urgency.

937. What is React Fiber and what improvements does it bring?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber brings improvements such as incremental rendering, interruptible work, prioritization of updates, better handling of animations and gestures, and improved concurrency support, all contributing to smoother and more responsive user interfaces.

938. What is React Fiber and why is it important?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber is important because it modernizes React's rendering architecture, enabling features like concurrent rendering, smoother updates, and improved handling of complex UIs, which are essential for building performant and scalable applications.

939. What is React Fiber architecture and why is it important?

Category: React Internals
The React Fiber architecture is a reimplementation of React's reconciliation algorithm designed for incremental rendering. It is important because it allows React to manage updates more flexibly and efficiently, supporting features like concurrency and prioritization.

940. What is React Fiber architecture?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber architecture is a tree of units of work (fibers) representing React elements and components. It enables React to perform incremental rendering, interruption, and prioritization of updates, improving the rendering process's efficiency.

941. What is React Fiber?

Category: React Internals
React Fiber is the name of React's new reconciliation engine that improves how React schedules and prioritizes rendering work, enabling features such as incremental rendering and concurrent updates to enhance app responsiveness.

942. What is React Hook Form and why use it?

Category: Forms
React Hook Form is a library that simplifies form validation and management by using React hooks. It is used because it reduces boilerplate code, improves performance by minimizing re-renders, and integrates easily with UI frameworks.

943. What is React Hook Form?

Category: Forms
React Hook Form is a performant, flexible library for managing form state and validation in React applications using hooks, designed to minimize re-renders and simplify form handling.

944. What is React Navigation and how is it used?

Category: React Native
React Navigation is a popular routing and navigation library for React Native applications. It allows developers to manage the navigation between different screens and provides a variety of navigators like stack, tab, and drawer navigators. It is used by setting up navigators and linking screens, enabling smooth transitions and deep linking within mobile apps.

945. What is React Profiler and how do you use it?

Category: Debugging
React Profiler is a tool built into React Developer Tools that helps measure the performance of React components by recording render times and identifying unnecessary renders. Developers use it to analyze and optimize component rendering behavior by profiling interactions and understanding how components update over time.

946. What is React Profiler?

Category: Debugging
React Profiler is a performance measuring tool for React applications that tracks how frequently components render and how long they take, assisting developers in identifying bottlenecks and improving rendering efficiency.

947. What is React Profiler’s flamegraph and how do you use it?

Category: Debugging
The flamegraph in React Profiler visualizes component render durations and their hierarchical relationships during a profiling session. Developers use it to quickly identify which components consume the most rendering time and optimize accordingly.

948. What is React Query and how does it help with data fetching?

Category: State Management
React Query is a data-fetching library for React that simplifies managing server state. It provides features like caching, automatic refetching, background updates, and synchronization, reducing boilerplate and improving the user experience by keeping UI data fresh and responsive.

949. What is React Router and how do you implement routing?

Category: Routing
React Router is a declarative routing library for React that enables navigation between different components or views. Routing is implemented by defining Route components inside a Router, which listens to URL changes and renders the matching components accordingly.

950. What is React Router and how is it used?

Category: Routing
React Router is used to handle client-side navigation in React applications, allowing developers to create single-page apps with multiple views. It is used by wrapping the app with a Router and defining routes to components that correspond to URL paths.

951. What is React Router and why is it used?

Category: Routing
React Router is used to manage navigation and routing within React applications, enabling developers to build dynamic, multi-page-like experiences in single-page apps. It handles URL changes, route matching, and rendering of components based on the current path.

952. What is React StrictMode and what benefits does it offer?

Category: Advanced Features
React StrictMode is a development tool that helps identify potential problems in an application by activating additional checks and warnings for its descendants. Benefits include detecting deprecated APIs, unexpected side effects, and other common issues early in development.

953. What is React StrictMode and what benefits does it provide?

Category: Advanced Features
React StrictMode provides enhanced runtime checks and warnings for React applications during development. It helps catch unsafe lifecycles, legacy API usage, and other side effects that could cause bugs, ultimately improving code quality.

954. What is React StrictMode and what is its purpose?

Category: Advanced Features
The purpose of React StrictMode is to highlight potential issues in an app by running extra checks and warnings without affecting the production build. It encourages best practices and prepares codebases for future React features.

955. What is React StrictMode and why is it useful?

Category: Advanced Features
React StrictMode is useful because it helps developers catch issues early by activating warnings about deprecated APIs, unsafe lifecycles, and side effects, improving code maintainability and readiness for future React releases.

956. What is React StrictMode and why should you use it?

Category: Advanced Features
You should use React StrictMode during development to identify unsafe practices and legacy code patterns early. It helps improve application robustness and future-proofs codebases by enforcing React best practices.

957. What is React StrictMode and why use it?

Category: Advanced Features
React StrictMode is used to detect potential problems in an app by enabling extra checks and warnings, making it easier to write safe and future-proof React code.

Category: Advanced Features
React StrictMode is a helper component in React that activates additional checks and warnings for its descendants during development to identify potential problems without affecting production behavior.

959. What is React Suspense and how do you use it?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense is a component that lets you display a fallback UI while waiting for some code or data to load. It is used by wrapping lazy-loaded components or asynchronous data-fetching components, improving user experience by showing loading states.

960. What is React Suspense and how does it help with data fetching?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense helps with data fetching by allowing components to "wait" for asynchronous data before rendering, showing fallback content in the meantime. This leads to simpler, more declarative data fetching and smoother loading states.

961. What is React Suspense and how does it improve loading?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense improves loading by showing fallback UI during lazy loading or data fetching, preventing blank screens and making the loading experience more user-friendly and responsive.

962. What is React Suspense and how does it relate to data fetching?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense relates to data fetching by allowing components to suspend rendering until the required data is available, integrating asynchronous data requests smoothly with the component tree.

963. What is React Suspense and how does it relate to lazy loading?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense is closely tied to lazy loading by providing a way to display fallback content while components or code chunks load asynchronously, enabling efficient code splitting and improved app performance.

964. What is React Suspense and how does it work with data fetching?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense works with data fetching by allowing components to "pause" rendering until the required data is available. When a component wrapped in Suspense encounters a data fetch that isn't yet complete, it throws a promise that React catches. During this pause, Suspense shows a fallback UI such as a loader. Once the data arrives, React resumes rendering the component. This approach simplifies asynchronous data management by integrating loading states declaratively within the component tree.

965. What is React Suspense and how does it work?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense is a component that lets you declaratively handle asynchronous operations in React by specifying fallback content to display while waiting. It works by catching thrown promises during rendering and showing a fallback UI until those promises resolve, enabling smooth loading experiences for lazy-loaded components or data.

966. What is React Suspense and how is it used for data fetching?

Category: Code Splitting
For data fetching, React Suspense is used by integrating with libraries or custom hooks that throw promises when data is loading. Suspense detects these promises and shows fallback content until data fetching completes, providing a declarative way to manage loading states without manually tracking them.

967. What is React Suspense and how is it used?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense is used to wrap components that rely on asynchronous operations, such as lazy-loaded code or data fetching. It allows developers to specify fallback UI to display during the wait period, improving UX by preventing abrupt or blank screens.

968. What is React Suspense and how is it useful?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense is useful because it simplifies handling asynchronous tasks in React by providing a built-in mechanism to display loading states declaratively. It reduces boilerplate code and enhances user experience through smooth transitions during lazy loading or data fetching.

969. What is React Suspense?

Category: Code Splitting
React Suspense is a React component that allows you to defer rendering of parts of the UI until some condition, typically data loading or code splitting, is met. It provides a fallback UI during the waiting period to enhance the user experience.

970. What is React SuspenseList and how do you use it?

Category: Code Splitting
React SuspenseList is a component that coordinates the reveal order of multiple Suspense components on the screen. It helps manage the loading sequence of multiple async parts, improving perceived performance by controlling whether components show up together or sequentially.

971. What is React SuspenseList and how does it help coordinate multiple Suspense components?

Category: Code Splitting
React SuspenseList coordinates multiple Suspense boundaries by controlling the order and timing with which fallback UIs are hidden and content is revealed. This coordination prevents jarring UI changes and creates a smoother loading experience when multiple async components are involved.

972. What is React SuspenseList and how does it work?

Category: Code Splitting
React SuspenseList works by wrapping multiple Suspense components and managing their reveal strategy, which can be in a "together", "forwards", or "backwards" order. It waits for components to be ready according to the chosen strategy before showing content, enhancing user experience.

973. What is React SuspenseList and how is it used?

Category: Code Splitting
React SuspenseList is used by wrapping a set of Suspense components within it and specifying the reveal order. This helps orchestrate complex loading sequences, especially when multiple lazy-loaded components need to render in a controlled way.

974. What is React SuspenseList?

React SuspenseList is a React component designed to coordinate multiple Suspense components by managing the order and timing of their content reveal during asynchronous loading processes.

975. What is React Testing Library?

976. What is React.Fragment used for?

977. What is React.lazy and how do you implement it?

React.lazy is a function that enables lazy loading of components by dynamically importing them. You implement it by wrapping the dynamic import with React.lazy and then rendering the lazy component inside a Suspense boundary to handle loading states.

978. What is React.lazy and how does it help with performance?

Category: Code Splitting
React.lazy helps improve performance by splitting the code at component level, loading components only when they are needed. This reduces the initial bundle size and speeds up the app's startup time.

979. What is React.lazy and how is it used?

React.lazy is used to defer loading of components until they are rendered for the first time. Combined with Suspense, it provides a smooth user experience by showing fallback content during component loading.

980. What is React.memo and how does it help with performance?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo is a higher-order component that optimizes functional components by memoizing their rendered output. It prevents unnecessary re-renders by comparing the current and previous props, and re-rendering only when they change. This is useful for improving performance in components that render frequently with the same props.

981. What is React.memo and how does it optimize functional components?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo optimizes functional components by wrapping them in a memoization function that performs a shallow comparison of props. If props haven't changed, the component is skipped during reconciliation, reducing rendering overhead and improving performance, especially in large or complex UIs.

982. What is React.memo and how does it work?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo works by wrapping a functional component and memoizing the result. When the component receives props, React compares them with the previous ones using a shallow equality check. If the props are the same, it reuses the last rendered result instead of re-rendering the component.

983. What is React.StrictMode and its benefits?

It does not affect the production build but improves code quality and maintainability during development.

984. What is React.StrictMode and what are its benefits?

The benefits of React.StrictMode include highlighting unsafe lifecycle methods, identifying legacy string refs, detecting unexpected side effects, and double-invoking certain functions to simulate mounting and unmounting. These checks help developers write more resilient and future-proof code.

Developers use React.StrictMode to catch common bugs and enforce best practices in React applications. It offers proactive feedback by simulating mount/unmount cycles and logging warnings for deprecated features, helping maintain clean and future-proof code.

994. What is React’s concurrent mode and how does it improve UI responsiveness?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React’s Concurrent Mode improves UI responsiveness by allowing React to interrupt rendering work to focus on higher-priority updates. It helps avoid blocking the main thread and enables smoother experiences by prioritizing urgent tasks like user input over less critical rendering.

995. What is React’s Concurrent Mode?

Category: Advanced Concepts
React’s Concurrent Mode is an experimental set of features that allow React to prepare multiple versions of the UI simultaneously. It helps make applications more responsive by splitting rendering into interruptible chunks and letting React prioritize important updates.

996. What is React’s render props pattern and how is it used?

The render props pattern in React is a technique where a component’s child is a function that receives props and returns React elements. It’s used to share logic across components by dynamically rendering UI based on shared behavior.

999. What is Recoil and how is it used in React?
