Recoil is a state management library for React that provides a more granular and flexible way to manage shared state. It uses atoms and selectors for state composition and makes it easier to manage complex and asynchronous state in large applications.

1000. What is reconciliation algorithm in React?

Category: Core Concepts
The reconciliation algorithm in React determines how to efficiently update the DOM when state or props change. It compares the new virtual DOM with the previous one and applies minimal changes to the real DOM to ensure optimal performance.

1001. What is reconciliation and how does React use it?

Category: Core Concepts
Reconciliation is the process React uses to update the UI by comparing the previous virtual DOM with the new one. React identifies changes and applies updates efficiently to ensure the UI stays in sync with application state.

1002. What is reconciliation in React and how does it work?

Category: Core Concepts
In React, reconciliation is the algorithm that updates the DOM by comparing the new and old virtual DOM trees. It identifies which elements have changed, been added, or removed, then updates only those parts of the DOM to optimize rendering.

1003. What is reconciliation in React and why is it important?

Category: Core Concepts
Reconciliation in React is important because it enables efficient UI updates. By calculating the minimal number of DOM operations needed to reflect UI changes, it improves performance and responsiveness in React applications.

1004. What is reconciliation in React?

Category: Core Concepts
Reconciliation in React is the internal process of determining how to update the DOM when a component's state or props change. It compares the current and previous virtual DOMs to apply only the necessary changes to the real DOM.

1005. What is Redux and why is it used with React?

Category: State Management
Redux is a predictable state container for JavaScript apps, commonly used with React to manage global state. It helps centralize application state, making it easier to debug, test, and manage data flow across components through actions, reducers, and a single store.

1006. What is Redux and why is it used?

Category: State Management
Redux is used to manage application state in a predictable way. It simplifies the complexity of state changes by enforcing a unidirectional data flow and enabling time-travel debugging, making large applications easier to maintain and scale.

1007. What is Redux Saga and when would you use it?

Category: State Management
Redux Saga is a middleware library that handles side effects like asynchronous data fetching in Redux apps using generator functions. You would use it when your app has complex async logic, such as chaining API calls, retries, or cancellation.

1008. What is Redux Thunk and how does it work?

Category: State Management
Redux Thunk is a middleware that allows you to write action creators that return a function instead of an action. This function can dispatch other actions and perform asynchronous logic, such as fetching data from an API before updating the state.

1009. What is Redux Toolkit and how does it simplify Redux?

Category: State Management
Redux Toolkit is the official, recommended way to write Redux logic. It simplifies Redux by providing utilities like createSlice, configureStore, and createAsyncThunk, reducing boilerplate and making code more maintainable.

1010. What is server-side rendering (SSR) in React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Server-side rendering (SSR) in React is the process of rendering React components on the server and sending the fully rendered HTML to the client. It improves initial load time, SEO, and performance for users with slower devices or networks.

1011. What is shouldComponentUpdate and how does it help performance?

Category: Lifecycle
shouldComponentUpdate is a lifecycle method in class components that lets you control whether a component should re-render after receiving new props or state. It helps optimize performance by preventing unnecessary renders.

1012. What is snapshot testing in React?

Category: Testing
Snapshot testing captures the rendered output of a React component and saves it as a snapshot file. On subsequent test runs, the current output is compared to the saved snapshot to detect unexpected changes in the UI.

1013. What is SSG (Static Site Generation)?

Category: SSR / SSG
Static Site Generation (SSG) is a pre-rendering method where HTML pages are generated at build time. It's ideal for pages that don’t change often, offering fast load times, improved SEO, and better performance compared to client-side rendering.

1014. What is SSR (Server-Side Rendering)?

Category: SSR / SSG
Server-Side Rendering (SSR) is a technique where pages are rendered on the server at request time, then sent to the client. In React, SSR helps with performance and SEO by delivering fully rendered HTML instead of waiting for JavaScript to load.

1015. What is state in React?

Category: State
State in React refers to a built-in object that stores property values that belong to a component. When the state changes, the component re-renders to reflect the new data. It's commonly used for dynamic content and interactivity.

1016. What is state lifting in React?

Category: State Management
State lifting is the process of moving state up to a common ancestor of two or more components that need to share or coordinate state. This allows sibling components to access or modify shared data through props.

1017. What is static site generation (SSG) in React?

Category: Advanced
Static Site Generation (SSG) in React, especially with Next.js, involves pre-rendering pages at build time. This results in faster performance and better SEO because users receive a fully rendered HTML page without client-side delays.

1018. What is Suspense and how does it work with lazy components?

Category: Performance
Suspense is a React component that allows you to show fallback content (like a loading spinner) while waiting for a lazy-loaded component to finish loading. It works in combination with React.lazy for efficient code splitting.

1019. What is Suspense and how is it used?

Category: Performance
Suspense is used to wrap components that are loaded asynchronously, showing a fallback UI during the load. It's especially useful for lazy loading components and handling asynchronous data with frameworks like React Query or Relay.

1020. What is Suspense component in React?

Category: Code Splitting
The Suspense component in React enables you to delay rendering part of your UI until a condition is met (like loading a component or fetching data). It provides a fallback UI during this waiting period.

1021. What is Suspense in React?

Category: Code Splitting
Suspense is a React feature designed for managing asynchronous rendering. It allows you to "suspend" rendering a component tree until some condition is met, like a component finishing loading or data being fetched.

1022. What is SWR and how is it used with React?

Category: State Management
SWR (stale-while-revalidate) is a data fetching library for React that keeps data fresh by revalidating it in the background. It provides built-in caching, automatic revalidation, and focus tracking for efficient and responsive UI updates.

1023. What is the App Router in Next.js 13+?

Category: SSR / SSG
The App Router in Next.js 13+ is a new routing system that introduces file-based routing in the app directory. It supports layouts, server components, streaming, and enhanced data fetching strategies, providing a more modern and flexible architecture for building apps.

1024. What is the Content Security Policy (CSP) and how does it apply to React?

Category: Security
Content Security Policy (CSP) is a browser security feature that helps prevent cross-site scripting (XSS), clickjacking, and other code injection attacks. In React applications, CSP headers can restrict the sources from which scripts, styles, and other resources can be loaded. Implementing CSP properly often requires avoiding inline scripts and styles, which aligns well with React's component-based structure and JSX syntax.

1025. What is the Context API and how does it help with state management?

Category: State Management
The Context API in React provides a way to share state across the component tree without passing props manually at every level. It helps eliminate prop drilling by allowing components to consume values from a shared context, which improves maintainability and scalability in medium to large applications.

1026. What is the Context API and how does it improve state management?

Category: State Management
The Context API improves state management by centralizing shared state and reducing the need for redundant prop-passing through component hierarchies. It makes global state more accessible and minimizes tightly coupled components, resulting in cleaner and more maintainable code.

1027. What is the Context API and when should it be used?

Category: State Management
The Context API should be used when multiple components at different levels need access to the same data or logic, such as theme settings, authentication status, or user preferences. It is most effective for relatively static or infrequently updated shared state.

1028. What is the context API in React?

Category: State Management
The Context API in React is a mechanism that allows data to be passed through the component tree without manually passing props at every level. It uses a Provider to supply data and a Consumer or useContext hook to access it in child components.

1029. What is the dependency array in useEffect used for?

Category: Hooks
The dependency array in useEffect determines when the effect should re-run. If a variable in the array changes between renders, the effect will execute again. An empty array runs the effect only once after the initial render, mimicking componentDidMount.

1030. What is the dependency array in useEffect?

Category: Hooks
The dependency array in useEffect is a second argument that lists values React should watch for changes. When any dependency changes, the effect runs again. It's essential for optimizing performance and ensuring correct behavior.

1031. What is the difference between <Link> and <NavLink>?

Category: Routing
Both <Link> and <NavLink> are used for navigation in React Router, but <NavLink> provides additional styling capabilities. It automatically applies an "active" class or style when the link matches the current URL, making it useful for navigation menus.

1032. What is the difference between a button type submit and button type button in forms?

Category: Forms
A button with type="submit" submits the form it belongs to, triggering the onSubmit handler. A button with type="button" does not submit the form and is typically used for other actions like toggling UI or handling events without form submission.

1033. What is the difference between a class component and a functional component?

Category: Basics
A class component is an ES6 class that extends React.Component and has a render method, while a functional component is a plain JavaScript function that returns JSX. Functional components can use hooks for state and lifecycle logic, making them more concise and preferred in modern React development.

1034. What is the difference between a controlled and uncontrolled component?

Category: Forms
Controlled components are form inputs that derive their values from React state and are updated via onChange handlers. Uncontrolled components manage their own state internally and use refs to access values. Controlled components provide better control and predictability in form handling.

1035. What is the difference between BrowserRouter and HashRouter?

Category: Routing
BrowserRouter uses the HTML5 history API for clean URLs and better SEO, while HashRouter uses the URL hash (#) to keep track of routes, making it more suitable for older browsers or static file servers where direct server-side route handling isn't possible.

1036. What is the difference between class and functional components?

Category: Components
Class components are built using ES6 classes and can manage state and lifecycle methods. Functional components are simpler functions that became more powerful with hooks, allowing them to manage state, side effects, and context without needing a class structure.

1037. What is the difference between client state and server state?

Category: State Management
Client state is data managed within the browser (e.g., UI state, form input), while server state refers to remote data fetched from an external source (e.g., an API). Server state is asynchronous, may become stale, and often requires caching or synchronization strategies.

1038. What is the difference between client-side and server-side rendering in React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Client-side rendering (CSR) loads a minimal HTML page and renders content using JavaScript in the browser. Server-side rendering (SSR) pre-renders HTML on the server, improving SEO and perceived load speed. CSR is better for dynamic, highly interactive apps, while SSR is ideal for content-heavy pages.

1039. What is the difference between client-side and server-side rendering?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Client-side rendering happens entirely in the browser after the JavaScript bundle loads, while server-side rendering sends a fully rendered HTML page from the server to the browser. SSR offers faster initial loads and better SEO, but CSR allows more dynamic interactions post-load.

1040. What is the difference between client-side and server-side routing?

Category: Routing
Client-side routing handles navigation within the browser using JavaScript without reloading the page. Server-side routing sends a new page from the server on each navigation. React uses client-side routing for seamless user experiences and faster transitions between views.

1041. What is the difference between client-side rendering and server-side rendering in React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
In React, client-side rendering loads a blank page and uses JavaScript to mount the app in the browser. Server-side rendering pre-renders the React app to HTML on the server and sends it to the browser. SSR improves SEO and first contentful paint, while CSR offers faster interactivity once loaded.

1042. What is the difference between client-side rendering and server-side rendering?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Client-side rendering renders the content in the browser using JavaScript, leading to a delay before meaningful content is visible. Server-side rendering prepares the HTML on the server before sending it to the browser, offering better performance for first-time visits and improved SEO.

1043. What is the difference between componentDidMount and componentWillUnmount?

Category: Lifecycle
componentDidMount is a React lifecycle method invoked once immediately after a component is mounted (inserted into the DOM). It’s used to perform initialization tasks like fetching data or setting up subscriptions. componentWillUnmount is called right before a component is removed from the DOM, and is primarily used for cleanup, such as cancelling timers or removing event listeners to prevent memory leaks.

1044. What is the difference between controlled and uncontrolled components in forms?

Category: Forms
Controlled components are form elements where React controls their value through state, updating on every user input via event handlers, ensuring a single source of truth. Uncontrolled components manage their own state internally and use refs to access form values when needed. Controlled forms offer better validation and predictable behavior, while uncontrolled forms require less code but less control.

1045. What is the difference between controlled and uncontrolled components in React?

Category: Forms
The difference lies in state management: controlled components rely on React state to control their input values, with updates handled via callbacks. Uncontrolled components allow the DOM to manage their own state, and React accesses their values using refs. Controlled components are preferred for complex forms needing validation, while uncontrolled can be simpler for quick, less interactive forms.

1046. What is the difference between controlled and uncontrolled components?

Category: Forms
Controlled components are fully managed by React, where form values are bound to state and update through React events. Uncontrolled components maintain their own internal state, with React reading values from the DOM using refs. Controlled components enable real-time validation and consistent data flow; uncontrolled components simplify initial setup but lack fine control.

1047. What is the difference between controlled and uncontrolled inputs?

Category: Forms
Controlled inputs receive their value from React state and require an onChange handler to update that state, creating a clear data flow. Uncontrolled inputs maintain their own state internally, and their current value can be accessed via a ref when necessary. Controlled inputs give full control for validation and conditional rendering, while uncontrolled are simpler to implement.

1048. What is the difference between controlled components and uncontrolled components in forms?

Category: Forms
Controlled components tie the form input’s value directly to React state, updating it through event handlers, which offers centralized control and easy validation. Uncontrolled components allow the browser to manage the input state internally, with React accessing the value via refs when needed. Controlled components are preferred for dynamic, complex forms; uncontrolled can be suitable for simpler use cases.

1049. What is the difference between development and production builds in React?

Category: Tooling
Development builds include helpful warnings, error messages, and debugging tools that assist developers during development but increase bundle size and reduce performance. Production builds are optimized and minified versions of the app with these debugging features removed, resulting in faster load times and better performance for end users.

1050. What is the difference between Fast Refresh and Hot Reloading?

Category: React Native
Hot Reloading reloads only the changed modules without losing the app state, but it can sometimes cause inconsistencies or stale data. Fast Refresh is an improved version combining the benefits of hot reloading and live reloading, providing reliable and instant feedback with state preservation, making it the preferred approach in React Native development.

1051. What is the difference between HTML and React event handling?

Category: Events
React event handling differs from standard HTML events in several key ways. In React, event names use camelCase (e.g., onClick), whereas HTML uses lowercase (e.g., onclick). React events are passed as functions rather than strings. Additionally, React uses a synthetic event system that normalizes events across different browsers, improving consistency and performance. This abstraction also enables features like event delegation under the hood.

1052. What is the difference between mapStateToProps and useSelector?

Category: State Management
mapStateToProps is a function used with the Redux connect higher-order component in class-based components to map Redux state to props. useSelector is a React Redux hook used in functional components to directly access the Redux store’s state. Hooks provide a more concise and modern way to access state without needing to wrap components, making useSelector more aligned with functional component patterns.

1053. What is the difference between props and state in React?

Category: Core Concepts
Props are inputs passed from a parent component to a child, immutable within the child component and used to configure or customize it. State is managed within a component and can change over time, representing dynamic data or UI status. While props enable communication and data flow from parents to children, state controls component behavior internally.

1054. What is the difference between PureComponent and Component?

Category: Performance
PureComponent automatically implements a shallow prop and state comparison in shouldComponentUpdate to prevent unnecessary re-renders, optimizing performance for components with simple data. Component does not implement this optimization, so it re-renders by default whenever its parent renders or its state changes, regardless of whether props have actually changed.

1055. What is the difference between PureComponent and React.memo?

Category: Performance Optimization
PureComponent is a base class for class components that implements a shallow comparison for props and state to avoid unnecessary updates. React.memo is a higher-order component for functional components that performs a similar shallow comparison on props to optimize rendering. Essentially, PureComponent applies to class components, while React.memo enables this optimization for functional components.

1056. What is the difference between query and mutation in GraphQL?

Category: Data Fetching
In GraphQL, a query is used to request and fetch data from the server without side effects, while a mutation is used to modify or change data on the server (such as creating, updating, or deleting). Queries are read-only operations, whereas mutations can cause changes and typically return the modified data after completion.

1057. What is the difference between React class and functional components?

Category: Components
Class components extend React.Component and manage state and lifecycle methods using class syntax, while functional components are simple JavaScript functions that accept props and return JSX. With the introduction of hooks, functional components can now manage state and side effects, making them more concise and preferred for most new React development.

1058. What is the difference between React class components and functional components?

Category: Components
Class components rely on ES6 classes and lifecycle methods, requiring this context, whereas functional components are simpler functions that use hooks for managing state and side effects. Functional components are generally easier to read, test, and maintain, and have become the standard with React’s hooks API.

1059. What is the difference between React components and elements?

Category: Core Concepts
React components are reusable, self-contained pieces of UI logic, implemented as functions or classes. React elements are the plain objects describing what to render on the screen and are the building blocks created by components. Elements are immutable and lightweight representations of UI, while components define how those elements are constructed and behave.

1060. What is the difference between React Context and MobX?

Category: State Management
React Context provides a built-in way to pass data through the component tree without prop drilling, primarily useful for global or theme-like state. MobX is a standalone state management library offering observable state with reactive programming capabilities, enabling automatic UI updates when data changes. MobX supports more advanced and fine-grained state management compared to the relatively simple React Context API.

1061. What is the difference between React Context and Redux for state management?

Category: State Management
React Context is a built-in API designed for sharing global data like themes or user authentication across the component tree without prop drilling. It’s best suited for simple or moderate state sharing needs. Redux, on the other hand, is a full-featured state management library that centralizes application state in a predictable store, supports middleware for side effects, and enforces strict unidirectional data flow. Redux is more appropriate for complex applications requiring advanced state logic, debugging tools, and scalable architecture.

1062. What is the difference between React context and Redux?

Category: State Management
React Context is a simple API for passing data through the component tree without props, primarily used for lightweight or static state sharing. Redux is a comprehensive state management solution with a centralized store, actions, reducers, and middleware support, tailored for complex and large-scale state management scenarios. Redux provides better tooling for debugging and managing complex state transitions, whereas Context is more straightforward but less feature-rich.

1063. What is the difference between React Context API and Redux?

Category: State Management
React Context API enables sharing of data globally within a React app without prop drilling and is part of React’s core API. It is ideal for simple state needs like themes or user settings. Redux is an external library providing a predictable state container with a centralized store, actions, reducers, and middleware for managing side effects. Redux excels in handling complex state logic, asynchronous operations, and offers extensive developer tools for debugging.

1064. What is the difference between React elements and components?

Category: Core Concepts
React elements are plain JavaScript objects describing what should be rendered on the screen (the virtual DOM representation), whereas components are functions or classes that return React elements. Components define the logic and structure of UI, while elements are the output of components used by React to build and update the actual DOM.

1065. What is the difference between React Hooks and lifecycle methods?

Category: Hooks
Lifecycle methods are class component-specific methods like componentDidMount or componentDidUpdate that manage component lifecycle events. React Hooks are functions like useEffect or useState that enable functional components to manage state and side effects, effectively replacing lifecycle methods with a more concise and composable API suitable for functional components.

1066. What is the difference between React Router v5 and v6?

Category: Routing
React Router v6 introduces several improvements over v5 including a simplified and more declarative API, nested routing support with Outlet, automatic route ranking for better matching, and removal of deprecated APIs. V6 is faster, has better TypeScript support, and focuses on route nesting and route matching improvements, providing a more intuitive developer experience.

1067. What is the difference between React.Fragment and <> syntax?

Category: Components
React.Fragment and the shorthand syntax <> both let you group multiple elements without adding extra nodes to the DOM. The main difference is that React.Fragment can accept keys and attributes, which is useful when rendering lists of fragments, whereas the shorthand syntax cannot.

1068. What is the difference between React.Fragment and a div?

Category: Components
React.Fragment groups multiple elements without creating an extra DOM node, keeping the DOM tree clean. A div adds an actual HTML element to the DOM, which can affect styling and layout. Fragments are preferred when you want to return multiple children without unnecessary wrappers.

1069. What is the difference between React.lazy and dynamic imports?

Category: Code Splitting
React.lazy is a React-specific API that uses dynamic imports under the hood to enable code-splitting of components, making lazy loading easy in React apps. Dynamic imports are a JavaScript language feature that returns a promise to load modules asynchronously, usable outside React as well. React.lazy wraps dynamic imports to integrate seamlessly with React's rendering and Suspense.

1070. What is the difference between React.lazy and React.Suspense?

Category: Code Splitting
React.lazy is used to lazily load components via dynamic imports, splitting bundles for better performance. React.Suspense is a component that wraps lazy-loaded components and provides a fallback UI (like a loader) while the lazy component is being loaded asynchronously. They work together to enable smooth code-splitting and loading states.

1071. What is the difference between React.memo and PureComponent?

Category: Performance Optimization
PureComponent is a base class for class components that implements a shallow prop and state comparison to prevent unnecessary re-renders. React.memo is a higher-order component for functional components that provides similar shallow prop comparison to optimize rendering. Essentially, PureComponent applies to classes, while React.memo applies to functions.

1072. What is the difference between React.memo and useMemo?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.memo is a higher-order component used to memoize entire functional components, preventing unnecessary re-renders when props haven’t changed. useMemo is a hook used inside functional components to memoize expensive computations or values between renders. React.memo optimizes component rendering, while useMemo optimizes calculations within a component.

1073. What is the difference between React.PureComponent and React.Component?

Category: Performance Optimization
React.Component is the base class for all class components and does not implement any optimizations by default. React.PureComponent extends React.Component and implements a shallow comparison of props and state inside shouldComponentUpdate, preventing re-renders when there are no changes in props or state. This shallow comparison can improve performance by avoiding unnecessary updates, but it only works for simple, shallow data structures.

1074. What is the difference between React.StrictMode and error boundaries?

Category: Error Handling
React.StrictMode is a development tool that activates additional checks and warnings for potential problems in an application but does not catch runtime errors. It helps identify unsafe lifecycles, deprecated APIs, and side effects. Error boundaries, by contrast, are components that catch JavaScript errors during rendering, lifecycle methods, and constructors, allowing graceful error handling and UI fallback instead of crashing the entire app.

1075. What is the difference between React’s controlled components and form libraries like Formik?

Category: Forms
Controlled components in React directly manage form inputs' state via React’s state and props, requiring manual handling of every input change and validation. Form libraries like Formik abstract much of this complexity by providing a higher-level API for form state management, validation, error handling, and submission, making it easier to build robust forms with less boilerplate.

1076. What is the difference between React’s synthetic events and native events?

Category: Event Handling
React’s synthetic events are a cross-browser wrapper around the browser’s native events, providing consistent behavior across different browsers. They are pooled for performance, meaning the event object is reused. Native events are the browser's standard DOM events, which vary slightly across browsers and are not pooled, so they have different lifecycle and performance characteristics compared to synthetic events.

1077. What is the difference between React’s useEffect and useImperativeHandle hooks?

Category: Hooks
useEffect is a hook for managing side effects in functional components, running code after rendering like data fetching or subscriptions. useImperativeHandle is a less commonly used hook that customizes the instance value exposed to parent components when using ref, enabling controlled exposure of imperative methods from child components.

1078. What is the difference between React’s useState and useReducer hooks?

Category: Hooks
useState provides simple state management with a setter function, best suited for local and straightforward state updates. useReducer manages more complex state logic by using a reducer function to determine state changes based on dispatched actions, similar to Redux patterns, making it suitable for managing intricate or interrelated state updates.

1079. What is the difference between React's concurrent mode and legacy mode?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Legacy mode is React’s traditional rendering mode, which blocks the main thread during rendering, causing potential UI freezes on heavy updates. Concurrent mode is an experimental React feature that enables interruptible rendering, allowing React to pause, resume, or abandon work to keep the UI responsive. It improves user experience by allowing React to prioritize urgent updates and avoid blocking user interactions.

1080. What is the difference between Redux and Context API?

Category: State Management
Redux is a standalone, powerful state management library with a centralized store, predictable state changes via reducers, and support for middleware, making it ideal for large, complex applications. React Context API is built into React and is primarily for passing data through the component tree without props drilling; it's simpler but not optimized for complex state logic or frequent updates.

1081. What is the difference between Redux and MobX?

Category: State Management
Redux enforces a strict unidirectional data flow with immutable state and pure reducer functions, making state predictable and easier to debug. MobX uses observable state and reactions to automatically track dependencies, allowing more direct and mutable state changes. Redux emphasizes explicitness and functional programming, while MobX offers a more flexible and reactive approach.

1082. What is the difference between REST and GraphQL APIs?

Category: Data Fetching
REST APIs structure data around resources with fixed endpoints returning predetermined data shapes, often leading to over-fetching or under-fetching. GraphQL APIs allow clients to request exactly the data they need through a flexible query language, reducing over-fetching and enabling single-endpoint requests. GraphQL also supports real-time updates via subscriptions and offers strong typing.

1083. What is the difference between server-side rendering (SSR) and static site generation (SSG) in React?

Category: Advanced Concepts
SSR generates the HTML of a page on each request on the server, enabling dynamic content tailored per request but increasing server load. SSG pre-builds HTML pages at build time, serving static files quickly and efficiently, ideal for content that doesn't change often. SSR supports dynamic, personalized content better, while SSG offers faster delivery and scalability for static content.

1084. What is the difference between server-side rendering and static site generation?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Server-side rendering generates HTML dynamically on every user request, providing up-to-date content but with higher server costs. Static site generation builds HTML at build time, serving static files which load faster but cannot reflect real-time changes without rebuilding. SSR suits dynamic applications, while SSG is best for static or less frequently updated content.

1085. What is the difference between shallow and deep rendering in React testing?

Category: Testing
Shallow rendering tests a React component in isolation without rendering its child components, allowing focused unit tests and faster execution. Deep rendering renders the full component tree including all children, useful for integration or end-to-end tests that require interaction with nested components but can be slower and more complex.

1086. What is the difference between shallow and deep rendering in testing?

Category: Testing
Shallow rendering tests a React component in isolation by rendering only the component itself without rendering any of its child components. This allows developers to focus on the component’s behavior without interference from its children, making tests faster and more targeted. Deep rendering, also known as full rendering, renders the entire component tree including all child components, which is useful for integration tests that require interaction between components but can be slower and more complex to maintain.

1087. What is the difference between shallow rendering and full rendering?

Category: Testing
Shallow rendering renders only the top-level component without rendering its children, enabling focused unit tests and faster execution by isolating the component. Full rendering, on the other hand, renders the entire component tree including all nested children, which is better suited for integration or end-to-end tests that verify interactions between components and the overall UI behavior.

1088. What is the difference between shouldComponentUpdate and React.memo?

Category: Performance Optimization
shouldComponentUpdate is a lifecycle method used in class components to manually decide whether a component should re-render by returning true or false. React.memo is a higher-order component for functional components that memoizes the rendered output and skips re-rendering if the props have not changed, typically using a shallow comparison. Both help optimize performance by avoiding unnecessary renders, but React.memo is designed for functional components and is simpler to use.

1089. What is the difference between SSR and client-side rendering?

Category: Advanced
Server-Side Rendering (SSR) generates the HTML of a page on the server on each request before sending it to the client, allowing faster initial page load and better SEO. Client-Side Rendering (CSR) generates HTML in the browser using JavaScript after the initial page load, which can delay content visibility but allows richer interactivity. SSR improves performance and SEO for the first load, while CSR enables more dynamic and interactive single-page applications.

1090. What is the difference between state and context?

Category: State Management
State is local data managed within a component that triggers re-rendering when changed, used for component-specific behavior or UI. Context provides a way to share data globally across many components without passing props explicitly through every level, making it suitable for global themes, user authentication, or settings. State manages component-level data, while context manages app-wide shared data.

1091. What is the difference between state and props?

Category: State vs Props
State is mutable data managed within a component that can change over time and trigger UI updates. Props are immutable data passed from parent components to child components to configure or customize them. State represents internal data controlled by the component itself, whereas props are external inputs provided by other components.

1092. What is the difference between strict mode and normal mode in React?

Category: Advanced
Strict Mode in React is a development-only feature that activates additional checks and warnings to help identify potential problems like unsafe lifecycle methods, deprecated APIs, or side effects. Normal mode is the standard runtime without these extra checks. Strict Mode does not affect production builds but enhances code quality during development.

1093. What is the difference between touched and dirty in form libraries?

Category: Forms
In form libraries, touched means a form field has received focus and then lost it at least once, indicating user interaction. dirty means the value of a form field has been changed from its initial value. Touched tracks user engagement, while dirty tracks value modifications.

1094. What is the difference between useCallback and useMemo hooks?

Category: Hooks
useCallback memoizes a callback function, returning the same function instance unless its dependencies change, useful for preventing unnecessary re-renders when passing callbacks to child components. useMemo memoizes the result of a computation, recomputing only when dependencies change, optimizing expensive calculations. Both help optimize performance but serve different purposes—memoizing functions vs. memoizing values.

1095. What is the difference between useCallback and useMemo?

Category: Hooks
Both useCallback and useMemo optimize React performance by memoization. useCallback returns a memoized callback function, while useMemo returns a memoized value from a computation. Essentially, useCallback(fn, deps) is equivalent to useMemo(() => fn, deps), but useCallback is intended specifically for functions, making your intent clearer.

1096. What is the difference between useDispatch and bindActionCreators?

Category: State Management
useDispatch is a React-Redux hook that returns the dispatch function used to send actions to the Redux store. bindActionCreators is a utility that wraps action creators with dispatch calls, allowing you to call them directly without manually dispatching actions. While useDispatch requires explicit dispatch calls, bindActionCreators simplifies dispatching by binding action creators upfront.

1097. What is the difference between useEffect and componentDidMount?

Category: Hooks
componentDidMount is a lifecycle method in class components that runs once after the component is mounted. useEffect hook in functional components can be configured to run once after mount by providing an empty dependency array. Unlike componentDidMount, useEffect can run on every render or when dependencies change, offering more flexible side effect management.

1098. What is the difference between useEffect and useInsertionEffect?

Category: Hooks
useInsertionEffect is a newer React hook designed to run synchronously before DOM mutations, useful for injecting styles or critical side effects that must happen before layout. useEffect runs asynchronously after rendering and painting, suitable for data fetching or effects that do not block visual updates. useInsertionEffect helps prevent visual inconsistencies when injecting CSS or DOM changes.

1099. What is the difference between useEffect and useLayoutEffect hooks?

Category: Hooks
useEffect runs asynchronously after the browser has painted the screen, suitable for non-blocking side effects like fetching data or event subscriptions. useLayoutEffect runs synchronously immediately after DOM mutations but before the browser paints, allowing you to read layout or synchronously modify the DOM to avoid flickering. useLayoutEffect is useful when you need to perform measurements or DOM manipulations that must happen before the user sees updates.

1100. What is the difference between useEffect and useLayoutEffect?

Category: Hooks
The key difference is timing: useEffect runs asynchronously after rendering and painting, while useLayoutEffect runs synchronously after DOM updates but before the browser paints. useLayoutEffect blocks painting until it finishes, preventing visual glitches during DOM manipulations, whereas useEffect is better for non-blocking side effects.

1101. What is the difference between useEffect and useLayoutEffect?

Category: Hooks
The main difference between useEffect and useLayoutEffect lies in their execution timing relative to the browser's painting phase. useEffect runs asynchronously after the browser has painted the UI, making it ideal for side effects that do not block visual updates, such as fetching data or event subscriptions. In contrast, useLayoutEffect runs synchronously after DOM mutations but before the browser paints, allowing you to make DOM measurements or changes that must happen before the user sees the update, preventing flickering or layout glitches. However, because useLayoutEffect blocks painting until it completes, it should be used sparingly to avoid performance issues.

1102. What is the difference between useImperativeHandle and useRef?

Category: Refs
useRef is a hook that creates a mutable ref object which can hold any value and persists across renders. It is commonly used to access DOM elements or store mutable variables without causing re-renders. useImperativeHandle is a more advanced hook used with forwardRef to customize the instance value exposed to parent components when using refs. It allows a child component to control what methods or properties are accessible through the ref, enabling encapsulation and controlled imperative actions.

1103. What is the difference between useMemo and memo?

Category: Performance Optimization
useMemo is a React hook used inside functional components to memoize expensive calculations or computed values, recomputing them only when dependencies change. memo is a higher-order component that memoizes the rendered output of a component, preventing unnecessary re-renders if the component’s props do not change. Essentially, useMemo optimizes value computations, while memo optimizes component rendering.

1104. What is the difference between useMemo and React.memo?

Category: Performance
useMemo is a hook that memoizes a computed value inside a functional component, recalculating it only when dependencies change, improving performance by avoiding expensive recalculations. React.memo is a higher-order component that memoizes a component’s rendered output, preventing unnecessary re-renders if props are unchanged. The former optimizes calculations, while the latter optimizes component re-rendering.

1105. What is the difference between useMemo and useCallback hooks?

Category: Hooks
useMemo memoizes the result of a function (a value) so that the computation only happens when dependencies change, ideal for optimizing expensive calculations. useCallback memoizes a function itself, returning the same function reference unless dependencies change, useful when passing callbacks to child components to avoid unnecessary re-renders. Essentially, useMemo caches values, while useCallback caches functions.

1106. What is the difference between useMemo and useCallback?

Category: Hooks
Both hooks optimize performance by memoization, but useMemo returns a memoized value from a computation, while useCallback returns a memoized function reference. If you want to cache a calculation, use useMemo; if you want to cache a function to avoid re-creating it unnecessarily, use useCallback.

1107. What is the difference between useState and useReducer hooks?

Category: Hooks
useState is a simple hook to manage local state, ideal for straightforward state updates. useReducer is a more powerful hook for managing complex state logic, especially when state depends on previous state or when there are multiple sub-values. It uses a reducer function to compute new state based on dispatched actions, making state transitions predictable and easier to test.

1108. What is the difference between useState and useReducer?

Category: Hooks
While both manage state in functional components, useState is simpler and suited for basic state updates, whereas useReducer handles more complex state changes with a reducer function and action dispatching, offering clearer control over state logic and making it easier to manage intricate state transitions.

1109. What is the difference between useState and useRef in React Native?

Category: React Native
useState manages reactive state that triggers re-renders when updated, allowing UI to respond to changes. useRef stores mutable values that persist across renders but updating a ref does not cause a re-render. In React Native, this distinction is crucial for optimizing performance by avoiding unnecessary UI updates when mutable values don't affect rendering.

1110. What is the Fetch API and how is it used in React?

Category: Data Fetching
The Fetch API is a modern browser API for making HTTP requests to servers. In React, it’s commonly used inside lifecycle methods or hooks like useEffect to fetch data asynchronously from APIs and update component state with the response. Fetch returns promises and supports a clean, readable way to handle network requests, making it a standard choice for data fetching in React applications.

1111. What is the importance of keys in React lists?

Category: Lists & Keys
Keys are unique identifiers assigned to elements in lists to help React efficiently identify which items have changed, been added, or removed during reconciliation. This optimizes rendering by minimizing DOM operations, preserving component state, and improving performance when updating lists dynamically.

1112. What is the key prop and why is it important?

Category: JSX
The key prop provides a stable identity for elements in a list, enabling React’s reconciliation algorithm to track changes and efficiently update the UI. Without keys, React may re-render all elements unnecessarily or lose component state, leading to poor performance and UI bugs.

1113. What is the order of lifecycle methods in mounting?

Category: Lifecycle
In class components, the mounting lifecycle methods execute in this order: constructor(), static getDerivedStateFromProps(), render(), and then componentDidMount(). This sequence prepares the component, processes props/state, renders the UI, and finally runs side effects after the component is inserted into the DOM.

1114. What is the order of lifecycle methods in updating?

Category: Lifecycle
During updates, the lifecycle methods execute in this order: static getDerivedStateFromProps(), shouldComponentUpdate(), render(), getSnapshotBeforeUpdate(), and finally componentDidUpdate(). This flow allows state/props updates to be processed, rendering to update UI, and side effects or DOM snapshots to occur safely.

1115. What is the purpose of key in a React list?

Category: Lists & Keys
The purpose of key in a React list is to give each element a stable, unique identifier so React can track individual items during rendering and efficiently update only those that changed. This improves performance and helps maintain component state correctly.

1116. What is the purpose of Babel in React?

Category: Tooling
Babel is a JavaScript compiler that transforms modern JavaScript and JSX syntax used in React into browser-compatible JavaScript. It enables developers to use cutting-edge language features and JSX without worrying about browser support, ensuring consistent functionality across environments.

1117. What is the purpose of generics in React TypeScript components?

Category: TypeScript
Generics in React TypeScript components enable type-safe and reusable components by allowing the component to accept types as parameters. This flexibility ensures that components can work with various data types while maintaining strong typing and reducing code duplication.

1118. What is the purpose of keys in lists and how do they help React?

Category: Lists & Keys
Keys give React a way to uniquely identify each element in a list, which helps the reconciliation process by efficiently determining which elements need updating, removing, or adding. This avoids unnecessary re-renders and helps React preserve component state correctly.

1119. What is the purpose of keys in React lists?

Category: Lists & Keys
Keys serve as unique identifiers for list elements, allowing React to optimize rendering by tracking items efficiently during updates. This prevents performance bottlenecks and bugs caused by improper element reconciliation.

1120. What is the purpose of React fragments?

Category: Components
React fragments allow grouping multiple elements without adding extra nodes to the DOM. They help maintain clean markup by avoiding unnecessary wrapper elements like div, which can simplify styling and improve performance.

1121. What is the purpose of React keys in lists?

Category: Lists & Keys
React keys in lists provide a unique identifier for each element in a collection. Their main purpose is to help React efficiently reconcile changes by tracking which items have been added, removed, or updated. This allows React to update only the affected elements instead of re-rendering the entire list, enhancing performance and preserving component state.

1122. What is the purpose of React keys?

Category: Lists & Keys
React keys uniquely identify elements within a list to aid in the reconciliation process. Keys ensure that elements maintain their identity across renders, which prevents unnecessary re-renders and allows React to optimize UI updates by efficiently matching virtual DOM elements with the real DOM.

1123. What is the purpose of React Profiler API?

Category: Debugging
The React Profiler API is designed to help developers measure the performance of their React applications. It provides insights on how frequently components render, how long rendering takes, and what caused renders. This information is invaluable for diagnosing performance bottlenecks and optimizing component rendering.

1124. What is the purpose of React.Fragment?

Category: Components
React.Fragment allows grouping multiple children elements without adding extra nodes to the DOM. This is useful for returning multiple elements from a component’s render method without wrapping them in unnecessary divs or other elements, helping to keep the DOM tree clean and efficient.

1125. What is the purpose of React.StrictMode?

Category: Advanced Features
React.StrictMode is a tool used during development to highlight potential problems in an application. It activates additional checks and warnings for deprecated APIs, unsafe lifecycle methods, and side effects, helping developers write safer, more future-proof React code. It does not affect production builds.

1126. What is the purpose of React’s error boundaries?

Category: Error Handling
React’s error boundaries are special components that catch JavaScript errors anywhere in their child component tree during rendering, lifecycle methods, and constructors. They prevent the entire app from crashing by displaying fallback UI and logging errors, improving user experience and application stability.

1127. What is the purpose of React’s StrictMode in development?

Category: Advanced Features
React’s StrictMode in development helps detect unsafe lifecycles, deprecated APIs, unexpected side effects, and other potential issues by intentionally invoking certain methods twice and performing extra checks. This promotes best practices and prepares code for future React versions without impacting production performance.

1128. What is the purpose of render() in class components?

Category: Basics
The render() method in React class components defines the component’s UI by returning React elements or JSX. It is a required method that React calls to know what to display on the screen and it runs every time the component’s state or props change.

1129. What is the purpose of setState?

Category: Basics
setState is a method in class components used to update the component’s state. It triggers a re-render with the updated state, ensuring that UI reflects the latest data. State updates via setState are asynchronous and can be batched for performance.

1130. What is the purpose of synthetic events in React?

Category: Events
Synthetic events in React are cross-browser wrappers around the native browser events. They provide a consistent interface and behavior across different browsers, improving compatibility. Additionally, synthetic events help with performance optimizations such as event delegation and pooling.

1131. What is the purpose of the Route component?

Category: Routing
The Route component in React Router is used to render UI components based on the current URL path. It matches the path prop against the URL and displays the corresponding component, enabling declarative navigation and page rendering in single-page applications.

1132. What is the purpose of the useCallback hook?

Category: Hooks
The useCallback hook memoizes a function reference so that it is only recreated when its dependencies change. This helps optimize performance by preventing unnecessary re-renders of child components that depend on stable callback functions, especially when passing functions as props.

1133. What is the purpose of the useEffect hook?

Category: Hooks
The useEffect hook allows React functional components to perform side effects such as data fetching, subscriptions, or manually manipulating the DOM. It runs after the component renders and can be configured to run conditionally by specifying dependencies. This hook replaces lifecycle methods like componentDidMount, componentDidUpdate, and componentWillUnmount in functional components.

1134. What is the purpose of the useImperativeHandle hook?

Category: Hooks
The useImperativeHandle hook customizes the instance value that is exposed when using ref in parent components. It allows a component to control which methods or properties are accessible to the parent through the ref, enabling controlled imperative interactions without exposing the entire internal implementation.

1135. What is the purpose of the useRef hook?

Category: Refs
The useRef hook provides a way to persist a mutable value that survives re-renders without causing additional renders when updated. It is commonly used to access DOM elements directly or store any mutable variable that does not require triggering a UI update.

1136. What is the purpose of useCallback hook and when should you use it?

Category: Hooks
The useCallback hook memoizes a function so that it only changes if its dependencies change. This prevents unnecessary re-creations of functions on every render, which is particularly useful when passing callbacks to optimized child components that rely on referential equality to avoid re-renders.

1137. What is the purpose of useCallback hook?

Category: Hooks
The useCallback hook returns a memoized version of a callback function that only changes if one of its dependencies has changed. This helps optimize performance by preventing unnecessary re-creations of functions on every render.

1138. What is the purpose of useDebugValue hook?

Category: Hooks
The useDebugValue hook is used to display a label for custom hooks in React DevTools, helping developers debug and monitor hook behavior more easily during development.

1139. What is the purpose of useReducer hook?

Category: Hooks
The useReducer hook provides an alternative to useState for managing complex state logic by using a reducer function. It is inspired by Redux and is useful for handling state transitions that depend on the previous state, especially in cases with multiple related state values.

1140. What is the purpose of useRef hook in React?

Category: Refs
The useRef hook in React is primarily used to reference DOM elements directly or to hold mutable values that persist across renders without causing re-renders when updated.

1141. What is the purpose of useRef?

Category: Refs
The useRef hook allows you to create a mutable reference that persists across component renders. It is commonly used for accessing and manipulating DOM elements or storing values that should not trigger re-renders.

1142. What is the purpose of useTransition hook?

Category: Hooks
The useTransition hook enables marking state updates as transitions, allowing React to prioritize urgent updates over less urgent ones. This helps keep the UI responsive during expensive rendering tasks by deferring non-urgent updates and showing intermediate loading states.

1143. What is the purpose of using React.Fragment?

Category: JSX
React.Fragment is used to group multiple JSX elements without adding extra nodes to the DOM. It is useful for returning multiple elements from a component without unnecessary wrappers, keeping the rendered HTML cleaner and more efficient.

1144. What is the React component lifecycle?

Category: Lifecycle
The React component lifecycle consists of phases that class components go through: mounting (creation and insertion into the DOM), updating (re-rendering due to changes in props or state), and unmounting (removal from the DOM). Lifecycle methods allow developers to hook into these phases to perform side effects, manage resources, or optimize rendering.

1145. What is the React Context API?

Category: State Management
The React Context API enables sharing data (such as themes, user information, or settings) globally across the component tree without passing props manually at every level. It simplifies state management for data that many components need to access.

1146. What is the React Profiler and how do you use it for performance analysis?

Category: Debugging
The React Profiler is a tool that measures the rendering performance of React components. It tracks when components render, how long rendering takes, and why they rendered. Developers use it to identify performance bottlenecks and optimize component updates by analyzing the timing and frequency of renders.

1147. What is the React Profiler tool in DevTools?

Category: Performance
The React Profiler tool in DevTools provides a visual interface for analyzing component rendering behavior. It records commits, displays render durations, and highlights components that re-render unnecessarily, enabling developers to fine-tune app performance by pinpointing costly renders.

1148. What is the Redux DevTools extension?

Category: State Management
The Redux DevTools extension is a powerful browser tool that helps developers inspect and debug Redux state changes. It provides features like viewing dispatched actions, tracking state changes over time, time-travel debugging to replay past actions, and monitoring the state tree, which makes managing and understanding complex state easier.

1149. What is the Redux store?

Category: State Management
The Redux store is a centralized container that holds the entire application state in a single immutable object. It manages state updates through dispatched actions and reducers, and allows components to subscribe to state changes, ensuring a predictable state management flow.

1150. What is the Redux Toolkit and why use it?

Category: State Management
Redux Toolkit is an official, opinionated set of tools that simplify Redux development. It provides utilities to reduce boilerplate, such as configuring the store, creating reducers with less code, and managing immutable updates. It makes Redux easier to learn and use, with best practices baked in.

1151. What is the render props pattern?

Category: Design Patterns
The render props pattern is a technique for sharing logic between React components using a prop whose value is a function. This function returns React elements, allowing dynamic rendering based on state or props, promoting code reuse and flexibility.

1223. What is the useMemo hook and how does it differ from useCallback?

Category: Hooks
Both useMemo and useCallback are hooks for memoization to optimize performance. useMemo memoizes the result of a computation, returning a cached value unless dependencies change. In contrast, useCallback memoizes a function reference itself, ensuring the function identity remains stable between renders unless dependencies change.

1236. What is the virtual DOM and how does React use it?

Category: Core Concepts
The virtual DOM is a lightweight, in-memory representation of the actual DOM. React uses it to efficiently determine the minimal set of changes required to update the real DOM by diffing the virtual DOM with its previous version. This optimization significantly improves rendering performance and responsiveness.

1237. What is the Virtual DOM in React?

Category: Core Concepts
The Virtual DOM is a conceptual copy of the actual DOM that React maintains in memory. It allows React to optimize UI updates by computing differences between virtual DOM states and applying only the necessary changes to the real DOM.

1238. What is the virtual DOM?

Category: Core Concepts
The virtual DOM is a virtual representation of the UI kept in memory. It acts as an abstraction layer over the real DOM to enable efficient updates by comparing the new virtual DOM with the previous one and updating only what’s necessary in the real DOM.

1239. What is throttling and how is it used in React?

Category: Performance
Throttling limits how frequently a function can execute over time. In React, it’s often used to control expensive operations like window resizing or scroll events by reducing the number of times event handlers run, improving app performance and responsiveness.

1240. What is thunk in Redux?

Category: State Management
Thunk is a middleware for Redux that allows action creators to return functions instead of plain objects. These functions can perform asynchronous operations (like API calls) before dispatching standard actions, enabling complex async logic within Redux.

1241. What is tree shaking in React build tools?

1242. What is useCallback and how is it used?

useCallback memoizes a callback function, returning the same function instance between renders unless dependencies change. It’s used to prevent unnecessary re-creations of functions, especially useful when passing callbacks to optimized child components or dependencies in hooks.

1243. What is useCallback and when should it be used?

1245. What is useEffect and how is it used?

1246. What is useEffect’s role in data fetching?

Category: Data Fetching
The useEffect hook plays a critical role in data fetching within React functional components. It allows developers to perform asynchronous data fetching operations after the component has rendered, ensuring that side effects such as API calls do not block the initial render. By specifying dependencies, it controls when the data fetching should reoccur, enabling efficient and declarative fetching tied to component lifecycle.

1247. What is useImperativeHandle and when is it used?

1248. What is useLayoutEffect and how is it different from useEffect?

Category: Hooks
Both useLayoutEffect and useEffect allow side effects in functional components, but differ in timing. useLayoutEffect runs synchronously immediately after all DOM mutations but before the browser paints, enabling measurements or DOM manipulations without flicker. useEffect, by contrast, runs asynchronously after paint, suitable for non-blocking operations like data fetching.

1249. What is useMemo and how does it help performance?

Category: Hooks
The useMemo hook memoizes the result of a computation, recalculating the value only when its dependencies change. This optimization avoids expensive recalculations on every render, improving performance particularly in components with heavy computations or frequent updates.

1250. What is useMemo and when should you use it?

1251. What is useReducer and how does it compare to useState?

1252. What is useRef and how is it different from state?

1254. What is useState and how is it used?

The useState hook allows functional components to have state variables. It returns a state value and a setter function to update that state. When the setter is called, React schedules a re-render to update the UI with the new state.

1255. What is Zustand and how does it compare to Redux?

1256. What libraries can be used for i18n in React?

Category: Internationalization
There are several popular libraries for internationalization (i18n) in React. The most widely used is react-i18next, which offers powerful translation capabilities, support for namespaces, and seamless integration with React hooks and context. Other notable libraries include react-intl, which is built on the FormatJS standard and provides formatting for dates, numbers, and pluralization, and LinguiJS, which focuses on simplicity and developer experience. These libraries help manage translations, locale detection, and formatting to make applications accessible to users worldwide.

1257. What libraries can help with form handling in React?

Category: Forms
Several libraries simplify form handling in React by managing state, validation, and submission processes. Formik is one of the most popular options, providing easy integration with Yup for schema validation and supporting both controlled and uncontrolled components. React Hook Form leverages React hooks for performant and minimal re-renders, making it lightweight and fast. Other options include Redux Form for integration with Redux, though it is less commonly used now due to performance considerations, and Final Form, which offers flexibility and a simple API for form management.

1258. What libraries help with form management?

Category: Forms
To manage forms effectively in React, developers often rely on libraries such as Formik, which provides declarative form handling and validation support. React Hook Form is favored for its hook-based approach, minimal re-renders, and ease of use with native HTML inputs. Final Form and its React wrapper, React Final Form, provide powerful subscription-based form state management. These libraries reduce boilerplate and improve maintainability for complex forms.

1259. What lifecycle methods correspond to error boundaries?

Category: Advanced Concepts
Error boundaries in React are implemented using specific lifecycle methods in class components. The primary methods are static getDerivedStateFromError(error), which updates state to indicate an error has occurred, and componentDidCatch(error, info), which logs the error or performs side effects such as reporting. These methods allow components to gracefully handle errors during rendering, lifecycle methods, or in constructors of child components, preventing the entire app from crashing.

1260. What rules must hooks follow?

Category: Hooks
Hooks in React must follow two fundamental rules: (1) They should only be called at the top level of React function components or custom hooks, never inside loops, conditions, or nested functions to preserve consistent hook ordering. (2) They must only be called from React function components or custom hooks, not from regular JavaScript functions. These rules ensure React can correctly track hook calls and manage state and effects reliably.

1261. What tools can be used for E2E testing in React?

Category: Testing
For end-to-end (E2E) testing of React applications, popular tools include Cypress, known for its developer-friendly interface, fast test execution, and easy debugging. Playwright and Puppeteer are also widely used for browser automation and testing across multiple browsers. These tools simulate real user interactions and test app behavior in a realistic environment, ensuring the full stack works as expected from UI to backend.

1262. What tools can you use to profile performance in React apps?

Category: Performance
Performance profiling in React apps can be done using the React Profiler built into React Developer Tools, which records rendering performance, highlights wasted renders, and tracks component render times. Additionally, browser performance tools like Chrome DevTools can profile JavaScript execution and paint times. Tools like Web Vitals and monitoring solutions such as New Relic or Datadog can provide further insights into app performance and user experience metrics.

1263. What’s the difference between React elements and components?

Category: Core Concepts
React elements are the smallest building blocks in React — they are plain objects that describe what you want to see on the screen, such as HTML tags or other React components. Think of elements as the instructions for React to create DOM nodes. Components, on the other hand, are reusable functions or classes that return React elements. Components define the structure, behavior, and appearance of UI sections and can be composed to build complex interfaces. Essentially, elements are the output of components, and components control what elements are rendered.

1264. When should you create a custom hook?

Category: Hooks
Custom hooks should be created when you need to extract and reuse logic involving React state or lifecycle features across multiple components. If you find yourself repeating similar useEffect, useState, or other hook patterns in different places, packaging that logic into a custom hook promotes modularity and reduces duplication. Custom hooks enable clean abstractions for complex behaviors like data fetching, subscriptions, or form handling, making your code more maintainable and readable.

1265. When should you use useReducer over useState?

Category: Hooks
You should use useReducer instead of useState when your component state logic is complex, involves multiple sub-values, or when the next state depends on the previous one. useReducer centralizes state updates into a reducer function, making state transitions predictable and easier to manage, especially for forms, complex user interactions, or deeply nested state. It also helps when you want to optimize performance by preventing unnecessary re-renders via dispatching actions.

1266. Why are keys important in React lists?

Category: Lists & Keys
Keys are essential in React lists because they help React identify which items have changed, been added, or removed. This identification allows React to optimize rendering by only updating the parts of the DOM that actually changed, rather than re-rendering the entire list. Keys must be unique and stable to maintain consistent identity across renders, ensuring smooth UI updates and preventing bugs caused by incorrect element reuse.

1267. Why can’t error boundaries catch errors inside event handlers?

Category: Error Handling
Error boundaries only catch errors that occur during rendering, lifecycle methods, and constructors of child components. They do not catch errors inside event handlers because event handlers run asynchronously outside of the rendering phase. To handle errors in event handlers, you need to use regular JavaScript try-catch blocks or other error handling logic within the event function itself.

1268. Why is immutability important in React state management?

Category: Core Concepts
Immutability is crucial in React state management because it enables efficient change detection. By ensuring that state objects are not mutated directly but instead replaced with new copies, React can quickly determine what changed by comparing references. This process supports React's reconciliation algorithm, leading to optimal re-rendering and predictable application behavior. Immutability also helps avoid unintended side effects and makes debugging easier.

1269. Why were Hooks introduced in React?

Category: Hooks
Hooks were introduced in React to address the limitations of class components, such as complex lifecycle methods and challenges with code reuse. They allow developers to use state and other React features inside function components, leading to simpler, more readable, and more composable code. Hooks improve logic reuse through custom hooks and reduce the boilerplate associated with classes, promoting better organization and maintainability.

1270. Why would you create a custom hook?

Category: Hooks
You would create a custom hook to encapsulate reusable logic that involves React hooks, such as state management, side effects, or subscriptions. Custom hooks allow you to share functionality across multiple components without duplicating code, enhancing modularity and readability. They provide an abstraction layer that helps keep components clean and focused on rendering UI rather than managing complex logic.
